#5996. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5979. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5976. Judges have put Trump's arch and golf course on hold, but only for a matter of days

Federal judges have issued temporary restraining orders to block construction of Trump's arch and keep East Potomac Park open. Julia Demaree Nikhinson/AP hide caption

A federal judge has extended her block on construction of President Trump's D.C. arch for two weeks, another short-term victory for those who don't want to see the 250-foot structure break ground.

D.C. District Judge Tanya Chutkan extended her temporary restraining order on Wednesday, the day before it was set to expire. She originally stepped in to block construction on the night of Nov. 5, after the National Capital Planning Commission and National Park Service gave the arch final approval and the Trump administration said construction could begin imminently.

Chutkan's new five-page order prevents the administration from "initiating, making, or otherwise effectuating any construction, excavation, demolition, land-disturbance, utility-installation, or roadway-closure activity, or any other construction-related activity" at Memorial Circle through Dec. 3.

"The judge's order recognizes that the conditions that called for emergency relief two weeks ago remain unchanged," Nick Sansone, the Public Citizen attorney representing the original plaintiffs in the lawsuit, said in a statement.

The extension gives Chutkan more time to consider plaintiffs' request for a longer-term preliminary injunction, which would prohibit construction as the lawsuit against the arch plays out.

That lawsuit started earlier this year with a challenge from a group of Vietnam War veterans and an architectural historian, but has expanded in recent weeks.

In late October, the governments of D.C. and Virginia filed another lawsuit, which was combined with the original. And nine members of the Kennedy family joined the fight last week, saying the arch will block historic sightlines and cast shadows over the graves of John F. Kennedy and others at nearby Arlington National Cemetery.

"This is one man who wants to build this arch. He's violated every rule. If this is a great idea, then bring it before Congress, follow the rules and get it approved," Max Kennedy, a son of Robert F. Kennedy, told NPR's All Things Considered.

Opposition to the arch — from area residents, veterans, local officials, Democratic lawmakers and more — has grown in recent months as the structure hurtled through the federal approval process.

Critics take issue with the arch's scale and location, saying it would interrupt the solemn and historic vista between Arlington National Cemetery and the Lincoln Memorial. Plaintiffs argue the structure violates several laws — including the Commemorative Works Act and D.C.'s Height of Buildings Act — and is fundamentally illegal without approval from Congress.

A jogger passes a mobile security camera truck at Memorial Circle, where Trump wants to build his arch. Tom Brenner/AP hide caption

The Trump administration has not sought that approval, arguing the arch is covered by Congress' authorization in 1925 of a pair of 166-foot columns at the same location, which were never built.

In court filings and a recently unveiled website, the administration characterizes building the arch as a matter of urgency — both to complete that century-old vision and to facilitate national security. Trump said in September that the arch would double as a military complex, but the plans that got final approval this month don't include any details to that effect.

In a declaration this week, Ricky Buria, chief of staff to Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, told the court that the agency is "currently in the planning process to determine how to integrate national defense uses … into the Arch without changing the existing plans for the Arch."

White House spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR on Thursday that the administration will "continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds."

The arch is one of several construction projects that Trump has pursued with vigor in his second term. Some of them, like the White House ballroom and Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, are well underway. Others, like the Kennedy Center closure and East Potomac golf course redevelopment, are held up by various legal challenges.

Democrats have said they will use their forthcoming majority in Congress to push back on Trump's construction agenda, including by blocking federal funding. But experts say the fate of many of these projects will ultimately be up to the court.

Trump's dream of redeveloping a municipal D.C. golf course into a championship-level venue is also temporarily on hold, despite his repeated social media pronouncements that the park would close and work would begin on Nov. 15.

Another D.C. district judge, Ana Reyes, intervened Saturday to order the administration to keep East Potomac Park open for two weeks, on the eve of its indefinite closure.

But the golf course itself is currently unstaffed, since its operator lease expired on Sunday. That hasn't stopped locals from showing up and teeing off, finding creative ways to mark the holes after the flagsticks were removed from the course.

Reyes has ordered both parties to weigh in on whether she has the authority to order the government to keep the golf course open.

On Wednesday, the Trump administration officially appealed Reyes' restraining order as it had promised. That order is currently set to expire on Nov. 28, the Saturday after Thanksgiving.

Noting that timing, the plaintiffs in the case — local golfers and historic preservationists — have asked Reyes to extend her order by another two weeks, until Dec. 12, while she considers the underlying legal issues and decides whether to issue a longer preliminary injunction.

The government, on the other hand, said "the court already has everything it needs to promptly consider a motion for preliminary injunction" and has proposed a schedule that would enable her to issue a final ruling by Nov. 28.

National Park Service employees walk past a golfer at East Potomac Park on Monday, after a judge ordered the administration to keep the park open. Julia Demaree Nikhinson/AP hide caption

In the meantime, the administration took another step forward in the federal review process on Thursday. The Interior Department presented its plan for the golf course to the Commission of Fine Arts, one of two federal agencies which would need to approve the project.

Commission Secretary Thomas Luebke said some 700 members of the public submitted comments ahead of the meeting, all but three in opposition to the golf course proposal.

The biggest concerns had to do with making the park less accessible to golfers of all levels, as well as cyclists, runners and picnickers who use the park's perimeter loop, which would be eliminated. There were other concerns about the impact of major road races — like the Marine Corps Marathon — that use that road, the loss of historic cherry trees and the cost of the project (the Interior Department said deferred maintenance alone would cost $43 million but did not specify how much the renovations would cost).

Over a dozen other people spoke out in the room, sharing their personal experiences with the park, raising questions about gaps in the presentation and urging the commission to defer or outright reject any plan that does not leave the perimeter loop intact.

Commissioners — all of whom were appointed by Trump — did not have the quorum needed to vote on preliminary approval. Several offered praise for the idea of beautifying the space and overhauling the golf course, but questioned whether this plan is the best way to do it.

Vice Chair Rodney Mims Cook, Jr., a Trump ally, said there seems to be enough space in the park to accommodate the aspects that people care about, like preserving the historic mini-golf course. His comments prompted applause from many of the people who had crowded into the meeting room.

Cook asked the Interior Department to bring alternative designs to its next meeting, in late January. The administration has said it plans to present the same proposal to the National Capital Planning Commission at its meeting in early December.

Administration officials said in Thursday's meeting, as they have in previous court hearings, that their plan is not yet finalized. Judge Reyes, however, is not convinced and accused the administration of already taking steps to carry it out, like cutting down hundreds of trees.

Plaintiffs in the case argue the administration has violated federal environmental law by splitting the project into smaller parts, like the tree removal, to bypass necessary reviews. And they say the administration skipped required legal steps in its rush to close off most of the park, which Reyes seemed to agree with in her order.

"In its haste to meet an arbitrary deadline, the Government has likely violated at least the National Environmental Policy Act and the Administrative Procedure Act," she wrote.

Trump has repeatedly described the East Potomac golf course as decrepit and dangerous, and challenged Reyes' authority over the project. Over the weekend, he reiterated that Reyes was appointed by President Biden and slammed her decision as "highly destructive and political."

"Why would a judge want to delay taking a terrible and very unsafe golf course, one that is mocked all over D.C.(and far beyond!), instead of quickly turning it into a great and admired one?" Trump wrote on Truth Social. "Time is of the essence, or golf seasons, jobs, prestige, and much money, will be lost!"

Government lawyers asked Reyes to recuse herself from the case in mid-October, accusing her of bias and hostility. She denied that request on legal grounds. Tensions between Reyes and the administration have dominated this case so far, with the judge ordering a misconduct inquiry into whether government officials and lawyers misled the court about its plans for the park.

Government lawyers have pushed back, telling the court Thursday that such an inquiry is "not necessary."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5972. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5970. New York City unveils a bold plan to move its jails off Rikers Island

New York City officials have created a road map for how they'll close the jail complex on Rikers Island and move to smaller facilities within the boroughs. Mayor Zohran Mamdani closed the North Infirmary Command building, seen here, on Rikers earlier this year. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

New York City officials on Friday unveiled their plan to close the troubled jail complex on Rikers Island, the city's main pre-trial detention center, which has been plagued with reports of abuse and neglect for decades.

"It is long past time to close what's been a moral stain on our city," Mayor Zohran Mamdani told NPR. "We cannot simply accept the old approaches to public safety just because they've always been done that way."

Rikers, which sits on a former garbage dump in the East River, has long been infamous for its dismal conditions – including crumbling infrastructure. Inmates have accused guards of excessive force, sexual assault and high rates of violence in multiple lawsuits.

Last year, a federal judge called the amount of violence at Rikers an "unconstitutional danger" for both the people held there and the staff.

Since 2022, more than 50 people have died in the custody of the New York City Department of Correction. Almost all had been held at Rikers. Over the course of the jail's history, people have been found unresponsive. They have died by suicide and been beaten to death. Federal court records say Rikers is "significantly more violent" than comparable jails.

But now, city officials say they finally have momentum to close the jail for good, and the newly released plan details how they intend to do that.

Empty cells at the Rikers Island jail complex in New York City on Nov. 18, 2026. The city's plan to close Rikers aims to transform the conditions inside the city's jails and lower their population. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

The plan — which takes on both the conditions inside the jails and the size of the population behind bars — is ambitious. It seeks to transform how the city government thinks about crime prevention and safety overall, says Insha Rahman, president of the Vera Institute of Justice, a criminal justice reform nonprofit.

"If New York City can do this at scale, which it will, it is encouragement for places like LA, like Chicago, like Philadelphia, like Houston to do the same," Rahman says.

In 2019, the New York City Council approved a plan to close Rikers by 2027, and to build in its place four smaller jails in Brooklyn, Manhattan, Queens and the Bronx.

Rikers is on an island between Queens and the Bronx, out of sight for many New Yorkers. The borough-based jails will be closer to the courts and to public transit, making it easier for families, lawyers and service providers to visit people inside the facilities.

Since 2019, not a lot of progress has been made toward closing Rikers. City officials acknowledge they won't make the 2027 deadline. But Mamdani says the city is in a new moment.

"We're not the first administration to talk about this. We also know that the last administration had no interest in advancing this," Mamdani told NPR. "And we are also confident that we can be the first administration to deliver on it."

Mayor Zohran Mamdani greets New York City Department of Correction Commissioner Stanley Richards on Sept. 4, 2026. Mamdani appointed Richards earlier this year to lead the department.Michael Appleton/Mayoral Photography Office Michael Appleton/Mayoral Photography Office hide caption

Earlier this year, he appointed a "Close Rikers Czar" dedicated to closing the jail and reducing the jail population. The mayor also appointed Stanley Richards as commissioner of the DOC, the first formerly incarcerated person to lead the department.

Mamdani says his administration has already taken concrete steps: The city revived plans for a housing initiative for New Yorkers released from jail with medical needs, for instance, and it opened a therapeutic housing unit at a public hospital in Manhattan.

Last year, a federal judge stripped the city of its authority over Rikers. Operations are now controlled by a remediation manager, who released his own initial plan earlier this year for bringing the jail system into legal compliance, independent of the city's plan.

Aspects of that plan took inspiration from carceral systems in Europe, a growing trend among correctional leaders across the U.S. The remediation manager, Nicholas Deml, traveled with Commissioner Richards to Germany earlier this year on a trip organized by the Vera Institute. There, they observed the country's prisons and jails, which are often regarded as an international model.

Staff walk in a prison courtyard in Berlin, Germany. The prison system in the country is often regarded as an international model, focusing on human dignity for people in custody. Lena Mucha hide caption

"While German correctional systems are not directly transferable to New York, they reflect a fundamentally different orientation — one that places human dignity at the center of incarceration," Deml wrote in his plan.

Last month, the Vera Institute took a second group of New Yorkers to Germany to see how jails can look when they're done right, including the Moabit jail in Berlin. Each member of that group had a part to play in reimagining New York City's criminal justice system: a prosecutor, a judge, a city councilmember, the chief who supervises officers at Rikers, among others.

At first glance, the group observed, Moabit looked like a lot of jails in the U.S., but key differences emerged on closer inspection. The jail was quiet. The cells had windows to the outside. In some of them, a door inside connected it to the adjacent cell.

"Some people are afraid of the first night here, and sometimes it really helps just to open the door to have someone to talk to," Katharina Spanaus, a staff member at Moabit, told the group.

There were also televisions in the cells to calm people down, and in the hallway, a vending machine selling cigarettes.

"I'll never forget the cigarette vending machine," says Christopher Watler, who was recently appointed chair of the New York City Board of Correction, which oversees the city's jail system.

Christopher Watler, who recently became chair of the New York City Board of Correction, the independent body responsible for overseeing the city's jail system, was struck in Germany by the humanity baked into the country's jail system. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

What struck him wasn't really the cigarettes, but what the gesture communicates about treating people as human, Watler says.

It also showed him how far New York City has to go.

Just a week earlier, he sat at his first board meeting in New York, where he heard stories that evoked a different image. At the start, the board listened to anonymous phone calls from people held at Rikers.

The callers voiced concerns: A woman reported black mold. A man said he was denied his medications. Another pleaded for a less violent environment.

"New York City's a great city. Our jail system isn't great," Watler says. "It's a broken thing. But how do you repair broken things?"

The four new jails won't be fully completed for another six years. Each facility will have more access to natural light and fresh air, and more space for recreation, vocational training and education.

Together, the new jails will hold about 2,000 fewer people than the approximately 6,600 people Rikers holds now.

The city's plan invests more than $250 million across the next several years in efforts to reduce the number of people in jail, for instance, by expanding alternatives to incarceration like diversion programs and electronic monitoring.

Construction continues on the new Brooklyn-based jail, one of four being built by the city to replace Rikers. The site is due to be completed in 2029. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

It also aims to keep people from committing crimes, by expanding services like supportive housing and mental health treatment.

Another way to lower a jail's population is to shorten how long people stay there. The average length of stay of people in New York City DOC custody was nearly twice as long in 2025 as it was in 2015, according to the policy journal Vital City.

The vast majority of people at Rikers are awaiting trial, and many of their cases take longer to process than national standards dictate. Nearly a quarter have been in jail for more than a year.

Judge Joseph Zayas, chief administrative judge of the New York State Unified Court System, told NPR there are many factors that can lengthen a jail stay, including processing evidence during discovery and administering mental health assessments, for instance.

Judge Joseph Zayas, chief administrative judge of the New York State Unified Court System, says many factors can delay a court case, which is one of the contributing factors to Rikers' population size. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

"The only way to solve this backlog problem is to hit each of these things. The sum of all of the efforts will help," Zayas says.

The city is planning a task force dedicated to speeding up court cases, but the forces affecting that are outside city hall's control. Judges act independently from the mayor, as do district attorneys. City officials say they are investing in those relationships.

Some people, including those with deep knowledge of Rikers, are concerned with what happens if the city doesn't lower the jail population enough.

Vincent Schiraldi, who was DOC commissioner under Mayor Bill de Blasio, says he can understand that.

"It's really hard to have the guts to forecast lower incarceration numbers. Because if you are wrong, then you have crowded jails and you're in trouble," says Schiraldi, who now runs a philanthropic fund to close Rikers.

Other cities have dramatically reduced their jail populations. After Hurricane Katrina in 2005, New Orleans reduced its jail population by more than two thirds in the decade after.

Schiraldi pointed out that aside from an increase during the pandemic, crime rates have trended downward for decades.

A single jail cell as construction continues on the Brooklyn detention center, one of four being built by the city to replace Rikers. Some people with knowledge of the city's criminal justice system are worried that the city doesn't have a back-up plan if it can't lower the jail population enough to fit the new borough-based jails. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

If crime increases again, Schiraldi says, "Then we deal with it. What is the scenario by which the government spends billions of dollars just in case?"

But Elizabeth Glazer, who led the Office of Criminal Justice under de Blasio, told NPR she hopes the mayor will be pragmatic when it comes to having contingency plans – including the possibility of building again on Rikers Island.

She says it is possible to shrink the jail population, but it's not going to be easy.

"It requires relentless attention, coordination and organization around a common goal to get it done," says Glazer, who is also the founder of Vital City. "Even if you hit that number, the question is, can that be sustained for the decades when all you have is that capacity?"

Still, for some people the city's plan is a crucial opportunity, after years of inaction have left thousands of people held at the jail under inhumane conditions.

Tina Luongo, who leads the criminal defense practice at the Legal Aid Society, speaks during a rally to protest a death on Rikers Island at City Hall on Oct. 25, 2022 in New York City. Luongo says the city's new plan represents an opportunity to move forward, after years of inaction have left thousands of people jailed on Rikers in inhumane conditions. Michael M. Santiago/Getty Images hide caption

"We wait for these perfect plans, and for all the reasons we know, including funding, those perfect plans never come to pass. And so what do we do? We do nothing," says Tina Luongo, who leads the criminal defense practice at the Legal Aid Society, which brought the class action lawsuit that resulted in the remediation manager's appointment.

Officials caution the new jails alone won't solve the systemic problems at Rikers.

Over the last decade, the federal government has documented a "culture of violence" among the guards. In 2023, more than 700 women sued the city alleging they'd been raped or sexually abused by staff at Rikers, according to a Gothamist investigation.

"People talk about culture change and that's really just a euphemism for the extraordinary brutality and violence that has plagued Rikers," Glazer says. "If you're going to move the very same system into the new buildings, you're going to move the problem of violence as well."

In their report, city officials outline plans for more training and mentoring for staff, tighter oversight, and more programming for people in jail.

Commissioner Richards told NPR he wants to see the jail's culture transform and become more like what he saw in Germany, where human dignity is non-negotiable for everyone. That includes staff, who Richards says have been demonized.

Stanley Richards, commissioner of the New York City Department of Correction, sits in an empty jail cell in the Rikers Island complex on Nov. 18, 2026. Richards is the first formerly incarcerated person to have been appointed to the role. Nicky Quamina-Woo/NPR hide caption

"Part of the work that I'm doing is showing value and appreciation to our staff. So they begin to expand their capacity to show that same value and humanity to the people who come to us," Richards says.

But he didn't offer specifics on what exactly will change. That may be in part because he has a more immediate focus: safety and security.

The plan set forth by Deml, the remediation manager, starts with the most basic correctional functions, including protocols for securing doors. Richards, who was incarcerated at Rikers himself 40 years ago, says they need to start somewhere.

"Everybody wants something to happen by tomorrow. And we are in a moment of urgency. But it takes time to turn," he says.

The larger goal remains the same: for the failures of Rikers to not be carried into the next generation of New York City jails.

Clarification: This story has been updated to say that the known fatal beatings at Rikers Island occurred prior to 2022.

#5969. Senate passes bipartisan energy bill amid environmental concerns

NEW ALBANY, OHIO - NOVEMBER 1: In an aerial view, a portion of the Meta Prometheus Campus, an AI data center and supercluster project, is seen on November 1, 2026 in New Albany, Ohio. Jon Cherry/Getty Images hide caption

As Americans struggle with rising energy costs and increased demand driven by artificial intelligence, the Senate on Thursday passed a bipartisan bill that will speed up the approval process for data centers and power plants.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, passed 62-33. Fourteen Democrats and one Independent joined most Republicans in supporting the bill. It now heads to the House. The bill was authored by two Democrats and two Republicans.

"Tonight, the passage of permitting reform in the Senate finally became reality and we took a major step towards addressing the affordability concerns of the American people," said one of the bill's co-authors, West Virginia Republican Sen. Shelley Moore Capito.

Senate Majority Leader John Thune said the bill would "cut through decades of red tape" and "save the average household hundreds of dollars each year, and Americans will see better roads, more American manufacturing, and better-paying jobs."

Rhode Island Democratic Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse, who co-authored the bill, said "Americans will quickly begin getting some relief on their energy bills if the House follows suit."

Nationwide, the cost of a kilowatt-hour has risen faster than overall inflation: more than 6% in the last year and 39% in the last five years.

Whitehouse also said the bill would make data centers pay for the power they use. There has been considerable pushback by the public against data centers in the U.S., cutting across party lines.

Some climate change advocates say the U.S. needs to speed up building renewable energy projects and transmission lines and argue that the bill will allow the country to build more solar, wind and battery projects.

But major environmental groups, including the Sierra Club and National Wildlife Federation, have opposed the bill, arguing it will weaken environmental accountability.

Many Democrats have also opposed the bill. They say it will worsen pollution by making it easier to greenlight oil and gas projects and energy-hungry data centers, while weakening protections for clean air and water.

There is also concern the bill could undermine tribal rights.

"Don't sacrifice the Clean Water Act, hunting or fishing grounds, historic or sacred sites, or protections of tribal sovereignty," said Washington Democratic Sen. Maria Cantwell on X.

California Sen. Alex Padilla had similar concerns. "I support cutting red tape and getting more energy projects built, but not at the expense of tribal sovereignty and California's environment," he said.

Jeff ⁠Merkley, a Democratic senator from Oregon, called the bill "a bad deal."

"It opens the gates to a flood of natural gas for data centers while leaving expanded transmission of renewable energy subject to Trump's obstruction," he said on X.

In a recent social media post, President Trump urged Congress to approve the bill, calling it a "BIG WIN for U.S. ENERGY INDEPENDENCE and DOMINANCE."

Julia Simon contributed reporting to this story.

#5966. GOP Sen. Johnson rejects tying government funding to an end to the Iran war

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., speaks to reporters at the U.S. Capitol in Washington on Nov. 16. Finn Gomez/Getty Images hide caption

Republican Sen. Ron Johnson of Wisconsin, who has defended President Trump's war with Iran, said Friday that he opposes tying government funding to an end to the conflict as Congress struggles to avert a shutdown before Christmas.

"We're not going to shut down the war. That is President Trump's power. He's exercising his constitutional authority," Johnson, who chairs the Senate Budget Committee, told Morning Edition.

Congress faces a Dec. 11 deadline to keep federal agencies operating, giving Democrats leverage in the Republican-controlled Senate, where most legislation requires 60 votes to advance. But both parties face divisions. Democrats disagree over whether to use the funding deadline to force an end to the war, while Republicans are weighing bipartisan negotiations against a party-line approach to securing additional military spending.

Johnson said Republicans would prefer to negotiate a spending agreement with Democrats rather than use budget reconciliation, a procedure that could allow them to approve defense funding without Democratic support.

"I think we'll give them one more chance," he said, referring to negotiations before the Thanksgiving recess.

Johnson, who previously supported military aid to Ukraine but later opposed additional funding, warned that temporary spending measures would hinder military modernization and weapons procurement. He pointed to the changing nature of warfare in Ukraine as an example.

A shutdown could furlough hundreds of thousands of federal workers or delay their pay just weeks before Christmas. Johnson said he hoped to avoid one but warned that Republicans would need Democratic support.

The war launched by the U.S. and Israel against Iran has killed thousands of people, threatened U.S. military bases across the region and become a point of contention in the American midterm elections. Asked what he wanted to see happen in Iran, Johnson said he hoped Iranians would rise up against their government and that Iran would never acquire a nuclear weapon.

"I want to see the Ayatollahs on the dustbin of history," he said.

#5964. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

Visit ES Foundry at RE+ 2026: Booth C7434, November 16–19, Las Vegas, Nevada.

Environmentalist Jassim al-Asadi stands on a boat and looks out over the marshes where he grew up in southern Iraq. Despite having been kidnapped, tortured and accused by an Iran-backed militia of being an Israeli agent, Asadi is back in the marshes. Claire Harbage/NPR hide caption

He endured the trauma by reciting poetry until his Bani Asad tribe mobilized armed tribal fighters to secure his release. Once freed, he refused European asylum so he could continue his work. #5959. Texas execution took over an hour as staff struggled to find vein; Reinaldo Dennes moaned about pain

This photo combination shows Reinaldo Dennes, left, on April 14, 2026 and Thomas Mitchell Overton. Texas Department of Criminal Justice/AP hide caption

HUNTSVILLE, Texas — Texas executed Reinaldo Dennes on Thursday in a procedure that took over an hour, as prison staffers struggled to find a suitable vein for the two intravenous needles carrying the lethal dose of the sedative pentobarbital.

And once it began, he moaned repeatedly about pain, groaned several times and asked God to take him away from the pain. A prison spokesperson said all procedures were followed appropriately and suggested that Dennes might have exaggerated or faked the pain.

The execution followed renewed scrutiny of capital punishment in the U.S. in the aftermath of Tennessee's failed execution in September of Christa Pike, who survived two doses of pentobarbital that her lawyers say probably did not enter her bloodstream correctly and instead pooled in her arms, leaving them burned and blistered.

Dennes, who was found guilty of capital murder in the death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer found shot multiple times in his Houston office in January 1996, was the second person put to death in the country on the day, after Florida carried out a record-breaking 20th execution of the year, of Thomas Mitchell Overton.

Dennes gave a brief statement in which he said he wanted his loved ones to know he was blessed the past 30 years "knowing the love of the father." Then he told the warden: "Go, warden!"

Almost immediately he exclaimed: "The pain! Go! Go!" He uttered an expletive and implored "the father" to "take this pain." "Let me go, father," he said again and again. "Let me go. … I'm here. I've been your son all these years."

Then he shouted: "My right leg is killing me!" and described it as a cramp. After another profanity, he grunted, then said, "I don't think this is normal. Take me, father. Don't let me suffer like this. C'mon."

After three loud groans, Dennes began snoring sounds — eight of them — that progressively got quieter before a final loud snore. Then he began breathing quietly, about a dozen breaths, also getting progressively less pronounced.

The procedure was completed at 7:07 p.m., 63 minutes after he was strapped to the gurney. A minute later, after being examined by a physician, Dennes was pronounced dead at 7:08 p.m. at the penitentiary in Huntsville.

Prison officials said all procedures were followed appropriately. Spokesperson Amanda Hernandez acknowledged a number of unsuccessful attempts to find a suitable vein before the execution team managed to insert the needles in Dennes' left foot and on the left side of his neck.

She did not know how many attempts were unsuccessful but said the sites ultimately used were in accordance with the state's procedures.

Hernandez also suggested that Dennes may have been exaggerating the pain, saying he began complaining before the drugs began to flow and also of pain in places where there was no IV.

Asked if he was faking, she replied, "I think, yeah, there were allegations. ... There was nothing abnormal on our part."

"This isn't the first time we had to insert IVs into other locations. It's also not the first time we've had an inmate make allegations of pain immediately," Hernandez said.

Witness Janice Copeland's husband, David, was a security guard shot during the robbery. He survived his wounds and died five years ago.

"David always felt Reinaldo would outlive him. Unfortunately he was right," she said Thursday. "Finally after 30 years, justice has been served."

Earlier in the day Florida carried out its execution of Overton, 71, who was convicted of murdering a pregnant woman and her husband, Susan and Michael MacIvor, during a home invasion.

Overton was pronounced dead at 6:19 p.m. following a three-drug injection at Florida State Prison. In a final statement, he denied ever breaking into the couple's home and attacking them, saying, "I never killed anyone."

Florida has now carried out more than half the nation's 39 executions to date this year under Republican Gov. Ron DeSantis, who has made aggressive use of the death penalty like few other governors.

DeSantis, who leaves office in January, has said he wanted justice for families who wait decades for death sentences to be carried out. He signed the death warrants that led to a record of 19 executions in 2025, then the most in a single year in Florida since the death penalty was reinstated in 1976. Florida still plans two more executions, in December.

Texas set a record for executions there when 40 people were put to death in 2000.

The executions of two men in their 70s cast a renewed spotlight on the aging of the U.S. death row population.

#5957. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5955. Trump touts meeting with Xi and Putin but offers little results

President Trump speaks as Secretary of State Marco Rubio listens during a news briefing at the White House on Friday. Trump is addressing the media after returning from a visit to China where he held trilateral talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping. Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump took to the White House briefing room Friday telling reporters his trip to Asia this week – where he met with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian leader Vladimir Putin – was a resounding success, despite offering few tangible results.

"The United States entered this trip from a position of unmatched economic and diplomatic strength," Trump said, standing at the podium with VIce President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio behind him.

But the trip resulted more in agreements to continue discussions rather than any concrete changes. The meeting with Putin and Xi, though historic as it was the first time they met, only resulted in an agreement to continue discussing reducing nuclear risks.

In Manila, where Trump met with allies before he traveled to China, Trump signed a trade agreement with Thailand. But there are still details to the deal being finalized and the document signed simply marked a "substantial conclusion" in negotiations.

At the White House, Trump gave a wide-ranging opening statement to the press that veered from recounting his trip and who he met with, to asides about Iran, the U.S. Federal Reserve, his quest for a Nobel Peace Prize and domestic manufacturing. It was the first time he took questions from the press since being in China.

One significant change Trump announced in the middle of his press remarks was that he and XI have agreed to establish "joint guardrails" on artificial intelligence – a new move from Trump, who has been adamant that regulation over the technology would inhibit US innovation and help China lead in the global AI race. No further details about what the guardrails would look like, when they would be negotiated and released and how they would be applied between the two nations has been announced.

Trump, who said Xi also likes his term "super intelligence" rather than artificial, said, "Problem is, it may be too super. So, President Xi and I agree that we're going to do some joint guardrails, the two of us."

The announcement of future guardrails comes as Trump is facing headwinds from growing tech outages in the U.S. that ultimately led to Trump replacing the White House's cyber director.

In the briefing, Trump added that he would continue to push for the construction of data centers in the U.S. This comes after midterm election voters from both parties showed their opposition to future unfettered growth.

"Make them pay your community, but take advantage. Don't shut them off," Trump said of the massive complexes that have drawn criticism from communities due to environmental and noise pollution.

He also announced penalizations against companies that try to build data centers outside the U.S.

"If it's not this country, there's somebody else outside. And we're not going to allow our companies to build data centers outside of our country. We're not going to allow that to happen," Trump said.

Trump's trip to China came as Trump's own cabinet on what approach to take toward China. Trump's Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has been in favor of selling China U.S. chips, a stance that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang shared. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has expressed a more hawkish view toward China, and Trump has acknowledged both perspectives.

The divide has become more prominent as Lutnick and Trump face backlash for their relationship to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned from his position after admitting to an affair with a woman who may be a Chinese spy.

On Friday, Trump said he only wants "older chips" to be sold to China, rather than the most cutting-edge technology the U.S. has, and he said he'd be OK with no chips going to China at all, though he pointed out the downside to that.

"You have to understand, China makes chips too," Trump said. "And if we're not going to sell them, they'll make them themselves."

#5953. Trump initially accepted the midterm results, but most GOP voters doubt they were fair

A firefighter walks past voting booths at a firehouse serving as a polling location in Detroit on Nov. 3. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump may have initially accepted the results of his party's midterm losses, but data released Friday shows there is still broad distrust in the elections process among Republicans.

Less than half of Republican voters nationwide considered the midterm elections "free and fair," according to new polling by the University of California San Diego. Just 52% of Republicans said they trusted votes were counted accurately.

In comparison, after the 2024 election that Trump won, more than 80% of Republicans said they had confidence in the vote-counting process.

While trust in elections often dips for voters of the losing party, the GOP has seen bigger swings in confidence in recent years, as Trump has falsely claimed that U.S. elections are corrupt.

"There are still a lot of people who remain unconvinced that they can trust elections," said Thad Kousser, a co-director of UC San Diego's Center for Transparent and Trusted Elections. "And they're predominantly located in one [political] party."

Overall, a wide majority — 66% — of Americans said they think the midterms were fair. That represents an improvement from before the election, when less than half of U.S. adults said they expected free and fair contests.

But that post-election increase in trust was buoyed by Democrats and independents — many of whom worried before Election Day about potential federal interference at the polls that didn't come to pass.

"It would be a mistake to [look at the 66%] and think, 'Oh, everyone has a pretty good view of the election,'" said Lauren Prather, also a co-director of UC San Diego's election center. "It really buries the lede a little bit about the partisan gap."

The widespread doubt among Republicans may be a reality check for those wondering whether Trump's brand of election denial has run its course in American politics.

On the one hand, there will be fewer politicians who deny or question election results in office next year. And the vast majority of election-denying candidates running for positions involved in overseeing statewide elections lost their races this year, according to tracking by the advocacy group States United Action.

Election experts have also celebrated as Trump and high-profile losing candidates on the ballot this year conceded their losses.

"Sure, I'll accept [the results]," Trump told Fox News' Bret Baier on election night, as it became clear Democrats would retake the House and Senate. "Why wouldn't I?"

But just a few days later, he began conspiracy theorizing about those same results.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump posted online.

The new polling from UC San Diego shows that among Republicans, there still may be a receptive audience to such claims. When asked if politicians are discussing voter fraud too much, too little or about the right amount, just 15% of Republicans answered "too much."

"There is both demand among the Republican base for continued focus on this issue, and as we saw even after the election, politicians are continuing to supply it," Kousser said. "As long as those two dynamics are there, there's still going to be a market for these concerns."

To be clear, in most elections — domestically and internationally — supporters of the losing party usually see a dip in trust.

"It's sort of like asking people after an overtime basketball game, do you trust the referees?" Kousser said.

But in an ideal world, he said that oscillation would be more like 10 percentage points in either direction. Not the 30-point swing seen among Republicans between 2024 and 2026.

The numbers do still represent an improvement from 2020, however. At that time, leading up to the Jan. 6 riot at the U.S. Capitol in 2021, just 22% of Republicans said they had confidence in the nationwide vote count, according to similar polling done by MIT's Election Data and Science Lab.

#5950. A second judge blocks Trump's arch, saying it's unlikely to succeed in court

President Trump holds up a model of his proposed triumphal arch at a White House event in Oct. 2025. Two different federal judges have ruled against it this week. John McDonnell/AP hide caption

A federal judge in Virginia plans to issue a preliminary injunction blocking President Trump's triumphal arch indefinitely.

District Judge Leonie Brinkema said during a Friday hearing that she would grant the request of the county board of Arlington, Va., which sued over the arch in October. Democracy Forward, the nonprofit representing the plaintiffs, confirmed the ruling in a statement shared with NPR.

"We are pleased that the court has recognized the irreparable harm that will come to people who live and work in Arlington and stopped the massive vanity project while our case is heard and considered by the court," said senior counsel Kate Talmor.

Justice Department spokesperson Matthew Nies told NPR it will appeal the decision immediately.

The 250-foot arch would be built in Memorial Circle, which is part of D.C. but sits on the Virginia side of the Potomac River, a short distance from both Arlington National Cemetery and Ronald Reagan Washington National Airport, both in Arlington.

Arlington County has argued that the arch would "cause significant harm" to its residents in multiple ways, including increasing noise and air pollution and "clogging thoroughfares needed for emergency transport services." And it said an arch that doubles as a military complex — as Trump has suggested — would pose public safety risks and require additional county resources to manage.

Arlington also argues the arch would disrupt historic sightlines between Arlington National Cemetery and D.C.'s National Mall, and pose logistical challenges for the roughly 24 to 27 funerals the cemetery holds every weekday. The county also has concerns about air and road traffic, saying construction-related delays could cause more accidents and disrupt emergency vehicles far beyond the traffic circle.

"In the last 60 days, Arlington police, fire, and emergency units have responded to approximately 200 incidents in the area surrounding the Circle, including traffic stops and assisting with unconscious persons within Arlington National Cemetery," they wrote. "And the visitors the Arch is intended to attract will increase demand for those same emergency services."

The plaintiffs, like many critics of the arch, accused the Trump administration of rushing through federally mandated environmental reviews and failing to consider alternatives. The National Park Service has defended the federal review process — which ended in approval earlier this month — and said building the arch somewhere else would defeat its purpose.

"The County brings this lawsuit to halt the agency's feverish pace toward construction of the Arch and to ensure that the 'hard look' mandated by [the National Environmental Preservation Act] is completed before the agency commences disruptive and harmful ground-disturbing activities," the plaintiffs wrote last month.

NPR has reached out to the White House for comment. Spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR earlier this week that the administration will "continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds."

Brinkema, an appointee of President Clinton, is the second judge to rule against the arch this week.

On Wednesday, D.C. District Judge Tanya Chutkan extended a restraining order that prevents the Trump administration from breaking ground through Dec. 3 while she considers the underlying legal arguments.

That case was brought earlier this year by a group that includes Vietnam War veterans and was joined by the governments of D.C. and Virginia last month. Nine Kennedy family members moved to get involved last week.

Friday's ruling goes even further, preventing construction while Arlington's legal challenge plays out.

And Brinkema suggested that Arlington is likely to prevail. The judge said in court that she didn't see "any way" the government could win the case on the merits.

She reiterated that the project lacks congressional approval, which the Trump administration has not sought. It argues that the arch is covered by a 1925 law authorizing a pair of much shorter columns at that same location, which were never built. Both judges have frowned upon that argument.

"It hurts your credibility with the court by focusing on that," Brinkema told lawyers Friday, according to Reuters.

Brinkema also called it a "rushed job," and seemed to find the government's analysis of the structure's environmental and economic impact inadequate, particularly concerning the impact of construction cranes — which the government has said would be taller than the arch itself — on airspace.

The Federal Aviation Administration has said the arch would not pose a hazard to aircraft as long as it is lit a certain way, including with a controversial eternal flame on top. But aviation safety experts are concerned that the structure would add an additional layer of risk to an already notoriously complicated airspace. They say that's especially true if it has any military capabilities, which were not included in the FAA's analysis.

#5948. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5941. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.
