#5869. From Social Leasing to Social Fleets: A Proposed Law Lends Credibility to the Goal of One Million Affordable Electric Cars

Social leasing could soon change in scale, name, and business model. The bill introduced by MP Christine Arrighi aims to create a public service offering one million “low-cost electric vehicles” (ALM). Scheduled for December 3rd in the National Assembly, its examination is expected to place at the heart of the debate a now crucial question: how to make social leasing sustainable and widespread?

Several presidential candidates have made reaching one million vehicles (or even more) one of the hallmarks of their campaigns¹. While this convergence is excellent news, the battle of figures says nothing about how to achieve this goal.

The current system cannot simply be scaled up twentyfold. Its cost per vehicle, intermittent financing, imperfect social targeting, lack of a guaranteed solution after three years, and the resale risks borne by dealerships are all structural limitations. The 2026 edition also revealed its dependence on Renault and Stellantis, which can at any time restrict volumes to favor more profitable traditional sales.

The proposed law changes its approach. Sponsored by MP Christine Arrighi, it draws inspiration from the proposals of T&E France and Bernard Julien, stemming from their study on the future of social leasing, published in June 2026.

In this new model, the government would directly purchase the vehicles at an average net cost of €15,000. These vehicles would be kept for twelve years in a public fleet and leased successively to one or more households for an average monthly rent of €150 before being resold. The day-to-day management of the fleet would be delegated to groups of public or private operators within the local areas.

The initial investment to build the fleet would be financed by borrowing, with a maximum outstanding amount of €7.8 billion in 2035. Lease payments and resales, supplemented by €350 million in annual public support (equivalent to the current social leasing budget), would cover service costs and repay the debt within eighteen years. By then, the system would be self-financing, and the state would own one million cars worth approximately €10 billion.

“This model is much more beneficial for public finances,” explains Diane Strauss, director of T&E France. “It requires an initial funding effort, but it eventually pays for itself. Instead of subsidizing at a loss, we build up an asset that generates its own revenue. This is what makes the move to a million vehicles financially viable.”

In addition to providing long-term protection for low-income motorists against rising and volatile fuel prices, the proposal represents a genuine industrial planning policy. The announcement of a public order for two million vehicles over twenty years (including fleet renewal) would indeed give the French automotive sector the visibility necessary to invest and prepare for the future.

“This will also allow us to direct our production towards the vehicles that households truly need: affordable, fuel-efficient, repairable electric cars, produced in France and Europe,” concludes Léo Larivière, automotive transition expert at T&E France. “This proposal must now open a substantive discussion among all stakeholders. It is high time to prepare the next steps for one of the rare examples of policies that reconcile climate, social justice, and industrial planning.”

¹ Raphaël Glucksmann and Marine Tondelier aim for one million vehicles. Jean-Luc Mélenchon proposes a public fleet of 1.4 million cars by 2032, and François Ruffin raises the target to three million. Gabriel Attal and Dominique de Villepin also advocate expanding social leasing, without specifying a target.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5868. Solar Insure Launches 20-Year Commercial Battery Warranty, Expanding Protection for Solar & Storage Projects

Launching at RE+ Las Vegas, November 17–20 | Visit Solar Insure at Booth C4190

Commercial property owners invest in solar and battery storage to manage energy costs and strengthen resilience. One question can shape that investment from the start. Who pays when equipment needs repairs years after installation?

Solar Insure today expanded its SI-Commercial Suite with two new warranties. SI-Commercial Battery is a 20-year limited warranty for commercial battery storage systems, and SI-Commercial Total covers solar and battery storage under one warranty. Together with the existing SI-Commercial Solar warranty, these additions allow Certified Providers to offer protection tailored to each project, whether it includes solar, battery storage, or both. Solar Insure protects more than 100,000 systems nationwide.

“Commercial projects are built for the long term, and their protection should reflect that,” said Ara Agopian, CEO of Solar Insure. “By expanding our commercial warranty suite, we’re helping solar companies offer protection that fits the systems their customers are investing in. It’s about giving property owners greater confidence not just in the installation, but in the years of ownership that follow.”

A failed inverter or declining battery capacity can lead to costs an owner did not budget for. Even when a manufacturer covers replacement equipment, the owner may still be responsible for labor and service costs. The expanded suite helps Certified Providers explain which repair expenses are covered so customers can evaluate their potential repair costs alongside system performance and installation price.

“Commercial battery owners are making a long-term investment, but some of the most significant expenses can emerge years after installation,” said Dean Chiaravallotti, Chief Revenue Officer at Solar Insure. “A battery replacement or unexpected repair can change the financial picture of a project. When providers can explain how they address those costs before the customer signs, they help buyers make a more informed investment decision. That’s the value we’re bringing to commercial storage.”

Solar companies must become Solar Insure Certified Providers to offer SI-Commercial warranties. Certification includes a review of the company’s qualifications, business practices, and commitment to customer service.

For qualifying claims, Solar Insure pays covered repair costs directly to the Certified Provider or service provider performing the work. If the original installer is no longer available, Solar Insure helps coordinate next steps, which may include identifying another qualified service provider. When a property changes hands, coverage transfers to the new owner at no cost.

“A lot can change over the life of a commercial energy system. Properties change hands, service providers change, and equipment eventually needs attention,” said Chiaravallotti. “Property owners need to know what happens w          hen those changes occur. Our goal is to give them a clearer path forward, with protection and support that extend well beyond the original installation.”

Solar companies can contact Solar Insure to learn about becoming a Certified Provider. Commercial property owners can speak with a Solar Insure Certified Provider about which warranty fits their project.

Solar Insure is a leading provider of solar and storage warranties, focused on protecting long-term system performance and elevating accountability across the renewable energy industry. Through rigorous provider certification and governance, Solar Insure delivers durable protection that residential and commercial installers can stand behind.

#5867. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5864. ACC Approves Gas Plants, Rate Hike, and Allows Annual Rate Increase

Phoenix – Yesterday, the Arizona Corporation Commission voted to approve Salt River Project’s (SRP) proposed Marigold gas plant in Pinal County, and also approved Tucson Electric Power’s (TEP) proposed rate increase, as well as its proposed Annual Rate Adjustment Mechanism (ARAM) that would allow the utility to increase its rates every year with very little oversight. Yet again, the Commission’s decisions are at odds with the best interests of ratepayers, issuing rulings that collectively worsen air quality, raise costs, and limit transparency.

SRP’s gas-fired Marigold power plant is a part of the larger Marigold Energy Center, which would include solar and battery storage. But the 675-megawatt gas plant exposes customers to volatile fuel prices while worsening air quality in a county that already experiences some of the state’s most degraded air, and fails to meet federal air quality standards. The Commission did not adequately analyze the plant’s environmental impacts before approving SRP’s Certificate of Environmental Compatibility (CEC) application and approved it without any opportunity for public comment before the Commission.

The ACC also approved a significant rate increase for TEP, approving a 10% surge that customers will see reflected in utility bills starting in December. The utility had initially proposed a 14% increase. The decision comes as TEP’s parent company, Fortis, netted more than $1.7 billion in 2025 across its portfolio of utilities. The Commission also approved the utility’s adoption of “formula rates,” enabling TEP to fast-track rate increases every year without a public rate case, with limited oversight by the Commission and limited opportunities for public participation. However, Commissioner Lea Márquez-Peterson filed a proposed amendment in the case to deny approval of TEP’s formula rate mechanism.

In September, Sierra Club published a report documenting various decisions the Commission has leveraged against the best interests of ratepayers despite the fact that Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country.

“Today’s decisions continue a pattern Arizonans have become all too familiar with,” said Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr. “The ACC has shown us time and again that it will continue to prioritize utility profits and polluters over the community it is supposed to protect. Rising energy costs, climate, and clean air are top of mind for Arizonans. The ACC clearly isn’t considering this as it continues to approve rate hikes and gas plants that pollute the air, and threaten groundwater.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5863. Louisiana Communities Rally Against Meta Gas Expansion

BATON ROUGE, Louisiana — Today, weeks after a national election revealed a country fed up with high prices and data centers, Louisiana residents and organizations rallied in downtown Baton Rouge against gas plants for a massive Meta data center. Marching and rallying with signs and megaphones, the group urged the Louisiana Public Service Commission (PSC) to reject Entergy’s proposal to build seven expensive, polluting gas plants for Meta’s Hyperion campus.

Entergy wants PSC approval to build seven additional gas plants for the Meta data center, which will need more energy than a city four times as large as New Orleans. The utility doesn’t want to implement the common-sense customer protections requested by numerous groups intervening in the case. Sierra Club has been urging rejection of this proposal for months because it presents a “substantial cost” that Entergy’s existing customers may be required to bear.

Due to Gov. Jeff Landry’s “lighting speed” initiative and a previous decision by a majority of PSC commissioners, the agency is rushing this proposal through and planning to provide only one opportunity for the public to speak out against this proposal: a December meeting when commissioners are scheduled to also vote on the proposal minutes later. Much of the analysis Entergy has submitted to the PSC has been shielded from public view by NDA restrictions.

In response to the PSC’s upcoming decision, Louisiana residents and Sierra Club staff, issued the following statements:

“It’s clear by now that Meta and Entergy don’t care much for normal Louisianans, but the PSC has a mandate to do just that. Instead they have denied  us a meaningful opportunity to speak on a project that will impact our state for generations,” said Margie Vicknair-Pray, project coordinator for the Sierra Club and a Louisiana resident. “Commissioners must know that the people will not sacrifice Louisiana for one big, risky, expensive, polluting experiment. They must vote no on this outrageous corporate favor.”

“Any claims that Meta is fully paying its own way are false,” said Darryl Malek-Wiley, a senior field representative for Sierra Club and a Louisiana resident. “These seven gas plants will guzzle our water, pollute our air, – and customers will be on the hook to pay if Meta walks away. We’re here to tell commissioners that we are done footing the bill for big tech – especially considering the extreme lack of transparency surrounding this project.”

In negotiations, Entergy showed little willingness to consider or agree to any of these stipulations.

Because this would send total pollution for the permit beyond the minor-source permit limits, Meta said that – instead of applying for a major source permit as required by the Clean Air Act – it simply wouldn’t count emissions from the previously approved 31 diesel generators when those are operating during emergency hours.

Sierra Club has urged LDEQ that it must consider emissions from those diesel generators running during emergency operations.

#5861. Biofuels in the EU Post-2030 Renewable Energy Framework

A deep-dive exploring the impacts of potential EU biofuels policy scenarios

The discussion around the future of the Renewable Energy Directive (REDIII) is underway as the European Commission devises what role renewable energy will play in transport in the post-2030 energy framework.

As the political priorities of the EU focus on energy independence and domestic industry, there is a renewed push for more first generation food and feed crop biofuels in the European transport energy mix. But these biofuels can have adverse impacts on the climate, environment and food supply chains.

To address these concerns, we have modelled three different policy scenarios to demonstrate the consequences of varying outcomes and their potential impact on the climate, environment and food security.

Our findings show that the current cap on first-generation biofuels set by the Renewable Energy Directive (RED III) can allow 72% more food- and feed-crop biofuels to be used in the EU transport energy mix, compared to what the bloc consumed in 2024.

When accounting for indirect land use change, even at current consumption, emissions from first generation biofuels already reach nearly 43 MtCO2e, about 23% more than the fossil fuels they replace.

When safeguards take the same form as stipulated in RED III and other key transport regulations, food- and feed-crop biofuel volumes, land-use area and emissions could all potentially double by 2040, emitting an equivalent to more than 5 million diesel cars.

In an unfavourable scenario, whereby the cap on first generation biofuels is harmonised at 7% across Member States’ transport sectors, volumes, land use and emissions would almost triple.

In a worst case scenario, where the cap on first generation biofuels is expanded beyond 7%, the negative consequences would be far heavier. We have used 10% as an example: volumes, land use and emissions could be four times today’s levels in 2040. Land dedicated to biofuels could reach 17.7 Mha, 18% of the EU arable land, an area larger than Greece, and emissions would exceed those of fossil fuels by 33 MtCO2e.

For advanced and waste biofuels, which are limited in supply, we show that the road sector is currently overconsuming available feedstocks, which should be directed towards sectors that cannot be electrified, particularly aviation. Furthermore, recent cases of fraud have highlighted the risks of relying on third country exports, making the case that all palm oil derivatives should be phased out and that effective safeguards should be put in place to allow for the most sustainable feedstocks.

#5859. Evidence Grows for Link Between Imaging and Brain Tumor Risk in Kids

— However, findings "should not be used alone when counseling families," experts say

Medical imaging with ionizing radiation was associated with a dose-related increase in brain tumor risk among children and adolescents, a retrospective cohort study suggested.

Compared with no exposure, brain tumor risk increased with cumulative radiation dose, with hazard ratios of 1.12 (95% CI 1.01-1.26) for the lowest exposure category (>0 to <1 mGy) to 3.85 (95% CI 2.43-5.75) for the highest category (≥150 mGy).

Cumulative dose was associated with brain tumor risk overall, with an excess relative risk (ERR) per 100 mGy of 1.27 (95% CI 0.83-1.77, P<0.001), and with a stronger association for aggressive histologies (ERR per 100 mGy 1.51, 95% CI 1.00-2.11, P<0.001) reported Diana L. Miglioretti, PhD, of the University of California Davis School of Medicine, and colleagues.

"This study adds to the growing evidence that pediatric medical imaging with ionizing radiation is associated with increased cancer risk," they wrote in JAMA.

"These findings reinforce the importance of ensuring that pediatric imaging examinations that use ionizing radiation are ordered only when clinically justified and performed using optimized protocols that minimize dose," they concluded.

Miglioretti and team also noted that the cumulative incidence of brain tumors by age 21 increased from 5.4 per 10,000 children with no exposure to 22.1 per 10,000 children with a cumulative dose of 150 mGy or greater, corresponding to an excess risk of 16.7 brain tumors per 10,000 at that dose level.

For children with cumulative doses of 50 to less than 75 mGy -- a dose range comparable to the dose from one head CT -- the cumulative incidence of brain tumors was 13.3 per 10,000 children, corresponding to an excess risk of 7.9 brain tumors per 10,000 exposed children. The estimated population-attributable fraction of brain tumors associated with radiation exposure from medical imaging was 10.1% (95% CI 5.8-14.2).

In an editorial accompanying the study, Ruggero De Maria, MD, of the Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore in Rome, and colleagues noted that the study "strengthens an existing epidemiologic signal and makes its potential population impact easier to understand."

However, while these findings "place a rare outcome in perspective," they "should not be used alone when counseling families," they cautioned. "For an individual child, the small potential long-term risk must be considered alongside the often much larger and immediate benefit of obtaining the correct diagnosis."

"For clinical practice, the standard remains straightforward: select the examination most likely to answer the clinical question, use ionizing radiation when its expected benefit justifies the exposure, and obtain the necessary diagnostic information with the lowest dose compatible with adequate image quality," they wrote.

The results from this study support those from other studies, including the European EPI-CT cohort study, which found a significant dose-response relationship, such that for every 10,000 people who received a single head CT scan, approximately one patient developed brain cancer attributable to radiation exposure in the 5 to 15 years following the examination.

However, "these studies were conducted outside North America and therefore reflect CT practices and radiation doses in those settings ... and all focused on radiation exposure from CT rather than cumulative exposure across the full range of medical imaging modalities using ionizing radiation," Miglioretti and colleagues pointed out.

The current study included children born in the U.S. or Ontario, Canada, from 1996 through 2016, and captures imaging histories from birth during a more contemporary era of pediatric imaging. The researchers estimated organ-specific radiation dose from CT, radiography, fluoroscopy, angiography, and nuclear medicine.

A previous analysis of this cohort evaluated hematologic cancer risk and found that up to 10% of blood cancers in children may result from radiation exposure associated with imaging.

The cohort included 3,704,860 children (51.3% male) with 45,671,641 person-years of follow-up (mean follow-up 12.3 years); 82.6% were followed until study end or age 21 years.

A total of 1,536 brain tumors were diagnosed. Of these cases, 55.7% were in males, and 64.5% were diagnosed between the ages of 2 and 10 years. Most tumors (82.5%) were classified as having aggressive histology.

Cumulative dose at the end of follow-up was 1 mGy or greater in 6.8% of all children and 7.9% of those who developed a brain tumor. Overall, 4.4% of all children and 5.9% of those who developed a brain tumor had a cumulative dose of 50 mGy or greater, and 1.1% and 2.3%, respectively, had a cumulative dose of 100 mGy or greater.

Before the end of follow-up, 4.3% of children had one head CT scan, 0.9% had two, and 0.4% had three or more. Among children with a brain tumor, 1.6% had two head CT scans and 1.2% had three or more.

Miglioretti and colleagues acknowledged their study had several limitations, including the fact that it could not account for cancer-predisposing syndromes, and that it did not capture radiation exposure from dental radiographs or cone beam CT.

#5858. Almost 90% of Europe’s Battery Production at Risk from Franco-German Car Deal

Europe has huge potential for battery plants but calls to weaken or delay the Industrial Accelerator Act and carmaker demands to weaken the EU’s CO2 standards, risk handing Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors

The Franco-German car deal will secure just 13% of Europe’s planned battery cell production in 2030, new T&E analysis shows. Weak Made-in-Europe rules, combined with weaker emission reduction targets, risks handing over the market for battery production to Europe’s competitors, says T&E.

The IAA introduces local content requirements to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives anchor homegrown EU manufacturing. 100 GWh of cells and 250 GWh of cathodes could leak abroad because of weak Made-in-Europe rules, the analysis shows — equivalent to 1.5 million battery packs.

At the same time, Europe’s battery industry faces a double blow from the EU Parliament’s proposals to weaken emission reduction rules which will cut cell demand by 247 GWh or approximately 3.7 million EU made EVs between 2027 and 2032 — almost three times the annual capacity of Europe’s largest battery plant, LG Energy Solution in Poland.

Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials Officer at T&E, said: “The Franco-German deal is a disaster for Europe’s battery industry. Securing a full European battery value chain, from mining to production, and the high value jobs it will provide, should be a top priority for Europe’s leaders. Instead, they are debating how far to weaken rules that would secure domestically produced batteries. Weak local content incentives for batteries in the IAA will send a terrible political signal for the future of Europe’s battery industry. Homegrown companies struggling to cross the valley of death should benefit from an IAA demand boost.”

With many cell factories only just beginning to ramp up, most EU production expected by 2030 comes from plants that have not yet entered production. The future of factories such as Lyten (formerly Northvolt) in Sweden, PowerCo Salzgitter in Germany, or Verkor Dunkirk in France, could be jeopardised by this deal.

The EU’s battery industry must not go the same way as its solar panels industry, says T&E, which calls on the EU to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries, and stick to the timelines and battery requirements of the EU Commission’s proposal. In addition, a strict Made-in-EU scope, or at most a narrow list of trusted partners of the EU27 plus the European Economic Area (EEA) and the UK, must be introduced, and there should be no further weakening of the car CO2 standards.

#5855. Last Chance to Get It Right: The Industrial Accelerator Act Will Make or Break Europe’s Battery Industry

How the Franco-German auto deal, a wide Made-with-EU scope, and carmakers abandon Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors.

There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries

The IAA marks a major shift in EU industrial policy. It introduces Union content incentives to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives are used to support European manufacturing. This demand-side pull is crucial if the energy transition is to deliver high-value industrial jobs and lessen critical geopolitical dependencies. There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries. But several proposals currently before co-legislators risk severely undermining that overarching goal. Diluting battery criteria in the IAA means giving up on a strategic value chain for Europe, leaving it to China only.

T&E’s assessment shows that only an IAA with a calibrated timeline, robust battery requirements and a strict geographical scope will deliver substantial benefits for Europe. Without secured demand, announced industrial capacity will simply not materialise.

The Franco-German automotive deal from 16 October 2026 is a major blow for Europe’s battery industry as it severely dilutes incentives for batteries. It suggests battery sourcing requirements for eligible vehicles of 10% in 2028-2029, 20% in 2030-2031 and 70% from 2032 on. This is far below what the market already does: over 50% of cells in electric vehicles (EVs) sold in the EU are already Made-in-Europe. By 2030, the deal would only secure 13% (62 GWh) of Europe’s announced cell production: a further 206 GWh would likely find European buyers, though with no guarantee, while 220 GWh would find no demand at all. Even in 2032, the 70% would still not secure a substantial share of potential cell capacity (only 45%).

Another major risk is applying a wide Made-with-EU geographical scope to battery criteria. Compared to a strict EU-only scope, including free trade agreement (FTA) partners puts around 100 GWh of cell demand and 250 GWh of cathode active materials (CAM) demand at risk of leakage in 2030. Limiting the scope to trusted partners only helps to a limited extent. Since Europe can produce enough cells to meet demand under a Made-in-EU IAA, adding partner countries risks leaving EU production without offtakers: even with trusted partners only, a third of potential EU cell supply could be affected. For CAM, around 30% of expected EU supply could be left without a buyer under a trusted-partners scope, rising to 50% under an extended one.

The midstream, CAM and their precursors (pCAM), is the weakest link in Europe’s battery value chain. Boosting this key segment and diversifying away from China is essential for resilience as it shields European industry from supply disruptions and export controls. Under a strict Made-in-EU scope, reaching IAA CAM requirements is technically and commercially viable by 2030, requiring only limited sourcing from trusted partners (33 GWh cell-equivalent). From 2032, EU production can cover demand from EVs under support schemes. A Made-in-EU IAA would generate 323 GWh of extra EU CAM demand in 2032, around the output of six mid-sized EU CAM plants, bringing total EU-made CAM to 407 GWh (securing 94% of expected EU supply).

pCAM is just as vital for resilience and to establish local offtake for European refiners and recyclers. A 28% EU pCAM requirement in 2032 applied to a carmaker’s entire fleet sales (not only for vehicles eligible for public support) creates enough demand for expected EU supply to materialise and projects to reach Final Investment Decision (FID). Onshoring CAM and pCAM adds only a modest cost of $10.6/kWh for Nickel Manganese Cobalt (NMC) and $6.5/kWh for Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP) batteries. This is a necessary sovereignty premium that will fall as the EU supply chain matures.

The car CO₂ standards are as critical to industrial delivery as the IAA. Even under a strict Made-in-EU scope, the European Parliament’s position as approved by the leading committee in November 2026 would sharply cut expected EV sales and reduce EU cell demand by 247 GWh between 2027 and 2032, which equals 3.7 million EU made EVs.

Finally, the proposed fleet booster mechanism fails to drive industrial onshoring and relocation if its compliance trigger is set below 85%. Carmakers’ efforts to lower that threshold aim at getting a stamp of approval on their sourcing strategy in China. A major risk for public subsidies ending up benefiting imported cars with Chinese batteries. Attempts to lower the Booster below a 85% threshold should be rejected, especially if combined with weaker battery criteria.

It’s key to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries.

Battery criteria in this deal are too weak to create additional demand and undermine the IAA’s core purpose.

By Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials OfficerBrussels (EU), T&E.

Jordane Faizant, Analyst, FleetsBrussels (EU)

Jeanne Andre, Analyst, Raw MaterialsBrussels (EU)
