#5846. Restoring trust: Embattled CDC, HHS officials on their priorities for 2027

Restoring trust: Embattled CDC, HHS officials on their priorities for 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Federal health officials are focused on rebuilding trust with the public and working across the aisle with the new Congress.

Centers for Disease Control and Prevention Director Erica Schwartz, M.D., and Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., spoke at the Milken Institute’s Future of Health Summit last week in Washington, D.C. Over 1,200 attendees gathered at the event, the theme of which was “better health within reach.”

In a fireside chat, a chipper Schwartz tried to answer a tough line of questioning about Americans’ loss of trust in her agency, controversial vaccine changes, measles and other public health issues.

When asked about her potential disagreements with Kennedy, Schwartz said, “I disagree all the time. He’s a lawyer, I’m a lawyer. […] What I love about the secretary is that he listens to those disagreements. He's not someone who just says, ‘No, I don't have room for that.’ He really does value differing opinions.”

The U.S. has lost its measles elimination status, global health officials announced just this week. On that issue, Schwartz stressed the importance of supporting CDC’s state partners on awareness, prevention and surveillance efforts.

“MMR is the number one way to prevent measles,” Schwartz said. Today’s outbreaks “happen in communities that are very close-knit communities … that typically do not and have not vaccinated their children,” she said. Most cases are currently among kids over the age of five and adults, according to Schwartz.

Under Schwartz’s leadership, the CDC changed its method of reporting measles deaths from real-time reporting by state public health departments to death certificate data, which can face multi-month lags. Schwartz is being investigated by Energy & Commerce Committee Democrats who, during a briefing on CDC’s alleged suppression of the total number of national measles deaths on its dashboard, have called her unresponsive and misleading.

“As the crisis worsens, we are concerned that you continue to direct an effort at CDC to downplay the outbreak and artificially reduce the number of measles deaths that CDC reports to the public, concealing the fact that 10 people have died of measles-related deaths this year alone,” the Committee leaders wrote (PDF) in a letter on Tuesday. “We are expanding our investigation to examine the threat your actions pose to Americans’ health and whether changes to your statutory authority are required.”

During her fireside chat, Schwartz acknowledged that "we've lost trust not only externally with the American public, but also internally with the team that works at the CDC,” she said. To address this, Schwartz wants the public to understand that CDC follows the science, is being transparent and “leading with humility.” She pointed to COVID-era “arrogance” from institutions, directing at instead of conversing with the public.

“I want to be a CDC director that really has a conversation with the American public,” Schwartz said.

Internally, the first priority is to add stability to the body, including hiring leaders within the agency so they have a permanent leadership role. The CDC has also created a strategic hiring plan for 2027 with the input of staff and is looking to its “force extenders” like epidemic intelligence officers and its Public Health Associate Program.

On how she’s planning to work with the new majority-Democratic Congress, Schwartz noted she was encouraged to see more Congress members coming in with a healthcare background, “who understand how these policies will impact patients.” She said is eager to work with the Congressional Doctors Caucus and to work with both sides of the aisle.

“I know that we may not agree on every issue, but I do think there's a common ground where we can actually focus on really trying to make Americans healthy again,” she said.

One of her priorities for 2027 is having a “21st-century CDC” by bringing innovation into the public health sphere. She called out the Biothreat Radar, a proposed wastewater surveillance program about which she recently spoke with Sen. Andy Kim (D-New Jersey). The program’s proposed (PDF) budget is $52 million. The CDC has also tentatively put out a call for new Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP) members, which will depend on a final ruling from the appellate court in the agency’s favor.

At the Summit, Schwartz’s comments did not necessarily satisfy some of the attendees. When asked by Fierce Healthcare for his impressions of the interview, a public health worker in the room who did not wish to be named called her a “traitor."

In his fireside chat closing out the Summit, Secretary Kennedy primarily spoke about food and obesity.

One of HHS' most consequential accomplishments during his tenure, Kennedy said, has been changing the dietary guidelines to be “science-based.” Earlier this year, the White House released new federal dietary guidelines, calling for prioritizing quality protein, healthy fats, fruits, vegetables and whole grains, while avoiding highly processed foods and refined carbs.

The agency is focused on transforming food across a number of sectors, including in Head Start programs—free federal early childhood programs—which have received about $151 million in supplemental HHS awards to strengthen nutrition services. HHS is also focused on rolling out healthy food options in the military, Kennedy said, and within SNAP by allowing states to request waivers to restrict what SNAP dollars are spent on. As of this summer, HHS has launched a Make Hospital Food Healthier Pledge, challenging hospitals to put nutrition at the center of patient care. HHS has also encouraged medical schools to increase their number of education hours on nutrition from an average of two to 40 hours, Kennedy said.

“Real” food sales in grocery stores, Kennedy claimed, went up 8% this year, while obesity rates have dropped by 7%. He acknowledged that the drop in obesity rates is more likely due to GLP-1 access rather than healthier eating. “If we can reduce obesity, we can reduce our healthcare costs,” he said, praising Trump’s Most Favored Nation negotiations for lowering the price of drugs like Ozempic.

“We reward people at every level—the hospitals, the providers, other providers, doctors, insurance companies, pharmaceutical companies—we reward them financially for keeping people sick. And as long as those financial incentives are in place, we are going to be operating a sick-care system rather than a healthcare system,” Kennedy said. HHS has started to change that by shifting more to value-based care, he added, so stakeholders get paid to keep patients healthy “rather than being paid to keep them sick.”

When asked what he wants his legacy as Health Secretary to be, Kennedy echoed Schwartz on the idea of following science. “What I want to do is to end corporate capture and the politicization of science … that Americans once again trust the regulatory agencies, they trust the science that’s coming from them,” he said.

He referred to newer guidelines meant to encourage extra transparency from researchers. In 2026, the HHS Scientific Integrity Policy was updated (PDF) to support publishing raw data and code in publicly accessible repositories, preregistering study protocols and transparently reporting all results.

He said he wants "to make sure that we’re not kowtowing to the food industry, the pharmaceutical industry, all these people who are at the trough taking on the $2.3 trillion that my agency gives out every year. And that instead, that Americans believe that this agency is devoted to their health, that they trust it, and that we are doing the science that we need to find out what is making them sick so that we can all eliminate it as a society.”

Kennedy's interview at the event did not touch on measles or vaccine policy, where the secretary's policies and communications have garnered broader criticism and controversy than his views on nutrition. Tuesday's announcement that the U.S. had lost its measles elimination status garnered a fresh call for Kennedy's resignation among his critics, including the Democratic Doctors Caucus.

Also coinciding with Milken's event was the announcement of the inaugural 2027 cohort of the Elevate Investment Initiative. The effort, part of the Milken Institute’s Women Health Network, aims to mobilize $1 billion in net-new capital for women’s health innovation. The 10 fund managers and companies include Borski Fund, Cross-Border Impact Ventures, Foreground Capital, Meridian Health Ventures, How Women Invest and more. [cut this or move?]

#5845. $1199 — Anyland Rev Pro: An Electric Dirt Bike With a Wheelie Assist Built for Beginners

When I was a kid, I loved to ride. Riding a bike, for me, is synonymous with childhood, in fact. We used them to get ourselves places, but we also just used them to entertain ourselves. Inevitably, this led to popping wheelies, building ramps and trying jumps, and doing all sorts of other stuff my Mom just didn’t want to know about, no matter how much I wanted to brag. As an adult, I still love to ride — as the famous saying from Animal Farm goes, “four wheels good, two wheels mo’ bettah!” (I’m paraphrasing, a lot, but you get the point).

So when Anyland hit us up to do a review of their new Anyland Rev Pro, an all-electric dirt bike capable of 34 mph and assisted wheelies, the inner child in me jumped at the chance.

Wheelies look effortless until you try one. Anyone who has learned on a dirt bike knows the usual lesson plan: too little throttle and the front wheel never lifts, too much and you’re flat on your back. Anyland, a startup electric dirt bike brand, created the Rev Pro as an entry-level electric dirt bike with a controller that lifts the front wheel to a preset angle and holds it there, so a new rider can focus on balance instead of juggling throttle and brake.

The system, which Anyland sells as WELIXON and says it co-engineered with controller maker Moxin, offers three angle settings: 25°, 35°, and 45°. Riders start at the lowest angle and move up as their balance improves. According to Anyland, the controller delivers continuous, smooth power to keep the front end at the chosen angle, which is designed to reduce the risk of a “loop out,” where the bike flips backward, one of the most common ways wheelie learners get hurt. Once you’re ready, you can switch the assist off, and the Rev Pro behaves like a regular throttle-controlled electric dirt bike for practicing manual wheelies.

Underneath the trick, the Rev Pro is a lightweight, mid-drive electric dirt bike. Anyland lists:

The speed modes set the first speed caps as low as 12 mph, and matter for the audience Anyland is targeting: teenagers and adults who are new to dirt bikes. Starting in the lowest mode lets a rider build skills before moving up, and gives parents a way to keep a first-timer in a gentler setting.

The Rev Pro arrives in a compact box and includes all the tools you’ll need to set the bike up. My friend and I (two untrained professionals) did it in about 40 minutes:

In short, the ride is really fun. I found the suspension incredibly responsive and comfortable, the acceleration smooth and quick, and the riding position much more comfortable than I anticipated. It’s been raining non-stop where I live, so while I got this bike a few weeks ago in the mail, I haven’t had a chance to get it up onto the trails yet, so stay tuned for that. Testing it on the dirt sidewalks and running over curbs shows me it will likely be a great addition to my weekends.

A wheelie assist doesn’t make any dirt bike risk-free. Wear a helmet, gloves, and protective gear every time, and start in the lowest speed mode at the 25° setting. Practice using the rear brake, supervise younger riders, and stick to private land or designated off-road areas. Check your local rules before riding anywhere else, since many electric dirt bikes aren’t legal on public roads.

Anyland’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday price starts as low as $1,199, and prices have varied, so check the current price before you buy.

For a startup brand in a crowded field of lookalikes, the Rev Pro’s pitch is simple: a beginner-friendly bike built around one clever feature. If you’ve always wanted to learn a wheelie without the bruises, it’s worth a look.

#5843. Two Tuberculosis Regimens Containing Novel Agents Show Promise

— Phase II platform trial's design could speed development of new combinations for TB

Two four-drug regimens that combined new agents with commonly used drugs showed promise in tuberculosis (TB), and should be evaluated further, results from a phase II trial suggested.

The time to positivity slope -- the rate of change from baseline to week 12 in the time to Mycobacterium tuberculosis growth in a positive sputum culture, with a steeper time indicating faster TB clearance -- was 0.129 log10 (days) per week with investigational ganfeborole plus bedaquiline, pretomanid, and moxifloxacin (BPMG) and 0.142 log10 (days) per week with investigational BTZ-043 plus bedaquiline, delamanid, and moxifloxacin (BDMT), as compared with 0.118 log10 (days) per week with standard isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol (HRZE), for relative differences of 9.1% and 20.5%, respectively.

The posterior probability of superiority was 0.83 and 0.99 with the BPMG and BDMT regimens versus HRZE, reported Nicholas Paton, MD, of National University of Singapore, and colleagues in the New England Journal of Medicine.

At week 48, 80% of those in the BPMG group and 93% of those in the BDMT group had favorable-outcome status compared with 89% of the HRZE group.

"Our findings show the potential of large-scale, early-phase clinical trials for evaluating multiple regimens simultaneously with a design that permits the use of early data on treatment failure or relapse to inform selection of regimens for further evaluation," Paton and colleagues wrote.

The four-drug combinations will move on to testing in the phase II trial's second stage.

In an accompanying editorial, Eric Rubin, MD, PhD, of the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, cautioned that the number of new TB agents in the development pipeline "creates a combinational nightmare."

This study outlines "a potentially very useful approach to prioritizing which regimens should be tested in very long and expensive phase III trials," Rubin noted. "The use of phase II trials with a design similar to that of the current trial might get some of these new agents into later-phase clinical trials more rapidly."

Despite the encouraging results, it's not clear whether they will lead to improved TB outcomes, he added. "Although almost all drug combinations can kill the bulk of bacteria, a small subpopulation seems to be responsible for the lengthy regimens that are necessary to cure all patients," Rubin wrote. The trial's time-to-positivity results "most likely tell us more about the bulk population than about the rarer bacteria that might survive for extended times."

While the current trial "provides useful guidelines for prioritizing late-phase trials," those later trials will still be critical, he pointed out. Referencing the movie "The Princess Bride," Rubin reminded investigators and clinicians alike that, "as Miracle Max says, 'You rush a miracle man, you get rotten miracles.'"

The PARADIGM-4TB adaptive platform trial assigned participants with rifampin-susceptible pulmonary TB to receive standard HRZE for 24 weeks; bedaquiline, delamanid, and moxifloxacin for 16 weeks; or one of 10 new four-drug combinations for 16 weeks. The new regimens contained ganfeborole, BTZ-043, or both, with combinations of bedaquiline, delamanid, moxifloxacin, pyrazinamide, pretomanid, and linezolid.

Ganfeborole blocks an enzyme essential to mycobacterial protein synthesis, while BTZ-043 inhibits an essential enzyme in cell-wall synthesis.

The study enrolled 401 adults at 14 clinical sites in South Africa, Tanzania, Uganda, Vietnam, and Moldova. Patients had TB symptoms or chest radiography evidence, as well as a medium or high semiquantitative result on the Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra assay. Patients with HIV were excluded. Median patient age was 30 years, 84% were male, 68% had a high bacterial load, and 77% had cavitation on a chest radiograph.

Severe adverse events occurred in 38% of the BPMG group, 28% of the BDMT group, and 24% of the HRZE group. Among the trial's 118 patients who had a serious adverse event, 45% had a decrease from baseline in creatinine clearance, "which contributed to excess severe adverse events in some new-regimen groups," Paton and team noted. Most of those patients had creatinine clearance fall by more than 30%.

Study limitations included the open-label design and the small sizes of each treatment group. Time to positivity is a surrogate endpoint that hasn't always predicted clinical endpoints, the authors cautioned. While the study population included only those with rifampin-susceptible TB, any treatments' target clinical population would include patients with rifampin-resistant TB.

HLTH 2026: American Hospital Association, West Health Institute announce new collaboration with BD" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The American Hospital Association (AHA) and West Health Institute announced Tuesday a new collaboration with medtech company Becton, Dickinson and Company (BD) aimed at accelerating the adoption of technologies shown to improve patient care.

#5841. Sierra Club Submits Comments Urging SEC to Preserve Shareholder Rights & Climate Accountability

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Thursday, Sierra Club submitted two comment letters opposing the Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) proposal to rescind Rule 14a-8, which allows shareholders to put proposals addressing climate risks and other corporate issues before fellow investors for a vote, and amend Rule 14a-4(c), which governs how companies may exercise discretionary voting authority over shareholder proposals. The Sierra Club submitted both a technical comment and a grassroots comment, reflecting concerns from over 1,100 of its members and supporters.

Together the submissions urge the SEC to withdraw its proposed changes and preserve investors’ ability to raise concerns directly with corporate management and press companies to address material risks, including climate-related risks, that can affect their long-term financial interests.

Jessye Waxman, Campaign Advisor on Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“Climate change and other systemic risks have real consequences for companies, investors, and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors an important way to bring those risks to management’s attention, push for meaningful action, and hold companies accountable when they fail to act. The SEC’s proposals would weaken those tools, making it harder for investors to protect their long-term financial interests and leaving corporate management with less accountability for the risks its decisions create. The SEC’s proposals would shield corporate management from meaningful shareholder scrutiny, while undermining the very investor protections the Commission is charged with upholding.”

Rule 14a-8 establishes when public companies must include qualifying shareholder proposals in the materials they send investors ahead of shareholder meetings. The SEC’s proposed repeal would leave decisions about shareholder proposals to state law and individual companies’ governing documents, eliminating a federal framework that investors have used for more than 80 years.

Sierra Club’s technical comments explain that material risks, like climate change, can both have significant financial consequences for companies and create systemic financial risks that extend across investment portfolios and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors a way to bring these concerns to company leadership and fellow shareholders, including by surfacing risks that management may overlook or have limited incentives to address. The letter emphasizes that, for decades, this process has served the broader market by helping investors collectively identify and address financially material risks.

The letter warns that replacing a consistent federal framework with varying state laws and corporate bylaws could make shareholder engagement less predictable and push disputes over proposals into costly litigation. It points to the increase in shareholder lawsuits challenging proposal exclusions during the 2026 proxy season as an indication that disputes do not disappear when the SEC steps back from administering the process; instead, they can shift to more costly and uncertain avenues.

The submission also opposes proposed amendments to Rule 14a-4(c), which would expand company management’s discretionary authority to vote shareholders’ proxies on proposals excluded from company proxy materials, unless shareholders affirmatively opt out. Sierra Club’s letter warns that these changes could allow management to vote on proposals that investors have not had an adequate opportunity to evaluate.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5839. Pacific Power Begins New Customer Protections Against Data Centers

Regulators approve new agreement to ensure data centers pay for their own energy costs

SALEM, Oregon — The Oregon Public Utility Commission has approved an agreement between advocates and Pacific Power. Now, Pacific Power customers have a strong suite of protections against data centers’ impact on Oregonians’ energy bills.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, new Pacific Power data centers will be held responsible for their own energy costs. These new rules will only apply to future data centers and existing ones making significant investments, which regulators identified as the most pressing challenge to address. Right now, the utility has very few existing data centers on its system.

“With a long line of data centers waiting at Pacific Power’s door, I’m confident that we have the right protections at the right time,” said Bob Jenks, Executive Director of Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board. “We were able to stop more extreme impacts on power bills just in time.”

Data centers over 100 MW will also have to pay a small surcharge to fund energy efficiency upgrades for low-income households. This includes funding for deep home retrofits, lowering energy use across the grid while saving customers on monthly bills.

“Many of the households we serve are already struggling to afford their energy bills and risk having their utilities shut off. Data centers will help fund weatherization, energy efficiency upgrades, and the critical home repairs that often prevent our clients from accessing these improvements in the first place,” said Charity Fain, Executive Director of Community Energy Project. “Requiring data centers to cover the costs of the energy infrastructure they need, rather than passing those costs on to residential customers, is an important protection for low-income households, seniors, and people with disabilities.”

Under this order, if there is a power emergency, Pacific Power can prioritize critical energy use for other customers, like renters and homeowners, by curtailing service to data centers. Increased electric demand by data centers strains the grid and increases the potential for blackouts during heat waves or hard freezes.

“This is a big win that puts Oregon at the forefront of managing data center growth the right way,” said Joshua Basofin, Oregon Clean Energy Program Director at Climate Solutions. “It protects Oregonians from rising costs and strengthens grid resilience. Data centers should not harm our ability to access essential electricity to power our lives and keep us safe all year.”

Regulators also ruled that new data centers can only be connected to Pacific Power’s grid if there is enough emissions-free electricity available to serve them. Under HB 2021, Pacific Power is required by law to serve all customers with 100% emissions-free electricity by 2040. This new rule will help manage costs for other customers outside of data centers during the transition to clean energy.

“We have long known that clean energy is the most affordable form of energy,” said Ben Brint, Senior Climate Program Director at Oregon Environmental Council. “This ruling is a win for data center accountability, and for Oregonians across the state — especially frontline communities already bearing the brunt of climate impacts and rising energy costs. It will save ratepayers money in the short and long run, allocating costs appropriately while advancing Oregon’s 100% clean energy goals.”

This change also comes at a time when many Oregonians are calling for broader changes to how the state handles data centers. Impacts on data centers are poised to be an issue again at the Oregon State Legislature in 2027.

“The POWER Act demonstrated the people’s power to fight back against the rising energy rates and ecosystem harms that large data centers inflict,” said Emily Bowes, Policy Strategist & Acting Director at Sierra Club Oregon. “In 2027, alongside new and returning climate champions, we will continue to fight for a clean energy transition in Salem and statewide.”

In 2025, the Oregon Legislature passed the POWER Act to help utilities rein in data centers’ added costs to Oregonians’ energy bills. Last fall, Portland General Electric began the work to roll out this new law in practice. In January, Pacific Power started a similar process for its customers. The next step is to address the few existing data centers on Pacific Power’s system, starting as early as summer 2027. It is unclear if the final phase will include rate adjustments.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5838. Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September

Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hospitals’ operations took another dip in September amid a slew of ongoing headwinds including weakened payer mix and rising costs, healthcare advisory firm Kaufman Hall reported alongside warnings of a “historically large portion” of the nation’s hospitals running on negative or near-zero operating margins.

The firm’s latest monthly benchmark report, released Tuesday, showed a calendar year-to-date operating margin index of 1.4% and a single-month operating margin index of 2.4% in September, the latter of which was a decline from August’s 3.0% operating margin (all including health system allocations for shared services costs).

The performance decline wasn’t for want of patients. Daily discharges and adjusted discharges both rose 3% from August to September, and are generally above the numbers from last year. Operating room minutes per calendar day also rose 6% from August—an encouraging sign for hospitals whose surgical slowdowns had hamstrung acuity—but remain 1% below September of last year.

The increased foot traffic also grew revenues, with daily net operating revenue rising 4% from August and 6% year over year while gross operating revenue rose 4% month to month and 8% year over year. Much of the increase came on the outpatient side, where daily revenue increased 5% from August and 10% compared to September 2025. Net patient service revenue per adjusted discharge was more mixed, rising 2% from August but remaining 1% below September 2025.

However, hospitals’ margins are being challenged by persistent bad debt and charity care burdens, Kaufman Hall’s data show. Daily bad debt and charity care may have remained flat from August, but is 17% higher than September 2025 and 16% higher when comparing both years’ first nine months. As a percentage of gross operating revenue, they’re 6% higher than Sept. 2025 and 8% higher year to date.

On the spending side, daily total expense rose 3% from August and is 5% higher than September 2025, primarily reflecting the heightened volumes even as hospitals held their average length of stay flat month over month or 1% shorter year over year. The expenses were more pronounced on the non-labor side, where increases on a per adjusted discharge basis were higher than labor’s per adjusted discharge decline.

"Hospitals continue to operate in an environment marked by significant financial variability," Erik Swanson, managing director and Data and Analytics group leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. “Many organizations continue to face mounting pressure from uncompensated care and rising expenses, underscoring the importance of proactive operational and financial strategies that build long-term resilience."

Another amplifying market trend is the “bifurcation of hospital performance” often highlighted by the firm and others that monitor multi-organization financial trends, such as credit rating agencies.

"The margin gap between higher and lower-performing hospitals has widened to 19 percentage points, with a historically large portion of hospitals operating at negative or very thin margins,” the report reads. “Even slight revenue reductions threaten organizations, which face increasingly difficult decisions on future operations, care delivery, and expense control.”

The firm encouraged hospitals to take “deliberate, proactive steps” to strengthen their financial situations, particularly as macro reimbursement trends stiffen and the site of care delivery shifts from the hospital to outpatient settings.

Kaufman Hall’s hospital reports pull data from more than 1,300 nationwide hospitals as collected by parent company Strata Decision Technology.

Accompanying the monthly hospital benchmark was also Kaufman Hall’s quarterly numbers on more than 200,000 employed physicians and advanced practice providers, which painted a much rosier picture for health system and hospital employers.

It showed, for the first time in five years of data collection, that healthcare employers’ investment/subsidy per provider and per physician has declined year over year (by 1%), and “across every cohort, including primary care, medical specialty, surgical specialty and hospital-based specialty.”

Kaufman Hall attributed the rare turnaround to revenue growth and sustained productivity increases that together outpaced expenses.

"Medical groups made meaningful performance improvements this quarter, achieving gains in revenue, productivity and thoughtful workforce management," Matthew Bates, managing director and Physician Enterprise Service Line leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. "The growing utilization of [advanced practice providers] and ongoing focus on operational and team effectiveness are helping organizations manage financial pressures while increasing access to care.”

#5837. Simple Lab Test May Predict Recurrence Risk in Most Common Type of Breast Cancer

— Higher eosinophil levels predicted better outcomes, better response to CDK4/6 inhibitors

Higher levels of circulating eosinophils had a significant association with improved recurrence-free survival (RFS) and distant metastasis-free survival (DMFS) in hormone receptor (HR)-positive breast cancer, a large retrospective analysis showed.

With an eosinophil cutoff of 0.08 × 109/L, patients with eosinophil counts above the cutoff had significantly better RFS (P=0.001) and DMFS (P=0.002). Higher levels of circulating eosinophils remained an independent predictor of improved outcomes in multivariable-adjusted statistical models. Reductions in the RFS and DMFS hazard ratios ranged between 50% and 60% before and after adjustment. The results were replicated in a large validation cohort of patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer, albeit with smaller reductions in the hazard ratios.

Additionally, higher eosinophil counts predicted benefit from treatment with a CDK4/6 inhibitor, reported Zhi-Ming Shao, MD, PhD, of Fudan University Shanghai Cancer Center in China, and colleagues in the Journal of Clinical Oncology.

"Circulating eosinophil count is a robust prognostic biomarker, including during endocrine therapy," the authors stated. "Although the prognostic [hazard ratio] for eosinophil count differed between the discovery and expanded cohorts, this reflects differences in cohort design, case mix, and sample size; importantly, both estimates were directionally consistent, showing a protective effect."

"Second, [eosinophil count] provides significant additive prognostic value beyond the 21-gene risk score, suggesting that eosinophil levels in peripheral blood capture an immune dimension distinct from tumor genomics," they continued. "Third, eosinophils predict adjuvant CDK4/6 inhibitor efficacy, optimizing patient selection... . Importantly, the treatment-by-eosinophil interaction remained robust and the direction of effect was consistently protective across all analyses, supporting eosinophil count as a predictive biomarker regardless of absolute [hazard ratio] magnitude."

The findings add to previous studies showing that higher peripheral lymphocyte count and lower neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio are associated with better outcomes in early-stage breast cancer, primarily HR-negative subtypes, noted the authors of an accompanying editorial. Collectively, they highlight the biomarker potential of readily available laboratory parameters.

"None of these prognostic factors is in routine use, however, despite being readily available from a complete blood count for virtually every patient with breast cancer globally," wrote Peter Savas, MBBS, PhD, of Peter MacCallum Cancer Center in Melbourne, Australia, and Roberto Salgado, MD, PhD, of ZAS Hospitals in Antwerp, Belgium. "To achieve greater clinical relevance for circulating biomarkers, we can seek guidance from the success of risk-of-recurrence assays and the popularity of prognostic calculators such as PREDICT breast. These tools take a carefully considered approach to implementation, integrating standard clinicopathologic variables in a form that supports shared decision making and communication with patients."

"This should be the aim for any proposed biomarker that hopes to see widespread uptake, in addition to robust analytical and clinical validation."

HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer accounts for about two-thirds of all new cases of breast cancer, and despite treatment advances, late recurrence and distant relapse have persisted, the authors noted in their introduction. The absence of simple, noninvasive, blood-based biomarkers to monitor dynamic systemic immune status remains an unmet need. In particular, robust biomarkers to predict therapeutic benefit from CDK4/6 inhibitors have remained elusive, despite the drugs' established role in treating patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer.

To date research into immune prognostic markers has focused primarily on tumor-infiltrating lymphocytes, ignoring the "prognostically relevant" myeloid compartment, the authors continued. To address the information void, investigators evaluated the prognostic potential of eosinophils, which previous studies have shown can prime antitumor T-cell responses. Investigators hypothesized that circulating eosinophil count could serve as a dynamic systemic marker of immune competence, distinct from tumor lymphoid infiltration.

Shao and colleagues retrospectively analyzed eosinophil counts in 472 patients with HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer treated with surgery and followed for a median of 83.1 months. The cohort had a median eosinophil count of 0.08 × 109/L. The cohort comprised 259 patients with eosinophil counts higher than the median and 213 with counts at the median or lower.

The data showed that patients with eosinophil counts exceeding the median had a 54% lower hazard for RFS (95% CI 0.28-0.77) and a 56% lower hazard for DMFS (95% CI 0.25-0.78). Multiple variable analyses produced hazard ratios of 0.49 (P=0.007) and 0.50 (P=0.017) for RFS and DMFS, respectively. Among 406 patients treated with endocrine therapy, higher eosinophil counts predicted better RFS (HR 0.41, 95% CI 0.24-0.70, P=0.001) and DMFS (HR 0.39, 95% CI 0.21-0.72, P=0.002).

To evaluate the functional impact of circulating eosinophil infiltration, the investigators implanted eosinophils into the mammary fat pads of mouse models of HR-positive/HER2-negative breast cancer. The results showed significantly reduced tumor growth in animals that received eosinophils versus those that did not.

Finally, Shao and colleagues investigated circulating eosinophils' association with RFS and DMFS in a validation cohort of 23,177 patients, 10,488 with eosinophil counts exceeding the median and 10,689 with values at or below the median. They had a median follow-up of 32.6 months. The high-eosinophil group had a statistically significant 17% lower risk of distant metastasis (95% CI 0.70-0.99, P=0.034). The association remained significant in a multivariable analysis that included age, nodal status, tumor size, and Ki67 value.

A separate analysis of 3,471 high-risk patients treated with adjuvant CDK4/6 inhibitors showed that the therapy improved DMFS overall, but patients with higher eosinophil counts derived greater benefit (HR 0.25, 95% CI 0.12-0.48, P<0.0001) than those with lower counts (HR 0.59, 95% CI 0.38-0.93, P=0.022).

The “camper” version of the ID Buzz has a few special features for this. Also, just to reiterate, it’s not cheap. Interestingly, you can get this camper version of the van in either the 5-seat or 7-seat version of the ID Buzz.

#5835. You Can Get A Free Classic Car And Garage In GTA 6, But Only Until Tomorrow

It's finally here. Can you believe it, fans of video game carnage? The internet will have to find some other source of amusement other than the "we got so-and-so before we got 'GTA VI,'" because guess what? Thirteen years after the release of the previous iteration, Rockstar Games has finally made the sixth installment of the wildly popular "Grand Theft Auto" franchise available to the masses. Well, those members of the masses with access to a PlayStation 5, a PS5 Pro, an Xbox Series X, or an Xbox Series S, that is. If that describes you, Rockstar might just have a surprise waiting for your first visit to Vice City in "GTA 6."

Of course, with a name like Grand Theft Auto, the automotive element of the action-packed title hasn't taken a back seat. We've been spotting the new cars in "GTA 6" since the extended trailer dropped, and connecting them to their real-life counterparts. For a very specific group of early adopters, logging onto the game will yield more than just the excitement of playing the long-awaited title. Rockstar is giving some players a free classic car and a garage to park it in.

Like it or not, you've probably encountered the "GTA 6" hype in some manner or another. That is, unless you've been living under a rock. On a deserted island. An island on another planet. The twice-delayed game is a big deal, and it's been available for pre-order since June 2026. Rockstar Games is giving out the Vintage Vice City Pack to those players who pre-ordered "GTA 6," and it comes with a handful of goodies. Most notably, the pack includes the '55 Vapid Stanier, a two-tone yellow-on-white classic two-door hardtop.

You might take one look at the Vapid Stanier and think, "huh, that looks awfully familiar." There's a reason for that. For starters, the two-tone Stanier is meant to resemble a 1955 Ford Fairlane Club Sedan and serves as a direct nod to the 1955 Oldsmobile Super 88. Not just any Super 88, but the very specific landmark car parked in front of the Avalon Hotel on Ocean Drive in Miami. And why not? Vice City serves as the Miami-based setting for the game, and the real-life Avalon Hotel boasts that its Super 88 is "one of the most photographed cars in America." Of course, the real Super 88 has a convertible top, whereas the game's Vapid Stanier has a painted hardtop.

The yellow-on-white classic is a fitting way to hit the streets of Vice City, but it's not the only treat in the pack. In addition to the '55 Vapid Stanier, players will get a special garage. Qualifying players will get a task in their Exploration map tab, which will direct them to the Ocean View Hotel. That's where they'll find the classic car and the garage. You'll likely want a garage to safely store your Stanier, too. If the new game is anything like "GTA Online," things will likely get quite explode-y.

So, are you afraid you missed your chance? Fret not. If you place your order for "GTA 6" today, you can still get your hands on the Vintage Vice City Pack. For now, the offer expires at the end of today. It also doesn't matter if your physical copy of "GTA 6" is in the mail; as long as you place your order before the end of the day, you can get the expansion pack.

Now, it might seem outlandish and unrealistic to expect a free classic car and a complimentary garage anywhere other than the fictional state of Leonida. But Rockstar seems to be trying to dial up the realism factor in its latest blockbuster game. Don't believe it? Unlike the campaign or online free-for-all of the fifth "Grand Theft Auto" title, players will have to fill up cars with gasoline virtually in "GTA 6" to keep driving. That's right. No infinitely full gas tanks anymore. We'll just have to see if the Vapid Stanier is a dainty fuel sipper or a guzzler.

If that wasn't enough, Rockstar also wants players to experience more life-like collisions. How? Well, the developers made sure "GTA 6" leaned into advanced physics for more realistic car crashes. So, you know, try not to hit stuff in your classic car.

#5834. HLTH 2026 Daily Tracker: F1 mania takes over HLTH; Why DJ Steve Aoki is investing in innovative science

HLTH 2026 Daily Tracker: F1 mania takes over HLTH; Why DJ Steve Aoki is investing in innovative science" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Fierce Healthcare team is on the ground at HLTH 2026. Stick with this news tracker to catch all the highlights from the show floor, and click here for additional coverage from the conference.

DJ and music producer Steve Aoki told the HLTH audience that his father's passing was the catalyst for his interest in healthcare.

Learning more about what caused his death, and if it could have been prevented, drew him to the impacts of dementia and cancer. And in exploring that, he said he decided he wanted to invest in the people who are pursuing the "science fiction concepts" in healthcare, and support cutting edge innovation.

Aoki said that his celebrity status made it easier to get into a room with people leading the charge on innovation, and learn as much as he could.

"I was just as intrigued as working with a big artist in the studio or playing a big show," he said.

It was with that learning that he established Aoki Foundation, an organization he said is dedicated to bringing back the science and investing in what's next.

"People are excited about science fiction, about the possibility of doing things that are impossible," Aoki said. "And I feel like the superheroes of our time are like right right in our midst, and it's about it's about highlighting their journey and giving them a spotlight."

It’s been several weeks since Doximity integrated Wolters Kluwer Health’s UpToDate into its AI clinical decision support tool.

Doximity VP of Clinical AI Partnerships Kamal Menghrajani, M.D., told Fierce Healthcare at HLTH said the integration gives her “a little bit more confidence” in her own practice.

“Now I can use UpToDate to supplement and feel like what I'm doing at the point of care really is the best thing for the patient,” she said.

Menghrajani, who previously served as assistant director for cancer innovation and public health at the White House, took on the role with Doximity approximately four months ago.

“[I get to] be the physician voice and represent the interests of doctors within the company,” she said. “And think about what it looks like to actually take the tools we're building and serve doctors better.”

Menghrajani said the rapid expansion of the company’s AI suite has been a key takeaway, adding that a focus for the company is what physicians want out of AI. “I've seen Doximity evolve to meet all of those needs,” Menghrajani said.

As a range of AI tools saturate the industry, Menghrajani said the biggest use she has seen amongst clinicians is ambient scribes. “I think that has been a real game changer in terms of actual practice,” she said.

On the HLTH show floor, you may have noticed a theme.

Las Vegas is abuzz with the upcoming Formula 1 race, which is taking place on Saturday, Nov. 21. The city has been preparing for the Las Vegas Grand Prix, which is on the Strip, for weeks, and HLTH is taking place at one of the hubs for F1 excitement, The Venetian.

So at this year's conference, each of the stages is themed around racing: the Velocity Stage, the Ferrari Stage, the Monacco Stage. And each breakout track is referred to as a "circuit." In the conference livery, HLTH mascot Petunia is show driving a race car.

Activations on the show floor also play into the F1 theme. A racing simulation game is set up for attendees near the Pharma Force stage, and swag giveaways allude to the race.

Panelists at a HLTH session discussing the uptake of ICHRA — scratch that, CHOICE — faced a challenge around the nomenclature.

Individual coverage health reimbursement arrangements, or ICHRAs, represent a growing area of interest for employers and insurers participating in the individual market. But on Sept. 3, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services announced that it would instead refer to these models as CHOICE, which stands for Custom Health Option and Individual Care Expense.

In these models, an employer offers enrollees a stipend to purchase coverage on the individual market rather than securing a traditional employer-sponsored plan. ICHRA (or CHOICE) has gained traction with employers that may otherwise struggle to offer health coverage.

Moderator Joseph Mercer, senior managing director at Marwood Group, joked that the panelists had all agreed to call these plans CHOICE rather than ICHRA. But throughout the session, slip-ups drew a chuckle.

Even the panel's title made reference to the evolution of the name: "The ICHRA (CHOICE) Debate."

Healthcare platform Simple HealthKit announced Tuesday a partnership with Waters Corporation to expand access to at-home cervical cancer screening.

The collaboration aims to expand access to BD’s Onclarity HPV Self-Collection Kit, which is tested with the BD Onclarity HPV Assay  and Simple HealthKit's artificial intelligence-powered direct-to-patient-provider platform.

"We envision a future where personalized, world-class healthcare is accessible to everyone, regardless of where they live,” said Sheena Menezes, Ph.D, Simple HealthKit CEO and co-founder, in a statement. “Our AI-powered direct-to-patient and provider platform brings that vision to life by connecting people with the right education, intelligent personalized engagement, and clinical care at every step.”

Menezes continued, “Through partnerships with innovators like Waters Corporation, we are empowering individuals to take charge of their health while making seamless, personalized care accessible at scale.”

Oura CEO joined Counsel Health CEO and founder Muthu Alagappan Tuesday morning to discuss the wearable company’s recently announced $625 million acquisition of the virtual primary care company.

Hale said Oura selected Counsel as a partner after reviewing the landscape, and “uptake” from customers was “very positive.”

“We started talking seriously about taking that partnership,” Hale said. “And while we had been dating for a while, we thought maybe it was time to get married.”

Hale said the agreement was “really driven” by Oura customers using Counsel in “really novel and interesting ways.”

Alagappan said one of the most exciting aspects of the partnership is the potential impact of AI-fueled access to physicians on preventive care, adding the daily engagement of wearing Oura rings struck him.

“The buy-in from almost all users is tremendous, and so you enable primary care with the thing that has always been missing, which is engagement,” he said. “And now we have a way to incentivize, motivate patients to make the changes that actually prevent disease, alongside the screenings and lab tests that are also very important.”

During a Monday fireside chat, CMS Deputy Administrator and Chief Product Officer Amy Gleason said HHS and OCR colleagues “have done an amazing job” at looking at the issue to “remove friction from the patient experience.”

Gleason said there are three main components of the rule: the designation of a “personal health app,” identity verification and speed. “It should be almost instantaneous with electronic data, and it also says that this information is free to the patient,” she said.

Throughout the session with Zus Health CEO and co-founder Jonathan Bush, Gleason also touted the agency’s voluntary CMS Health Tech Ecosystem initiative.

“The idea of this was to stop waiting on regulation, like healthcare does for some reason, and to just charge forward and get the technology and people that are willing to go faster to work on it,” Gleason said. “And we set up a bunch of different technology working groups that are really about taking the friction out of the system.”

Evvy CEO and co-founder Priyanka Jain spoke with Fierce Healthcare Monday about research gaps in women’s health and how her company looks to bridge them through vaginal microbiome testing.

Jain said women were excluded from medical research from 1977 to 1993 after a drug being prescribed off-label for morning sickness was causing birth defects, adding the ramifications ar still being seen today.

“Women to this day are diagnosed on average four years later than men across over 700 diseases,” Jain said. “And it's hard to not point right back to the fact that we didn't study women until 1993.”

To bridge these gaps, Evvy offers an at-home vaginal health test with clinician-reviewed results and personalized treatment plans. “If we've essentially never studied women, then there must be so many biomarkers in the female body that we under leverage in our definition of health and disease, and so that's our focus,” Jain said.

Jain added Evvy uses AI and precision medicine to “actually predict the right treatment programs” for each patient using the biomarkers.

And, Jain said women’s health needs attention, awareness and funding when asked about recent Trump administration initiatives to focus on women’s health.

“I’m really excited to see the attention and awareness that has been happening,” Jain said. “But now we need to actually move dollars.”

Department of Health and Human Services Chief Counselor Chris Klomp said the administration is focused on bringing in top tech talent as it looks to make the massive, but aging, government insurance system work better.

Klomp spoke on a panel Monday morning at HLTH, where he discussed the challenges in harvesting the data necessary to transform Medicare, Medicaid and other key government programs. Klomp was recently nominated to serve as Deputy Secretary at HHS and is a rising star in the White House.

He said that technology can play a critical role in accelerate processes that have been historically inefficient and time consuming. At the Food and Drug Administration, for example, tech is hastening the process of developing drugs and making approvals, a timeline that previously taken a decade or more.

But embracing technology in this way isn't compromising safety for these medications, Klomp said.

In addition, he said the administration is leaning on the personal nature of healthcare to bring in workers with the tech know-how necessary to drive critical advancements and change.

"Most people don't care about healthcare unless you work in it," he said. "People also start to care about healthcare when you lose your health, or someone you love loses their health. And than it quickly becomes the most important thing to you."

"We're using that as an opportunity to drive requirement, drive amazing talent," Klomp said.

Houston Methodist announced Monday it has expanded its advanced inpatient monitoring care to include rural hospitals throughout Kansas.

Participating hospitals will provide patients with Medtronic’s BioButton, which continuously monitors vital signs, while the Houston Methodist remote monitoring team will review the data and work with bedside nurses.

The vital monitoring devices are used at all eight acute-care hospitals in the Houston Methodist system, across more than 2,500 inpatient beds, according to the Nov. 11 announcement.

“Innovation is most meaningful when we identify patient needs, take what works or doesn't work and find new ways to extend the positive impact,” said Sara Loewy, Houston Methodist virtual care vice president, in a statement. “What we’ve built in Houston has the potential to reach much further. By thoughtfully extending this model into new care settings, our goal with this program is to demonstrate how our system’s existing technology and clinical expertise can strengthen access and improve outcomes in rural communities.”

Loewy added the expansion is an opportunity to “explore how the capabilities of a major health system can be shared across geographic boundaries.”

While 72% of health tech startups lead with “AI-powered” in their descriptors, zero surveyed venture capitalists in a new report from HLTH and SolComms said the phrase increased their confidence.

Independently surveyed health tech reporters also ranked “AI-powered” as one of the most overused phrases in pitching–in addition to “revolutionizing/transforming” and “disrupting.”

Alongside these survey responses, researchers compared–and scored–the messaging of 72 U.S.-based, venture-backed health tech startups at series A to series C for the “Healthtech Attention Economy Report.” Startups’ messaging across their website and social media was scored on four main dimensions: clarity, differentiation, proof and policy awareness.

SolComms Vice President of HealthTech Isabella Morreale said in a statement the companies that will get attention within the next year “are leading with proof, not adjectives.”

“That means speaking to clinical data, measurable outcomes, and validation from third parties,” Morreale said. “Founder-led storytelling will also continue to stand out. Among the data, one common through-thread for investor and reporter interest was founders with a real point of view on the industry.”

Morreale continued, “Reporters don't need another company description; they need a source who can help them explain what's happening in healthcare.”

Less than half (42.2%) of patients say communications from healthcare providers “often” or “always” feel relevant to their needs, a new report from Lirio found.

Analysts at the behavioral science and AI-powered behavioral intelligence platform polled 1,500 adults across the U.S. for the survey. While 58% of respondents report communications not consistently feeling relevant to their needs, 55.6% rate overall communication experiences with healthcare organizations as “good” or “excellent.”

When asked why healthcare communications are ignored altogether, respondents report generic or irrelevant content (24.4%), too many similar messages (21.8%) and confusing, jargon-filled messages (18.8%) as top reasons.

Nearly half of respondents (47.9%) report the potential value of more personalized, better-timed messages as an “8, 9 or 10” for how much it could help them make better care decisions and stay engaged.

Lirio Chief Behavioral Officer Amy Bucher, Ph.D., said in a statement patients “are not rejecting healthcare communications outright.”

“They are telling us, in specific and measurable terms, that the system is talking at them instead of to them,” Bucher said. "The instinct in healthcare has long been to solve engagement with more outreach - more emails, more reminders, more portals. This data shows that volume is not the problem. What is missing is relevance — content and timing designed for where a specific patient is in their own health journey, rather than a single message broadcast to an entire population.”

Bucher added the healthcare organizations that close the communication gap will be “the ones that use behavioral data to determine the right message, the right channel, and the right moment for each individual patient, which is the model Lirio was built to deliver."

Community-based specialty palliative care provider Vynca is expanding its reach into four more states.

Patients will now be able to access Vynca’s services in Georgia, Hawaii, New Jersey and Texas. Vynca is one of the first practices approved to provide services under New Jersey’s Community-Based Palliative Care Medicaid benefit, launched in April, according to the Nov. 16 announcement.

Founded in 2013, the company’s hybrid care model combines in-home visits with virtual clinical support and predictive technology, and executives say it has shown 43% fewer emergency department visits and 52% less hospital stays within six months of enrollment.

“Health plan and government program leaders understand the value of community-based palliative care for people with serious illness,” said Darren Schulte, M.D., Vynca CEO, in a statement. “Our expansion in four key states reflects growing momentum to expand specialty outpatient palliative care outside of hospices. The key is to broaden access and convenience for the entire eligible population by offering home-based options. Too often, patients are limited to seeking palliative care when available in the community, at hospital-based clinics and facilities.”

#5833. Leapmotor Produces Its 2 Millionth EV

Leapmotor just produced its 2 millionth vehicle, all EVs of course. The EV startup has seen a meteoric rise in the highly competitive Chinese EV market, and increasingly abroad. Of course, most of us in the US and even in Europe know nothing or next to nothing about Leapmotor, but it has clearly been more effective than hundreds or even thousands of other companies that have tried to launch and scale up EV production.

One interesting thing to note regarding this milestone: it’s peanuts compared to Leapmotor’s plans for the next few years. In 2026 alone, Leapmotor plans to produce one million vehicles, and it’s on track to do so. That means that, this year, the company plans to produce as many vehicles as half of Leapmotor’s cumulative total at the moment. So, it will just be sometime in the middle of next year that the company will reach 3 million cumulative EVs produced.

The company is now producing more than 100,000 vehicles a month — for four months running.

Naturally, the company’s plan is to keep growing. As part of that effort, it just launched a new model, the Lafa 5 compact hatchback, with pre-sales starting earlier today. Also, another new model, the D19 SUV, just had its 50,000th unit produced.

So, yes, keep an eye on Leapmotor. It’s already a wildly successful young EV company, and it looks like it is going to continue growing fast.

#5832. First siRNA Therapy Approved for Myasthenia Gravis

— Cemdisiran is the second C5 inhibitor the FDA has authorized in a week

The FDA approved subcutaneous cemdisiran (Livsayis), a small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapy, to treat generalized myasthenia gravis in adults who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody-positive, Regeneron announced Wednesday.

Cemdisiran targets complement component 5 (C5), inhibiting terminal complement activation, a key driver of AChR-positive myasthenia gravis. It is the first approved siRNA therapy for myasthenia gravis.

The drug is the second C5 inhibitor the FDA has authorized to treat myasthenia gravis in a week, following the agency's approval of gefurulimab (Klygefa) last Friday.

The approval of cemdisiran was based on data from the 24-week phase III NIMBLE trial. For the primary outcome, Myasthenia Gravis-Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) scores improved from baseline by 4.5 points in the cemdisiran group versus 2.2 points in the placebo group, corresponding to an adjusted improvement of 2.3 points (P=0.0005). MG-ADL improvements were seen as early as 2 weeks after starting treatment.

A key secondary outcome showed a 4.2-point improvement in Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG) total scores at 24 weeks compared with a 1.5-point improvement with placebo, corresponding to an adjusted improvement of 2.8 points (P=0.0015).

The MG-ADL is a patient-reported measure of disease severity and functional impairment, with scores ranging from 0 to 24. The QMG is a physician-administered evaluation of muscle function, with scores ranging from 0 to 39.

In the NIMBLE trial, adverse events occurred in 69% of participants in the cemdisiran group and 77% in the placebo group. The most common adverse event in the cemdisiran group was upper respiratory tract infection (12%), which occurred at a similar rate in the placebo group (11%).

Serious adverse events were less frequent in the cemdisiran group (3%) than in the placebo group (14%). No serious or meningococcal infections were reported in the cemdisiran group.

No deaths were reported during the double-blind treatment period. Two deaths occurred afterward: one, due to pneumonia, was considered treatment-related by the investigator but not by the sponsor.

Cemdisiran is administered subcutaneously four times a year and is available only through a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) program, Regeneron said.

The drug carries a boxed warning for serious and life-threatening infections caused by Neisseria meningitidis. Besides gefurulimab, three other C5 inhibitors have been approved by the FDA for myasthenia gravis: eculizumab (Soliris), ravulizumab (Ultomiris), and zilucoplan (Zilbrysq). All carry a similar warning, and patients are required to comply with recommendations for meningococcal vaccinations.

People with generalized myasthenia gravis "experience profound muscle weakness that can affect how they talk, see, breathe and move, and with it their ability to carry out everyday activities," said Samantha Masterson, president and CEO of the Myasthenia Gravis Foundation of America. "This approval is a positive milestone for the [generalized myasthenia gravis] community, reflecting continued progress on outcomes that matter most to patients."

#5828. Doctor's Mass Shooting Stuns Medical Community

— Colleagues never perceived Matt Lambert as a danger to himself or others

Doctors who knew Matt Lambert, MD, the emergency physician at the center of last week's mass shooting in Washington, D.C., were shocked by what happened, and said they never expected him to do something so dire.

Those who had interacted with him described a collegial, friendly person who showed no signs of any distress or any mental health conditions. Physicians spoke with MedPage Today about Lambert on the condition of anonymity.

One said Lambert spoke about problems with his condo unit, but it was "no different than if I said I was frustrated with a building problem. There was never any dark humor or anything that seemed off."

Nor did Lambert ever talk about guns, they said -- despite the fact he had at least three of them in his apartment the day of the shootings. On Nov. 12, he killed the building's property manager, then shot and wounded four other people before killing himself.

Joel Braithwaite, an attorney who represented Lambert, said at a press conference that he had spoken with Lambert not long before the shooting and said he was in "great spirits. ... I'm at a loss for how things took any sort of turn that would have us here today."

Braithwaite described Lambert as a "humanitarian" who helped people with scholarships for medical school. "He was also a great thinker, an author, someone who's very calm and collected."

Michael Brice-Saddler, a reporter for the Washington Sun, spoke with Lambert about a year ago about his ongoing issues with his condo unit. In a video posted with his report, Brice-Saddler said there were "no indications to me that he was dangerous or capable of violence."

"I went back and visited him multiple times," he said. "If I thought my safety was in question, I would have pulled the plug."

Last December, Lambert said this to Brice-Saddler about his ongoing condo unit fight: "It's affected every part of my life -- my sleep, dietary habits. I pride myself on my resilience, and I've been kind of surprised at how much it has been affected."

Brice-Saddler wrote that that Lambert did sometimes use "intense, combative language -- and at one point, his frustration veered into what at the time felt like dark humor." He made a reference to Luigi Mangione, the suspect in the 2024 shooting death of UnitedHealthcare's CEO who is accused of inscribing "delay, deny, depose" on his ammunition. Lambert said those words described the condo association's legal strategy, according to Brice-Saddler's report.

Lambert also posted something similar last month on the X account he created for his unit: "After months of 'Luigi' tactics (delay, deny, defend) by the HOA, we were finally able to submit an independent engineering report into the record," it stated in part.

Brice-Saddler uncovered something else about Lambert in his reporting -- the sad fact that he was raised in Appalachia by a single mother who died of cancer during his first year of medical school at Marshall University in Huntington, West Virginia.

Physicians who knew Lambert told MedPage Today it was unclear if he had a partner.

It's well established that physicians with mental health conditions don't often seek help, which is particularly concerning given that doctors experience elevated rates of burnout, depression, anxiety, and suicide compared with the general population, according to a special communication published last year in JAMA.

Jesse Ehrenfeld, MD, MPH, a past president of the American Medical Association and a co-author of that communication, said that much work stands to be done on reducing barriers to mental healthcare for clinicians. That includes expanding confidential care options and removing intrusive questions on licensure questionnaires.

"We need to make that a best practice everywhere instead of just in a handful of states," Ehrenfeld told MedPage Today.

N. Adam Brown, MD, MBA, an emergency physician and healthcare executive who writes about clinician mental health, noted that "having letters behind our names as physicians does not grant us any special privilege of not having an underlying mental health condition," and that the U.S. needs to "take very seriously the health and wellness of clinicians."

"We also need to recognize the role that gun violence plays in our society," Brown told MedPage Today. "We need to take a look at both sides of the coin -- the mental health aspect, as well as the accessibility of guns -- to prevent acts like this in the future."

#5827. If You Want GTA 6's Analog For A Pontiac Trans Am With The Screaming Chicken, Do This

If you're just reading this, you were likely not one of the 43 enlisted soldiers taking advantage of a four-day leave to play "Grand Theft Auto 6," and instead worked a full day at your job and/or took care of other real-life responsibilities. Being late to the party, though, means there's more insight on the internet for your playthrough, telling you to make sure you fill your gas tank, or remember that Flock-like cameras will track you during your crime sprees. In this case, we found a quicker and easier way to take on one of the first car theft missions in the game.

The mission, called "Unwanted Attention," is to steal a beloved red Phoenix from a police impound lot to acquire some misplaced "cargo," i.e. the "like 20 pounds or something" of weed that Cal tells you is hidden somewhere in the car. According to Polygon, this can be done one of two ways. The first — which is not recommended — is to make your presence very known with weapons and a show. The other, much subtler method is acquisition by stealth.

Now, the version Polygon points you toward is doable, but looks to be pretty involved. Its version has you using special machines to get onto the roof and sneaking into an office where you might have to take down a mechanic. However, a favorite YouTuber of mine found a way that's even more chill, with far fewer steps.

The fun begins when you pull up to the impound lot in Hamlet. First, you'll want to ensure that Jason doesn't have any weapons on his person. Meanwhile, in gameplay, Cal sends a text: "Help her rise from the flames (fire emoji x3)." Polygon's guide tells you your best bet from here is to not hop the impound lot fence, but if no one is around, you can go ahead and scramble over it, like Jacksepticeye did. From here, casually walk through the little lot to the far back right corner, where you'll see some shipment boxes stacked together in a convenient way that leads up to a storage container on the other side of the fence. Climb up and enjoy a view for a beat.

It's not easy to spot upon first glance, but you'll essentially want to look for the white tow truck under the awning tucked toward the farthest wall from where you are standing. The elusive Phoenix is attached behind it. Carefully climb down from the container and walk casually toward the right of the tow truck. You'll want to be careful to avoid being seen or detected by the cop on the left side of the lot.

Just to be safe, you can use your Waink car scanner on your phone to verify the car attached to the truck is indeed the Phoenix. It'll tell you it's a 1977 Imponte Phoenix (or, in real life, a Pontiac Firebird Trans Am, recognizable thanks to the large gold "Screaming Chicken" on the hood). You can easily unhook the Phoenix from the truck.

Now all you have to do is get in the car and hot-wire it — the game will tell you how to do so. Take the car toward the impound lot gate (where you came in) to open it, which will take a painstaking minute, especially thanks to all of the attention you have now drawn trying to drive the car out of the lot. Once it's open, get going! Keep an eye out, however, with your newly acquired flock of enthusiastic followers: the police.

GTA gamers will know you just have to keep on driving to lose these guys. Once you do that, the game will find ways to let you know to take the car to the Hamlet Pay 'N' Spray shop, where you can fix your likely very dented car as you get acclimated to the game. You'll also want to give it a little makeover  so as to avoid drawing any additional attention. Then it's time to deliver the finished product to Brian's garage where you'll collect a $150 cash reward, likely to fund your own makeover, as you'll also need a way to be less identifiable to outsmart the cops. With that all done, your mission is finally complete.

#5826. Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency

Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Earlier this year, Optum Rx teased the rollout of new pharmacy tools designed to address medication cost and access.

Now, the company is offering a look at how those tools work.

The pharmacy benefit manager said Tuesday that the tools build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

The first platform, called Shop MyScript, allows members to compare prices for their medications as well as their options for pharmacies or delivery before the prescription is dispensed. The tool will notify members of potential cost savings shortly after a script is written.

In addition, Shop MyScript embeds in the prescribing workflow, Optum said, so that the member does not have to make comparisons after the fact. If they choose to keep the existing prescription, it will then be sent to the pharmacy.

Optum said that early adopters have averaged $28 in savings through the program.

"People should have clear information about their medication costs and options when they can actually use it, not after a decision has already been made," said Katte Carey, president of Optum Rx, in the announcement. "These tools give people more information earlier in their journey, so they can make choices that work for them and get the support they need along the way."

In addition, the pharmacy benefit manager announced Optum Assistant, a conversational AI guide that helps members complete routine tasks and track down key information. The tool can triage concerns on a range of topics, from questions about medication and benefits to pharmacy experience challenges.

If the AI guide can't address the individual's needs, they can choose to be connected to live support to find a solution. Optum said about 80% of conversations using the tool led to a resolution without needing a live advocate.

The final new launch aims to support cash-pay options, according to the announcement.

Optum Price Wise will offer members a complete cost breakdown, including the price of a drug and any fees. That total, all-in price allows members to fully understand what their options may be and make the choice that works best for them at the pharmacy counter.

The PBM said new tools like these build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

#5825. AGCO Celebrates Construction Milestone at Olmedo Solar Project in Spain

OLMEDO, Spain, Nov. 19, 2026 — AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) today announced that Project Olmedo, the solar installation at the center of its first Virtual Power Purchase Agreement (VPPA), has completed construction and commissioning and is expected to begin delivering renewable electricity to the Spanish power grid in the coming months, pending final regulatory approvals. BRUC, one of the largest renewable energy groups in Spain, built the new solar installation in the northwest province of Valladolid.

The milestone follows AGCO’s September 2025 announcement of its 10-year VPPA with BRUC. The installation’s two solar projects, Los Silos and El Granero, span approximately 300 hectares (741 acres) and hold approximately 150,000 solar panels. AGCO’s portion of the installation’s annual production is expected to be approximately 150 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of renewable electricity a year, equivalent to the annual electricity consumption of approximately 40,000 European households, based on Eurostat data.

“Project Olmedo will give AGCO long-term price certainty for the majority of the electricity certificate cost in Europe,” said Tim Millwood, Senior Vice President, Chief Supply Chain Officer. “In volatile energy markets, securing that stability for 10 years strengthens the resilience of our supply chain while supporting new renewable generation.”

A VPPA lets a company support new renewable energy projects without taking direct delivery of the electricity they generate. Because AGCO’s agreement supported the construction of a brand-new installation, it will add new renewable capacity to the grid rather than drawing on existing sources, a principle known as additionality. The agreement also helps reduce AGCO’s Scope 2 greenhouse gas emissions from purchased electricity in its Europe/Middle East (EME) region and supports its commitment to reduce absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 55% by 2033 from a 2022 baseline.

“Additionality is what makes this agreement truly meaningful,” said Roger Batkin, Senior Vice President, Corporate Affairs, General Counsel & Corporate Secretary. “We did not simply buy renewable electricity that already existed; we supported the construction of a brand-new renewable solar project. Accomplishing this project required over two years of involvement from many teams, functions, and areas of expertise across AGCO, along with technical advisory support from Schneider Electric and a strong partnership with BRUC.”

Reflecting AGCO’s Farmer-First strategy, Project Olmedo supports local farmers to further diversify their land use by leasing land for the project while maintaining the option for livestock to graze among the panels, maintaining the vegetation in place of machinery.

AGCO and BRUC leaders, joined by local community representatives José Emilio Escudero García, Mayor of Bocigas, and Ursino Vara Pérez, Mayor of Almenara de Adaja, marked the milestone with a ribbon-cutting ceremony at the site in November. Additional drone footage from Project Olmedo and the ribbon-cutting event are available on AGCO’s YouTube page.

For more details on AGCO’s sustainability initiatives, visit the company’s sustainability webpage and read the 2025 Sustainability Report. For more information about BRUC, visit the company’s website at brucenergy.com.

AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) is a global leader in agricultural machinery and precision agriculture technologies. Driven by a Farmer-First strategy, AGCO delivers value through its differentiated leading brands, Fendt™, Massey Ferguson™, PTx™ and Valtra™.  AGCO’s high-performance equipment and smart farming solutions, including brand-agnostic retrofit technologies and autonomous offerings, empower farmers to drive productivity while sustainably feeding the world. For more information, visit www.agcocorp.com.
