#5824. Medicare Shared Savings Program saved $2.5B last year: CMS

Medicare Shared Savings Program saved $2.5B last year: CMS" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Medicare Shared Savings Program has reached the highest savings number in its history, according to new federal data.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Tuesday that the program generated more than $2.5 billion in net savings for Medicare in 2025, making for the ninth year in a row that it led to cost savings. The agency added that every accountable care organization met or exceeded quality reporting requirements.

ACOs participating in MSSP earned $4.9 billion in shared savings payments during 2025, CMS said, with 80% of organizations earning performance payments.

As of 2026, 511 ACOs representing more than 700,000 providers are participating in the Medicare Shared Savings Program, which represents 12.6 million people enrolled in traditional Medicare, another record.

"Patients want doctors who work together, care that puts their needs first, and fewer unnecessary hurdles—that is what accountable care is designed to deliver," said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. "These record savings show the power of rewarding providers for quality and coordination. We want more people with Medicare, and more of the providers who care for them, to benefit from this approach."

CMS said that it wants to make it easier for providers to participate in MSSP if they choose, especially given the program's results to date. It included changes in the latest Physician Fee Schedule to encourage participation, including increased opportunity for certain ACOs to earn shared savings and new financial incentives to draw in first-time participants.

In addition, CMS said that would create more predictable spending targets that make planning and participation straightforward. The agency is also aiming to streamline patient notices and simplify required technology.

“We are excited to see continued progress on both savings and enrollment for Medicare beneficiaries. Beneficiaries are best served when a provider is watching out for them and coordinating their care,” said Center for Medicare Director John Brooks in the announcement.

Emily Brower, CEO of the National Association of ACOs, said in a statement that the 2025 results "build on years of progress and underscore what the accountable care community can accomplish through collaboration, innovation and the widespread adoption of proven best practices."

"NAACOS looks forward to partnering with CMS to build on this momentum," said Brower. "Policies that unleash innovation, reduce burden, and create sustainability through fair and predictable budgets will allow ACOs to deliver even greater value for patients and the Medicare program."

#5823. Prostate Cancer Screening Is Changing. Health Systems Must Catch Up.

— Better preventive tools should be accompanied by accessible and affordable follow-up

For years, the medical community has been divided on prostate cancer screening. Urology organizations have continued to recommend prostate-specific antigen (PSA) testing, while other societies and groups moved to emphasize shared decision-making and individual choice, after careful consideration of potential risks and benefits.

The American Cancer Society's (ACS) new recommendations incorporate updated data and make a clearer suggestion in favor of screening. Longer follow-up research has confirmed that screening prevents prostate cancer deaths, and advances in diagnosis and treatment reduce complications.

To support that recommendation, we must make the safer approach available wherever men receive care.

ACS now recommends a PSA blood test every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 through 69, and annually beginning at 45 for Black men and men with a strong family history. Decisions at 70 through 74 should be individualized. Informed patient agreement remains essential, and screening recommendations generally stop at 75 or when life expectancy is under 10 years.

The strongest evidence for this change comes from the European randomized screening trial. After 23 years, men invited to repeated PSA screening had a 13% lower risk of dying from prostate cancer. The absolute reduction was 0.22 percentage points: approximately two fewer deaths per 1,000 men invited. One death was prevented for every 456 men invited to screening. That benefit is meaningful, but modest.

Screening also finds cancers that would never threaten a man's life. Reasonable experts weigh those trade-offs differently: the U.K. National Screening Committee recently declined to recommend population screening. Recommendations for earlier screening in Black men also rely substantially on modeling because trials inadequately represent them. Supporting the new recommendations requires that we acknowledge these uncertainties.

What strengthens the case for screening today is our growing ability to separate an elevated PSA from a mandatory biopsy, and a cancer diagnosis from an automatic, potentially debilitating treatment.

An elevated PSA should first be confirmed before further evaluation. MRI can then help determine who needs a biopsy. In the Göteborg-2 randomized trial, an MRI-directed approach reduced detection of clinically insignificant cancers by 57% compared with routine systematic biopsy. That finding supports a more selective diagnostic process, although it does not yet prove equivalent long-term survival.

Biopsy itself can be made safer. Transperineal biopsy reaches the prostate through the skin rather than the rectum (e.g. transrectal biopsy, which is the historically more common approach). In the PREVENT randomized trial, infections occurred in none of the men receiving this approach without preventive antibiotics, compared with 1.6% undergoing transrectal biopsy with targeted antibiotics. Cancer detection was similar.

For appropriately selected low-risk cancers, active surveillance can spare or postpone treatment. In the prospective Canary PASS study, estimated prostate cancer mortality at 10 years was 0.1%, and metastasis occurred in 1.4%. About half had received treatment by then. Surveillance means scheduled reassessment and intervention when warranted; its safety depends on continued and adequate follow-up.

Treatment is improving too. In the randomized MIRAGE trial, MRI-guided radiation with smaller treatment margins reduced cumulative moderate-or-worse urinary toxicity over 2 years from 51% to 27%, compared with CT-guided treatment. Bowel toxicity also fell. These results support precision in radiation delivery, while longer follow-up remains important.

In surgery for radical prostatectomy, the NeuroSAFE PROOF trial used rapid examination of tissue during the operation to guide nerve preservation. Among men with good erectile function beforehand, the approach improved erectile-function scores 1 year later and early continence recovery. Neither technique eliminates treatment harms, but both show that preserving quality of life can be a measurable treatment objective.

Aligning these advances with a safe screening program requires changes in how we deliver and pay for care.

First, insurers should cover the entire recommended evaluation without financial barriers: repeat PSA testing, appropriate MRI or biomarkers, and biopsy when indicated. A study of privately insured men found that median out-of-pocket spending for MRI plus biopsy reached up to $620 in 2020. A screening recommendation loses value when patients cannot afford the steps needed to interpret its result safely. Making this a nationwide requirement may take an updated U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommendation with an A or B grade, explicit protection for follow-up testing, or legislation. The task force's current C grade does not trigger the Affordable Care Act's no-cost preventive coverage requirement.

Second, health systems need reliable referral pathways. Primary care teams should know where to send patients, who tracks abnormal results, and how quickly evaluation should occur. MRI quality and interpretation require oversight. A negative scan cannot become a reason to abandon follow-up when other findings remain concerning. Regional imaging networks, specialist consultation and patient navigation can help extend expertise beyond major cancer centers.

Third, active surveillance must be supported as ongoing care. Patients need understandable explanations, dependable recall systems, and accessible reassessment. Payment should recognize the clinical work involved in monitoring a cancer carefully. Men also should have access to balanced consultations about surgery and radiation, including likely effects on sexual, urinary, and bowel function.

Finally, health systems need to measure whether screening delivers better outcomes. Quality programs should track unresolved abnormal results, biopsy-related infections, surveillance participation, treatment complications, and diagnoses of metastases. Results should be examined by ethnicity, insurance, and geography to identify gaps and guide improvement. Research programs should also expand enrollment of Black men in screening and surveillance studies, so future recommendations rest on evidence that better represents those at greatest risk.

I support the ACS recommendations. We now have stronger evidence that screening saves lives and better tools to reduce its harms. We need to make those tools available to every man we screen, with affordable follow-up, careful decisions about treatment, and support throughout his care.

No matter which version of the game you buy, you start out with Jason's base Vapid Ganado, which isn't in the best condition. To upgrade to the iconic Retro Build, you'll have to drive the Ganado to a location called Coastal Auto Care, found in Key Lento. Just don't forget to fill up on the way, because cars in "Grand Theft Auto 6" don't have infinite fuel. The player is introduced to Coastal Auto Care early on via a mission, and you can find it on your map by filtering for "Mod Shops."

You can upgrade your Ganado during the "Just a Scratch" mission, or you can visit Coastal Auto Care any time after completing the mission to apply the upgrade. Once you've driven into the garage's entrance, you get the car customization screen. This screen offers you a choice of different customization options, like cosmetics, bodywork, performance, and interior. Instead of choosing any of those, select the "Pro Builds" tab, which offers you the choice between full kits and wheel kits. For Ultimate Edition players, the Ganado Retro Build kit is found under "Full Kits," and it's free to apply. Unfortunately, this classic design and slick color palette is specific to Jason's Ganado, so you can't bring in other cars and apply the Retro Build kit to them.

Once you're cruising around in the sickest car in the game, you'll want to practice defensive driving. GTA 6 has an advanced physics engine for more realistic car crashes, and nobody wants to see such a beautiful car smashed to bits in a fender-bender.

If you're the type of person who likes to try out every car possible, you'll have your work cut out for you in "GTA 6." There are over 300 different rides you can find throughout the course of the game. Some, like the Declasse Akane (inspired by the Chevy Sonic) are pretty unexciting, albeit still fun to commit major crimes in. Others are breath-takers that can help under-thrilled enthusiasts live their exotic and classic driving dreams.

Take the '95 Grotti Cheetah, inspired by the Ferrari Testarossa. This is another Ultimate Edition-only vehicle, but boy is it sick. If you figure you'll probably never drive a real Testarossa without renting one in Vegas, driving the Cheetah around is a fun second-best. Another eye-grabbing option is the obnoxious green Canis Kamacho, inspired by the Jeep Crew Chief 715 concept. It's a solid in-game off-roader that's available in the base game.

For players with aspirations that extend beyond the asphalt, the Shitzu Squalo is a special boat available to owners of the Ultimate Edition of the game. It's based on the Christ-Craft Stinger 390x and can be found at the docks of Washington Beach. It has a striking pink and blue gradient color scheme, and despite its name, it's not squalid at all. You'll also get access to biplanes, jets, helicopters, and even airboats as you play the game, allowing you to explore the world in whatever style fits your fancy.

#5820. Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House

Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The White House pulled the nomination for Heidi Overton, M.D., Ph.D., to lead the FDA just hours before the Senate was scheduled to vote on her confirmation.

The move hardly comes as a surprise after Overton’s confirmation hearing before the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee drew contention.

The news was first reported by Axios on Tuesday evening. The White House confirmed the news to Fierce the following morning.

In September, the HELP committee questioned Overton in a hearing marked by tightrope-walking on issues like vaccines, abortions, flavored vapes and whether or not President Donald Trump is capable of being wrong.

Overton conceded multiple times during the hearing that vaccines approved by the FDA, as well as the “abortion pill” mifepristone, are safe and effective. But she stopped short of admitting that Trump has been wrong when dispensing medical information out of line with what scientific evidence shows.

Overton’s nomination faced mounting pushback and uncertainty from senators ahead of a planned HELP Committee vote on Nov. 18. Fierce reached out to Sen. Ed Markey, the veteran Democratic lawmaker from Massachusetts, ahead of the vote, and he told Fierce that he would “vote against her nomination, and I urge my colleagues to do the same.”

“The FDA needs a leader who will stand up for science and for our kids’ safety, not one who stood by while both were undermined,” he added.

Republican Sen. Bill Cassidy of Louisiana, who chairs the Senate health committee, and other Republicans had also raised questions about Overton's nomination.

As support for Overton looked thin, Republicans were discussing Sen. Roger Marshall, a Kansas Republican, as a potential replacement, Semafor reported today. Health Secretary Kennedy reportedly met with Marshall in recent days to discuss filling the role, according to Politico, but the White House told the publication that it is not considering him.

Robert Steinbrook, M.D., health research group director for Public Citizen, commented today urging the HELP committee to reject Overton’s nomination: “The FDA needs a strong commissioner who stands for science and the integrity of the agency,” Steinbrook said. “Overton is the wrong choice for FDA Commissioner and has done nothing to dispel the well-founded reasons to oppose her candidacy.”

Analysts from Citi shared that the White House’s decision to pull the nomination has a negative impact on biopharma, “extending uncertainty around permanent agency leadership.”

“We view the withdrawal as a modest sentiment negative, but not a material near-term change to the regulatory environment facing most drug and biologics sponsors,” the analysts said in the note.

The withdrawal of Overton's nomination leaves a gap at an FDA that has already faced significant upheaval. The White House only shifted its focus to Overton in August, after the agency had spent three months without a permanent leader.

Since former FDA commissioner Marty Makary, M.D., departed the agency in May, Kyle Diamantas, who hails from the agency’s food regulation division, has been at the helm in an interim capacity.

#5819. It's Not 'Car Mechanic Simulator,' But GTA 6 Is Leaning Harder Into Vehicle Repair Than Before

More so than any of its predecessors, "Grand Theft Auto 6" leans heavily into realism. You can't just carry your entire arsenal on you anymore — instead, you must rely on a few guns on your person and a stash in your trunk. Building off that simulated realism, the game now puts a greater emphasis on the exciting world of vehicle maintenance directly into the gameplay. There will be times when you have to get your car repaired, repainted, or refueled. Who knows — maybe "Grand Theft Auto 7" will go one step further by including a mission called "Doing Taxes."

To be fair to "Grand Theft Auto 6," it does remember that it is an action/adventure game and not the next coming of "Car Mechanic Simulator." For instance, stops to get your car fixed are generally pretty brief. That way, you can get right back to running heists and stealing a car that looks a lot like a Pontiac Trans Am out of a police impound lot.

Still, the game does feature four different ways to get your car fixed: Pay 'N' Spray shops, mod shops, gas stations, and a guy named Joey who can be called upon to do repairs. Gas stations are limited in the repairs they can do and are more expensive than other repair shops — there's that realism again. For more extensive repairs, you'll need to stop at one of the other shops or call Joey.

Missions can be pretty rough on your vehicles, especially when you're trying to get away from the cops. Your car can get engine damage or cosmetic damage, and fixing it can be important to the gameplay. One of the early missions, "Just a Scratch," will actually make you take your vehicle into Coastal Auto Care to get fixed. That's where you meet Joey.

Throughout the game, there is an engine condition indicator that goes from green to orange to red. It ends off at red with an "X" in the middle, indicating that the engine is dead. Green indicates the engine is in good shape, orange means there is moderate damage, and red indicates critical damage. An engine that is not in good shape can affect how the car performs, which is not something you want to deal with as your wanted level increases.

And don't forget to fuel up, as each car has a working fuel gauge. But don't worry, because you won't have to stop for gas too often; apparently "Grand Theft Auto 6" cars get pretty great gas mileage. EVs are also a part of the universe, which means you'll have to stop for a charge every so often if you grab one.

The cars in "Grand Theft Auto 6" are not indestructible, but they can keep running even after getting pretty banged up. So, why bother to fix body damage? You don't want the NPCs to point and laugh at your car, do you? Even if you aren't worried about the opinions of Vice City pedestrians, the condition of your car's body can affect gameplay as well. Want to sell that hot car that you just stole? Well, just like in real life, damage to the body of the car can affect how much it sells for.

Then there's the matter of getting away from the cops after stealing a car. They will often have the car's description, including its color. There are even Flock-like cameras in GTA 6 that can track your movements. Repainting it another color, even when the body is in good shape, can help hide it from the police. It's like a wardrobe change, but for your vehicle. Pay 'N' Spray is the place to go for a quick paint job, while mod shops will be able to make more extensive changes to your car.

Overall, it seems like Rockstar has found a nice balance between the realities of car ownership and the escapist fantasy that the Grand Theft Auto series has always embodied. We'll just have to wait and see if "Car Mechanic Simulator 2027" will be adding heists.

#5818. The EV Takeover Is Taking Shape, Despite Heroic Efforts To Stop It

Whelp, the AI boom has had one positive knock-on effect. It is creating new multi-millionaires by the bushel, any one of whom can afford to splurge on a pricey new electric vehicle. That aside, EV stakeholders in the US are springing back to life after a year-long slumber. Even General Motors is in play, stirring up long lost memories of its 1990’s-era EV1 debacle instead of hiding its head in shame. What’s up with that?

I’m just going to stick my neck out and say that the eponymous Aptera solar-powered autocycle from the US startup Aptera will attract enough AI-enriched drivers to make a go of it. The on-again, off-again, on-again automaker has been on this year, working towards volume production of a plug-in, three-wheeled two-seater EV that can travel up to 40 miles on its own solar-plus-storage system (see more Aptera news here).

Autocycles were once common here in the US and overseas markets, distinguished from motorcycles by their front-facing seats and steering wheels, rather than straddled seats and handlebars. They fell out of favor in the US by the middle of the 20th century, but 21st century hot-rodders have revived the form with a focus on eyebrow-raising, open-air, gas-guzzling showpieces. In particular, interest has spread in states that do not require autocycle drivers to invest in an additional motorcycle license.

The Aptera is showy in its own right, resembling a rocket ship with unique aerodynamic styling. Perhaps it may even show up in the next James Bond adventure with James Pattinson at the wheel. In the meantime, the company has just made good on its pledge to reward early investors in the world’s first solar-powered car with the first production models to role off the assembly line. Aptera #1 goes to the founder and CEO of GDP Venture, Martin Hartono, aka “Accelerator #1.”

Drivers can reserve an Aptera of their own for just $100, and they can expect to pay about $40,000 for the ride. Considering that the solar fuel is free, anyone in the mood for a head-turning ride will get quite a bargain for their $40,000 compared to other competitors in the zero emission two-seater field, such as the recently refreshed Tesla Roadster.

Speaking of refresh, General Motors has been weirdly lavishing attention on the EV1 sedan all this year, even though the automaker has been roundly criticized for the killing the short-lived car in its cradle. The automaker began producing EV1 electric sedans starting in 1996, following the unveiling of its Impact electric concept car in 1990. Unlike the Impact, EV1 was designed for use on the road. However, it was also designed mainly for the California market, and primarily for lease, not for sale. Production halted in 2003 at just about 1,000 vehicles, all to be recalled by GM and sent to the crusher as chronicled in Chris Paine’s 2006 documentary, “Who Killed The Electric Car?”

Or, maybe not so weird after all. GM’s electrification journey long predates the launch of Tesla Motors in 2003. The legacy automaker is now determined to stake out its claim on automotive history, with 2026 marking the 30th anniversary of the start of EV1 production.

GM has been celebrating the anniversary all this year with multiple long form articles about the Impact and EV1 on its website, eager to remind everybody that its industry-leading vehicle electrification efforts of the 1990’s continue to manifest decades later, in the form of heat pumps for climate control, by-wire technology, and regenerative braking among other elements.

GM’s latest love letter to the EV1 popped up on November 17, when the automaker officially celebrated the completed restoration of the EV1 “V212” alongside its a private owner, Billy Caruso. The rare survivor of GM’s the junkyard crusher was evidently hiding out in an impound lot in Georgia all these years, until Caruso acquired it at auction and enlisted Jared Pink of the popular show The Questionable Garage to fix it up. Considering TQG’s large following on YouTube, GM wisely lent its engineers to the effort, along with several donated parts from its own EV1.

US President Donald Trump and the Republican majority in Congress have made a heroic effort to dial down the vehicle electrification movement, but facts on the ground keep getting in their way. For starters, today’s advanced batteries apply across multiple energy resources, fossil and non-fossil alike. So, it’s no surprise to see the US Department of Energy continuing to throw taxpayer dollars at new energy storage projects (here’s one example), even though some of that activity will eventually ripple into the vehicle electrification field.

The US Department of Defense is also getting into the act. DoD’s electrification journey has been a tedious one, but in recent years it has begun creeping stealthily towards a vision of ground mobility enabled by electricity, providing the key tactical advantages of silent running and a low heat signature.

The latest news on that score involves a $1.4 billion loan commitment from DoD’s Office of Strategic Capital (OSC), enabling the silicon-based battery materials startup Sila to vault its signature product into commercial production. DoD’s primary interest in the company consists of applications in the electric drone and autonomous mobility fields, but Sila has long been eyeballing the EV market as well.

Sila secured the loan commitment in August, and it has not let the grass grow under its feet. On November 18 the company announced an off-take agreement with the well-known Tesla EV battery supplier Panasonic Energy Corporation of North America. “The multi-year agreement is part of a long-term commercial relationship with the potential for over $1 billion of offtake over time,” Sila explained in a press statement, further elaborating that Panasonic will deploy its proprietary Titan Silicon silicon-carbon anode material in battery cells.

“The partnership is also expected to support significant manufacturing and construction job creation in the United States. With Sila’s U.S.-based anode production underpinned by proprietary American technology and know-how, Panasonic and its OEM customers gain a clearer path toward a more secure, resilient, and sustainable domestic supply chain,” Sila emphasized.

That’s…interesting! Aside from Tesla as one of Sila’s OEM customers, keep an eye on another Panasonic offtaker, the up-and-comer US startup Harbinger Motors, which also has a foot in the DoD door.

Photo: The vehicle electrification movement is far from over in the US, with the startup Aptera among the stakeholders betting on an EV sales rebound in the coming years (cropped, courtesy of Aptera).

#5817. HLTH 2026: How Whoop aims to play a bigger role in healthcare

HLTH 2026: How Whoop aims to play a bigger role in healthcare" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

LAS VEGAS—Whoop gained traction as a performance wearable used by elite athletes, professional teams and Olympians to track physiological strain and sleep.

The company has evolved into a broader clinical and digital health platform that layers medical-grade hardware, blood diagnostics, electronic health record (EHR) syncing, and physician care on top of its continuous biometric tracking. The company's FDA-cleared ECG detects signs of atrial fibrillation and allows members to share results with their healthcare provider.

Whoop is a screenless, wrist-worn device that measures strain, recovery and sleep 24/7, paired with Whoop Coach, an AI-powered, in-app health and fitness assistant. The company expanded its Advanced Labs testing—combining Quest blood testing, clinician reviews and AI insights—to non-members, alongside integrating Grail's multi-cancer early detection test. Whoop partners with health platform HealthEx to let users connect their medical records directly to the Whoop app, combining medical history with biometric data. A feature rolled out earlier this year lets Whoop members directly connect with clinicians through the app, including live, on-demand video consultations.

The company has raised over $900 million to date, including a $575 million series G round in March, pushing its valuation to $10 billion. Whoop told Bloomberg it plans to hire 600 people this year, a roughly 75% boost to its headcount, as it works to further integrate AI into its health and wellness monitoring tools and to expand internationally. The company is on track for an initial public offering in 18 months, executives told Bloomberg in August.

The company is positioning itself as a continuous health monitor that goes beyond fitness tracking. The device's 24/7 physiological data can function almost like an ongoing health check, becoming a bridge between everyday health and medical care, Will Ahmed, founder and CEO of Whoop, said during an on-stage interview at HLTH on Tuesday.

Ahmed argued that the conventional annual medical physical is "frozen in time."

"The reality is wearing a continuous health monitor like Whoop that should be a 24/7 annual checkup. That's how it should function from a data standpoint," he said during a fireside chat with Michael Phelps, U.S. former competitive swimmer, who is the most decorated Olympian in history with 23 gold medals, and also a Whoop customer since 2015.

"Then there will be alerts when something is really wrong, and particularly as we combine these different data sources together, again, 24/7 physiological data, blood tests, medical records, how you're feeling and contextual cues. When we have all this information in one place, and now we've got 3.5 million members, about to be 4 million members, it's just amazing the insights that you can pull together from that. And so you can start to predict that someone may need to get a cancer screening, or may actually have a real heart problem," he said.

Whoop can pull together biometric data, labs, medical records and information to establish a baseline for an individual, recognize meaningful changes and recommend an action. By combining continuous monitoring with information such as blood tests, the company hopes to help users piece together health signals and determine when they may need care, Ahmed said.

For Phelps, Whoop has shifted from being about maximizing elite athletic performance to helping him manage his health, energy and recovery around everyday life and longevity, he told the audience.

"For me, for 20-plus years, I've been trying to chase time, and now I'm just trying to be the healthiest person that I can be," Phelps said. "For me, the data that I get every single day, if my body isn't 100% or I'm not full go, then I take a step back and I tone down my workout. When I'm talking to my trainer, I'm taking it down a little bit, so I have enough energy and I have what I need to be a good dad, to be a good husband, to be a good worker, hard worker, whatever it is, just to be a good person."

Whoop sees opportunities to turn deviations from an individual's baseline into actionable signals. During the COVID-19 pandemic, Phelps said his Whoop device signaled that his temperature, respiratory rate and HRV changed, but he noted that the real benefit was that Whoop knew what normal looked like for him.

Using biometric data to recognize signals in a person's health can have broader implications, Ahmed noted, citing examples where members identified potentially serious health problems after noticing significant changes in their physiological data.

"We're just starting to see all of these different maps presenting themselves, and it's very empowering. We're excited about the role that we can play with the broader health ecosystem," Ahmed said.

Ahmed said Whoop's high user engagement and continuous monitoring can fill gaps between doctors' visits and provide clinically relevant information to care teams.

"I do believe that technology like Whoop can make the whole system much more efficient and also make a doctor's life easier if we do our jobs properly, which is again to say, how do you take all this information that's being collected about a patient or a consumer and how do you simplify it to the one, two, or three questions that a doctor might actually want to know the answer to before you show up to their office?" he said.

Ahmed sees the AI coach evolving into a personal health companion sitting on top of a longitudinal health record.

"That's another area that we're really excited about, where this idea that you can have a conversation with Whoop or what feels like this entity that sits on top of your data, and all of a sudden you feel like you have a health companion, and yes, you've got 24/7 physiological data from sensors, but you can also now have your medical records. You can also now have your blood tests. There are all sorts of conversational cues. 'How are you feeling today?' 'Did you drink alcohol last night?' Were you traveling?' You put all that information together, all that data in one place, and the insights are quite profound," he said.

Partnerships with other digital health companies and acquisitions help Whoop broaden the platform, he noted.

"We like to think about it from the consumer's vantage point first, which is to say, if you're an individual, if you're a consumer, what is data that you want on your body or you need on your body, and that may be data that's being collected somewhere else, in which case, we feel like there could be an opportunity for a partnership," Ahmed said, adding, "We're also comfortable now acquiring technology and acquiring different innovations, which has become more of a muscle that we've built over the last 12 months."

Whoop believes its biggest strategic advantage is the consumer relationship.

Members open the app roughly eight times a day and wear the device nearly 23 hours a day.

"There are so many of us who have been in health tech where we would die for that kind of engagement, and I think that's something we're always striving for because we're an industry that was never built with the consumer in the center," said Lynne O'Keefe, founder and manager partner of Define Ventures, who moderated the on-stage interview.

The company is strategically expanding into an older, more medical population. Whoop is a participant in the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services' ACCESS model. Ahmed said members 65 and older are actually Whoop's most highly engaged demographic, challenging the assumption that consumer health technology primarily appeals to younger people.

"It's clearly a population that cares a lot about their health. They want to focus on what's going on with their blood pressure, their ECG, or whether they need to send data to their doctors," he said.

Ahmed framed CMS' ACCESS model as a move from consumers simply paying for a wearable toward government reimbursement tied to measurable health outcomes, such as improvements in blood pressure or weight.

"I think as a model of innovation for a government and its citizens, it's pretty interesting because it incentivizes companies to actually create technology or programs that are making people healthier. It should change the cost curve because you're paying if that outcome is delivered, and then to the consumer, you're both hopefully being healthier and not paying for a product that otherwise would have had to," he said.

Whoop also positions itself as a platform that can wrap around drugs and other healthcare interventions. The company announced this week a partnership with Novo Nordisk as part of the pharma company's redesign of its direct-to-consumer NovoCare program. Novo Nordisk relaunched its NovoCare direct-to-consumer program. Patients taking a GLP-1 can integrate wearable data from Whoop as part of their program.

Whoop can function as a potential companion for GLP-1 users, monitoring exercise, sleep, resting heart rate, HRV and other signals while helping users preserve health and fitness during treatment, Ahmed said.

When asked about the future vision for Whoop, he said, "I think in simple terms, it's our responsibility to build technology that either makes you healthier or one day saves your life. You're going to have all of this health data on your body. I think organizing that information in one place, alongside 24/7 wearable data, and alongside a powerful AI that can sit on top of that data and go off on your behalf and take actions and schedule appointments with the doctor, or go speak to your doctor on your behalf, or alert you when something's wrong," he said.

He added, "God forbid this day comes, but you should wake up and be told from Whoop you need to go to the doctor in the next 30 minutes, and it's been scheduled, and you show up, and all of a sudden you find out that you're having a heart attack, or you're having some serious life event. To me, that feels like an inevitable future, and I do predict that a large percentage of humanity in the future will be wearing a continuous health monitor."

#5816. Is AI A Public Or A Private Asset?

Isabelle Ferreras is a professor and research director at the University of Louvain, an Accelerator fellow at the Institute for Ethics in AI at the University of Oxford, and a senior research associate at the Center for Labor and a Just Economy at Harvard University. It safe to say she knows a thing or two about computer technology and AI.

She told The Guardian this week that she has grave concerns about the way AI and the corporations promoting it are managing this brave new technology. Her concerns are spurred by the number of high profile computer engineers who have quit recently over fears that AI development is out of control.

Last month, a person responsible for overseeing safety reports at OpenAI left the company, claiming its culture was “broken.” He added that the entire AI industry was careless when it comes to developing AI technology. The month before that, a researcher at Anthropic — which went public yesterday at a valuation $1.8 trillion dollars — warned that AI “could kill us all by the end of the decade.”

“When the only way for a conscientious engineer to raise the alarm is to leave,” Ferreras said. “it is a clear sign that corporate governance in the industry is broken. Each departure takes with it the very knowledge needed to steer these systems safely. We cannot regulate AI safely from the outside if the companies building it remain autocracies on the inside.”

Ferreras warned that governments think of AI either as a “consumer product to be tested for defects or as a geopolitical race to be won by relaxing rules on business. Corporate lobbyists encourage this view as a trade-off between safety and innovation by arguing that legislation imposes too many burdens.

“This is a dangerous path. AI is not merely a software product. It is a technology of government. Yet inside frontier labs such as OpenAI, Anthropic, Microsoft, and Google, a handful of executives and capital investors decide which values are coded into models that shape millions of lives,” she said.

Interestingly, Michael Connolly, the award winning author of the Bosch and Lincoln Lawyer novels, published a book last year in which the family of a young woman, who was murdered by her boyfriend after he interacted with an AI agent, sues the company behind the AI agent. The essence of the lawsuit is that AI only does what it is programmed to do, and since the lead programmer of this particular AI agent was a misogynist in real life, it reflected his values when it interacted with humans. The book is called The Proving Ground and it is a fascinating read.

While Ferreras applauds those who warn of the malignancies built into AI, she pointed out that humans have short attention spans. “Time and again, a safety researcher or engineer walks out of the door with a public warning that commercial pressures are overriding safety guardrails. As a society, we applaud the courage of these individuals and debate their warnings for a news cycle — and then we move on. But we miss the deeper institutional failure those resignations reveal. As the great political scientist Albert Hirschman made clear, exit is not voice.”

In a democratic society, the exercise of governing power requires the consent of the governed, Ferreras told The Guardian. “Yet corporate law still treats firms as the property of shareholders, and the labor investors, who put their intellect, health and lives into the firm, as subordinates. To align AI with the public interest, we need a dual strategy: alongside solid public regulations on AI ‘from the outside,’ we need to enter the corporate black box and force corporate governance into alignment with democratic principles.”

An analogy can be made to the Federal Communications Commission, which licenses broadcasters and promotes the free flow of information to the public. The digital world thinks such controls are archaic, but the lack of them is a clear and present danger to a civil society.

Ferreras suggested that “governments must outlaw non-disclosure and non-disparagement clauses that silence staff on safety risks, and enact robust whistle blower protection. Tech workers also need a collective standing over corporate strategy, independent channels to report safety breaches, and formal representation inside frontier labs before models are deployed.”

In addition, she argued that “legislators must establish a binding right to co-decision over workplace AI. No algorithmic system that monitors, evaluates or disciplines workers should be deployed without prior negotiation. At national and EU level, we need to consider collective bargaining on AI deployment and veto rights for works councils or trade unions, backed by independent technical experts and a strict ban on automated firing.”

Lastly, she supported the democratization of boardrooms and rewarding firms that refuse to concentrate power at the top. “Legislators should mandate worker-elected directors on boards, moving towards dual-majority governance in which strategic decisions require the consent not only of capital but of labor.” Some, like Elon Musk and Mark Zuckerberg, would characterize her suggestions as liberal claptrap, and they would be correct. Ferreras would happily unravel the “tech bro” manosphere in order to promote a more open and just society.

She pointed to the research by Daron Acemoglu and Simon Johnson, the 2024 Nobel laureates in economics, which demonstrates that technological revolutions do not automatically produce shared prosperity. Instead, institutions decide who benefits and who pays.

“Extending democracy into the firm is not an old battle over power for its own sake, but a practical necessity for more ethical judgment. Aligning AI with the good of workers and society requires more wisdom than closed executive rooms can offer. It needs what the Yale political theorist Hélène Landemore calls ‘democratic reason’ — the collective intelligence of diverse, deliberating voices, brought to bear on corporate decision-making,” Ferreras wrote.

“Left to self-regulation and the occasional whistleblower, AI governance will entrench an unaccountable algorithmic oligarchy. Accountability and alignment begins with giving those who build these systems and those whose work they govern an enforceable voice, from the code floor to the boardroom.”

Ferreras’ point of view is decidedly liberal, and yet her arguments are clear and concise. How can we give so much power to digital agents and not take reasonable steps to ensure the needs of society as a whole are respected? The tech industry enjoys almost unfettered freedom of action today. It may be time to consider whether that is a good long term strategy.

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. #5815. At $4,000, Is This 2002 Honda CR-V EX AWD A Keeper?

A lower-mileage engine and rare five-speed manual make the 241,000 miles on today's Nice Price or No Dice CR-V less of a concern. Let's see if this utilitarian compact crossover's price proves a hurdle we can't get past.

One of the coolest parts of the Mission Impossible franchise—both the '60s TV series and more recent movies—is the conceit where an agent masks up to imitate a target. The chef's kiss is seeing the secret agent reach under their collar to yank off the duplicative mask, thus revealing their true identity—typically Tom Cruise.

We had a similar experience on Thursday with the 1987 Fiero-based Corson, which was trying—somewhat successfully from a few angles, some might argue—to look like a Ferrari 512 BBi. At $26,000, there was no fooling our jaded eyes, however, causing the Corson Fiero to go up in flames in a massive 90% 'No Dice' loss.

Because yesterday's candidate was excessive in its owner's aspirations and the fakery it would take to make those aspirations a reality, it might be a nice contrast today to go with a candidate that is more sensible and grounded. That's not to say that this 2002 Honda CR-V EX AWD is dull as dishwater. How could it be with all those letters in its name? The CR-V has been Honda's best-selling vehicle here in the U.S. since 2017, and according to the company's press site, "America's CUV best-selling since its U.S. launch in 1997." By the way, that's "Crossover Utility Vehicle" to you and me.

Loosely based on the Civic's platform and mechanicals, the first-generation CR-V offered a tall wagon experience in a manageable size and with reasonable fuel economy. That first generation also had an externally mounted spare tire and could have originally come with a clever, pull-out folding-leg table in the load area.

Okay, so far, that, save for the spare and the table, could describe just about any of the CR-V's competitors from when it was new or even clogging showrooms today. So what makes this particular car interesting?

This 2002 edition was originally spec'd with a five-speed manual transmission and Honda's "Real Time" AWD behind its 2.4-liter, 160 horsepower VTEC four. That's a very rare combo on this car. According to the ad, that drivetrain has racked up a notable 241,000 miles under this CR-V's tires.

Not everything has made it that far, as the ad also touts an engine replacement in 2024 with a 60K unit and new gearbox synchros installed at some point. This year, it got a new catalytic converter, so smogging the car shouldn't be an issue. No mention is made about the clutch, but who does an engine swap or gearbox work without replacing the clutch? Reassuringly, the seller does claim that "everything works great," including the A/C, which they say blows cold.

Despite the miles and age, this CR-V still looks to have plenty of fight left in it. That's not to say it doesn't have a couple of battle scars. It does, after all, live in Los Angeles. Honda was very generous with the black plastic moldings on these first-gen cars, and they tend to turn gray and develop swirlies over time. A good plastic protector helps a lot, and this car seems to have been wiped down before its photo shoot. Missing on the exterior are the chrome inlay for the Honda badge in the grille and a chunk of the plastic fender arch over the left-rear wheel. Taking its place, and calling even more attention to its absence, is a sizable dent just behind the fuel filler door. Other than that, and some minor yellowing of the headlamp covers, the exterior looks to be in solid shape.

The cabin tells a similar story, and it happily features patterned mouse-fur upholstery rather than less durable leather. Seat controls are manual, but offer a number of knobs and levers to help occupants get comfortable. The rear bench includes a center fold-out armrest with similar intent. Sadly, the pull-out table appears to have gone missing, or was never there in the first place.

In the film "Invasion of the Body Snatchers" (any of them, take your pick), the heroes fight an overwhelming effort by the titular invaders to assimilate them into a hive-mind collective. I'm not suggesting any of us get sucked into the CUV or Crossover cult, but if we were, this CR-V might prove a reasonable point of entry, given its specs. The question is: would it be worth $4,000 to do so?

Los Angeles, California, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5813. The Metals Company Has a Seabed Mining Production Gap That Its Pilot Has Not Closed

In 2022, The Metals Company (TMC) and offshore contractor Allseas recovered 3,021 wet tons of nodules from the Pacific seabed. Their integrated test system moved material up a riser stretching more than four kilometres from the ocean floor to the Hidden Gem. During production runs, the collector achieved 86.4 wet tons per hour. Four years later, TMC is discussing a million tons a year from an upgraded system and eventually 12 million tons annually across four vessels.

What matters between those endpoints is the operating record: sustained collection rates, marine maintenance, vessel availability and delivered tons sufficient to carry fixed costs. TMC has yet to demonstrate those economics.

The November 12, 2026 corporate update describes a phased approach: begin with an upgraded pilot collector and limited modifications to Hidden Gem, then invest in larger systems as operations develop. Phasing reduces the initial capital commitment. It also pushes the demonstration of the projected commercial production rates into the future.

The annual arithmetic is straightforward. At 5,000 productive hours, about 57% of a calendar year, a system collecting 200 wet tons per hour delivers one million tons. The second-year target of 1.5 million tons requires 300 tons per hour. Three million tons from one production system requires 600 tons per hour. TMC’s 2022 production-run rate of 86.4 tons per hour would deliver 432,000 tons at that utilization.

The upgraded machines are expected to be different from the 2022 test collector. Their performance must be demonstrated, not assumed from the pilot. The operating gap is central to the project economics, and it grows with production ambitions.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines TMC’s production gap alongside Impossible Metals’ autonomous collection model and the investment consequences of underperforming equipment. The source assessment and fleet sensitivities are linked there.

A collector, a multi-kilometre hydraulic riser and a production vessel have to work together. The crawler encounters uneven ground and varying nodule coverage. The riser and pumps handle abrasive slurry. The vessel must remain on station while crews inspect, maintain and repair equipment. Offshore access and weather affect when those activities can occur.

A system can produce at its test rate and still lose the year on maintenance and interruptions. Fewer productive hours push the required hourly rate higher. Repeating a successful short production run does not establish a year’s availability, let alone multiple years of fleet productivity.

The difference matters because capital and offshore operating costs continue while production is interrupted. Ships, crews, marine maintenance equipment and financing cost money whether or not nodules are arriving on deck. Smaller annual production spreads fixed expenses over fewer tons and raises the cost of every ton recovered.

TMC’s August 2025 prefeasibility presentation estimated offshore collection and transport at $179 per dry ton, or about $129 per wet ton under its 28% moisture assumption. Those are projections for a future system. They are not an audited cost record from continuous industrial mining. The revised first-production configuration needs its own cost data.

The missing commercial evidence is a long enough operating record to estimate productive hours, rate degradation, planned and unplanned maintenance, retrieval time, replacement costs and the delivered cost per ton. Until then, moving from a pilot to a production forecast remains a major assumption.

Impossible Metals proposes to collect individual nodules with autonomous underwater vehicles rather than a tracked crawler and riser. A vehicle would descend, select nodules, fill its cargo compartment, return, undergo surface recovery, unload and prepare for another mission. Every trip must be repeated across an entire fleet.

Its September 2026 scientific trial, reported by Reuters, recovered approximately 450 grams of nodules from more than 4,000 metres depth. It demonstrated selective scientific collection. A commercial robot would have to carry vastly more material and sustain cycle times and availability that the experiment did not measure.

In the 2025 commercial concept examined by metallurgical specialist Lyle Trytten and me, each proposed vehicle carried 12 tons of nodules and had 18 collection arms. At one nodule every 1.5 seconds per arm and an average nodule mass of 100 grams, filling the cargo requires roughly 120,000 successful picks. With all arms continuously working, collection alone occupies approximately 2.8 hours.

The proposed complete mission cycle was four hours. Descent, ascent, searching, repositioning, failed picks, recovery, unloading, inspection and relaunch also consume time. The architecture has too many interdependent activities to fit those requirements into that cycle at the assumed performance.

Autonomous navigation and manipulator tests cannot substitute for evidence of an entire fleet repeatedly executing commercial missions. Increasing payload changes vehicle weight, buoyancy, energy use and recovery equipment. Faster picking leaves the surface operation and deepwater transit to be solved.

Our 2025 independent techno-economic assessment tested the financial effects of weaker-than-assumed robot productivity. At the illustrative $180 per dry ton nodule sales value, the model required approximately $2.52 billion in capital with the originally assumed robot throughput. Halving throughput while maintaining output increased required capital to approximately $4.51 billion.

At a 10% discount rate, the modeled net present value moved from positive $863 million to negative $769 million. Even at two-thirds of original productivity, NPV fell to about $15 million, a negligible margin for a multibillion-dollar first-of-a-kind industrial fleet.

These are scenario calculations, not actual bills or calibrated probabilities. They identify the key failure mode: more robots, recovery capacity, batteries, vessels and maintenance are needed to compensate for each production shortfall. A financial model based on high productivity can look attractive until realistic availability and turnaround times are included.

TMC is further along technologically than Impossible Metals, but its future-scale production economics are also unsupported by a sustained operating record. Neither company has demonstrated the productive hours, throughput and maintenance costs needed to support its advertised commercial proposition.

Policymakers and investors should ask for extended end-to-end offshore operating records and audited unit economics before treating future production targets as investable output. The technology risk exists before accounting for nodule processing, metals markets, permitting or environmental liabilities.

The complete TFIE Briefing and linked assessment show the fleet calculations, technical assumptions and capital sensitivities. The remaining articles in the five-part series address processing, metals markets and American Samoa’s exposure.

#5811. Longevity platform Hone Health nabs $228M to build out offerings

Longevity platform Hone Health nabs $228M to build out offerings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hone Health, a physician-led virtual longevity care platform, has raised $228 million.

The investment is structured as a combination of equity and growth financing. The $28 million series A extension was led by MarcyPen Capital Partners, with participation from Pentland Ventures, Kinship Ventures, Hanwha, Gannet Capital, humbition and Comma Capital. Other angel investors also participated, including the Jonas brothers. The $200 million in growth financing came from General Catalyst’s Customer Value Fund.

Hone will use the investment to support a number of expansions. It plans to launch more programming for metabolic health, PMOS, inflammation and immunity, and to launch a physician-supervised peptide program. It will add more diagnostics, including coronary CT angiography and gut microbiome analysis.

Hone also plans to continue building out its AI operating system that brings data together to help personalize care. And it plans to build out clinical trial infrastructure to support pharma partners.

“Longevity is thought of as this super exotic thing. […] I actually think longevity is now becoming primary care,” Saad Alam, co-founder and CEO of Hone, told Fierce Healthcare.

It costs $65 to test for 70 biomarkers with Hone, followed by a clinician consult with the option to add more diagnostics. Labs, health history, symptoms and medications are run through its AI, which then gives providers treatment recommendations. Hone can keep adding additional diagnostics and medications, per Alam, “and the AI gets smarter as we get more patients into that system.”

Additionally, Hone is publicly announcing Hone Black, a new concierge longevity membership that has operated in stealth for a select group of patients for the past year. It combines diagnostics on over 1,600 health markers with ongoing medical care from physicians. Offerings include prescriptions, supplements, peptides and lifestyle guidance, plus exosome and stem cell therapies.

Alam founded the company after experiencing a shift in his health at 35 years old, including mental health issues, depression and weight gain. His doctor was initially of little help. It took Alam a year to find out he had low testosterone, and after treatment, he finally felt back on track. That experience, along with his father dying from kidney disease and his mother going through early menopause, opened his eyes to the longevity space.

“This whole concept of longevity is going to be one of the single greatest problems I can dedicate my life to solving,” Alam realized at the time. He experimented with everything, from peptides to cold plunges to meditation.

Hone is a subsidiary of Time Therapeutics, which aims to support production of drugs that help people live longer. Alam, who started his career doing market research at Eli Lilly, knew clinical trials are expensive and inaccessible. He wanted to create a company that could help commercialize drugs. The first step to that is Hone, or a DTC virtual care company that can capture longitudinal data on patients. The next phase will be publishing research. Finally, Hone will look to partner with pharma to get drugs to market faster.

The company has been providing care for five years, treating about 200,000 patients to date. It requires patients to have a physician and get bloodwork every 90 days in order to get medication refills. Members can contact their clinician between sessions as needed.

“We make sure, for the most part, that you are doing bloodwork and talking to a physician, a real conversation with a physician, in order to get prescribed medication,” Alam said.

It isn’t about overprescribing medications. It’s about finding the most personalized protocol for each patient. “The companies that have—no pun intended—longevity are the ones that are going to figure out how to reduce the number of medications somebody might need,” Alam said.

Hone began with hormone therapy, then moved into longevity care with biomarker analysis and women’s health. Two months ago, Hone’s offerings for women surpassed offerings for men in terms of net new members. It soon plans to run clinical trials on poly-chronic patients as well to help determine the right drug at the right time. Hone crossed $100 million in revenue in 2025, per Alam, and is on track to double this year.

“When I started goop, wellness was a niche conversation,” Gwyneth Paltrow, founder and CEO of goop and co-founder of Kinship Ventures, said in a press release. “Today, it’s one of the biggest categories in the world, and the next step is bringing real health and wellness to the masses, not just the few. Saad has built Hone to do exactly that, with serious science and at real scale. That’s why Kinship invested, and we could not be more excited about what’s ahead.”

Hone will soon offer 14 longevity care categories, which Alam argues is akin to primary care. He estimates that Hone only refers to in-person care about 10% of the time, usually for higher-acuity conditions. Some Hone patients are already healthy; most are sick and willing to pay out of pocket because the current system has been inadequate in solving their health needs, per Alam.

“This is just the beginning of not only a way but a complete change in the way that medicine is practiced,” Alam said. “A lot of other companies are what I’ll call pill mills, and where we’ve really focused—our energy is making sure that we go through the more difficult work of managing the long-term health of our patients.”

#5810. Electric Vans — Are They The Final EV Frontier? Kia PV5.

We took a trip to see the Kia PV5 electric van. Our brief takeaway: “Kool like a Kia.” Should keep the bean counters happy.

Inspired by a comment in the Electric Vehicle Council report about electric vans, Majella and I decided to have a short visit to a group of dealerships under the Norris Motors banner. We hoped to check out the LDV eDeliver 5 van, touted as selling for under AU$50,000. However, there was no stock to view and we ended up chatting to the dealer and then moving on to the MG U9 BEV ute. You can read about it here. And then we found the Kia PV5 delivery van.

Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls the lack of affordable electric vans in Australia “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” We think that is about to change. Since its launch in May, 496 PV5 have sold in Australia.

The Kia PV5 reminded me a lot of the Renault Kangoo in both size and shape, and it is able to carry about the same payload. And the front looked like the BMW i3. The one we looked at had been sold and was awaiting a fitout as a camper van. The Norris Group Kia specialist was generous with his time and the information he shared. He has been selling the Kia EV5 for the past 5 months and delivered 5 of the vans. He sold his first one within a week of its arrival on the lot — to Ray, a driver working for Amazon.

Ray appreciated the 390 km range. My friendly sales rep tells me that this will be increased in 2027 models to 420 km. Ray was getting out of diesel and most of his questions were around charging and regen braking. Most of his trips were around the burbs and he was not intending to take the van on the highway. The Kia PV5 is a workhorse, with a passenger car feel and level of comfort. It even has electric lumbar support for the driver — great when you are spending so much time in the van delivering parcels in the lead-up to Christmas. It certainly has a lot more tech than the diesel he was trading in.

In fact, he was “blown away” by the standard features — the 360-degree camera, for example. “Vans are not known for their visibility.” On his return to the dealership for his first service, he commented on the smoothness of the torque: “It has no issues going uphill.” He reported that the van felt “well planted on the road.” Ray has gotten used to trickle charging at home and has lost his range anxiety. Like most new EV drivers, once he started living and working with the vehicle, his fears vanished. The PV5 supports fast charging also for when Ray needs a boost on the road.

But what about that all-important question of cost? Can we make the bean counters happy? The Kia PV5 retails for AU$56,000 (before on-road costs), which is about the same price as comparable diesel vans like the Ford Transit and the Toyota HiAce. Add to that the savings on fuel, the peace of mind from fuel security, and the reduced servicing and it is easy to build a business case for the Kia PV5 — cost per parcel delivery. The PV5 even comes with vehicle to load.

And it isn’t made in China! Can the PV5 replace Toyota’s HiAce? Find the answer in this video. The reviewer points out many features that make the PV5 an attractive option: the charge port is on the front. The van has lots of nooks and crannies for storage. She measures storage by capacity to fit in bottles of wine — for example, two in the glove box. There is a step provided to alight the van, aided by grab handles. More storage space is provided above the steering wheel, including a nifty phone slot. There are customizable buttons on the steering wheel to create shortcuts; for example, to the air conditioning settings. A wireless phone charger is nestled near the dash. The glovebox contains the V2L adaptor supplied with the van.

The fact that the van reverts to the lowest regen setting every time you restart the car is a little niggle. That, and there is no frunk — reducing the reviewer’s all-important need for wine storage. Full specs can be found here. Kia even gives you helpful advice on how to apply for electric vehicle subsidies (last page, bottom right). One of the pictures that caught my eye shows the PV5 as a flatbed truck.

The PV5 was recently named 2026 International Van of the Year. It measures 4695mm long, 2255mm wide, and 1899mm tall on a 2995mm wheelbase. Range is stated as 416 km, but will of course vary with driving style and load. The battery is a 71.2kWh nickel cobalt manganese (NCM) lithium-ion battery. Kia offers a 165,000 km warranty on the battery. The van is front-wheel drive.

The PV5 has over 4 cubic metres of cargo capacity, double both that of the ID Buzz and the Tesla Model Y. Space enough for two euro pallets. There are multiple access points, with sliding doors on both sides and barn doors in the rear. The rear barn doors open to 180 degrees, and a low 419mm load height improves accessibility.

Goods being shipped are protected by padding on the rear doors and insides of the back. The PV5 can carry up to 740 kg. Tiedowns on the floor of the rear of the van help to secure cargo. There is also a power point in the back of the van. Perhaps a tradie can recharge their tools on site. No frunk for wine.

From the spec sheet: “Standard driver assist features include blind-spot collision avoidance, navigation-based adaptive cruise control, lane-keep assist, lane centring with hands-on detection, and parking collision avoidance assist.”

A launch in the USA is financially difficult because of the 25% “chicken tax.” Kia builds the PV5 in South Korea and classifies it as a commercial vehicle, and it faces a 25% US import tariff on light trucks and commercial vans.

Sadly, the Kia PV5 doesn’t come in red, but only in white, grey and black. Most companies may take up the option of wrapping the van in their own livery. Overall, we were impressed with Kia PV5 and look forward to seeing more of them on the road throughout the suburbs as Australian delivery drivers move towards a bright electric future.

#5809. Can Tonsillectomy Help for Non-Asian Patients With IgA Nephropathy?

— Study showed more achieved early remission when adding surgery to immunosuppression

Immunoglobulin A nephropathy (IgAN), a chronic autoimmune kidney disease, is driven by mucosal immune activation that produces galactose-deficient IgA1, forming immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli and trigger progressive kidney injury.

While tonsillectomy combined with pulsed corticosteroids has long been an established treatment in Japanese practice, international guidelines have explicitly steered nephrologists away from surgical intervention in non-Japanese patients due to a lack of evidence outside Asian cohorts.

However, a recent prospective comparative study in Nephrology Dialysis Transplantation suggested that pairing tonsillectomy with immunosuppressive therapy may substantially improve remission rates and curb kidney function decline in white patients at high risk of IgAN progression.

"One could not be surprised at all, considering IgAN as the kidney's response to aberrant activation of MALT [mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue]," study author Vladimir Dobronravov, MD, PhD, of Pavlov University in St. Petersburg, Russia, told MedPage Today. "It would be logical to assume that removing the trigger concurrently with targeting of glomerular inflammation might lead to a reduction in disease activity and better outcomes."

In the single-center, nonrandomized study, Dobronravov and a co-author assessed 221 white patients with primary IgAN at high risk of progression -- 85 who underwent tonsillectomy alongside immunosuppressive therapy and 136 who received immunosuppression alone.

Nearly all patients (94.6%) received renin-angiotensin system inhibitors, and 72.3% were treated with glucocorticoid monotherapy (intravenous methylprednisolone induction followed by a 6- to 12-month oral prednisolone taper). Average patient age was 35 years, and 52% were male.

High risk of disease progression was defined as proteinuria over 1 g/24 hours with hematuria, or proteinuria under 1 g/24 hours with hematuria plus either an estimated 5-year renal replacement therapy probability above 11% or at least one active histological lesion.

Over a median follow-up of 33 months, patients who underwent tonsillectomy were significantly more likely to achieve early overall (complete or partial) remission (HR 3.36, 95% CI 2.30-4.92) and early complete remission (HR 6.32, 95% CI 2.62-15.22) compared with those who received immunosuppression alone. Results remained consistent after 1:1 propensity-score matching to account for baseline differences between groups.

Nearly all (95.2%) in the combination group achieved early overall remission (vs 72.6% of those on immunosuppression alone) and 33.9% achieved complete remission (vs 11.3% on immunosuppression alone).

"Such a substantial rate of early remission can unlikely be expected at the 30% to 62% decline in baseline proteinuria, as reported in recent and ongoing studies of targeted-release budesonide, complement inhibitors, and anti-[B-cell activating factor/a proliferation-inducing ligand] therapies," said Dobronravov.

Disease progression -- defined as at least a 40% decrease in estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) from baseline, an eGFR below 15 mL/min/1.73 m², or initiation of renal replacement therapy -- occurred in three patients (3.5%) on combination therapy compared with 39 patients (28.9%) in the immunosuppression-alone group (adjusted HR 0.13, 95% CI 0.04-0.43).

"In contrast to the current guidelines, the combination therapy may represent a reasonable and safe practical approach to the improvement of long-term prognosis in the selected subpopulation of white patients with severe IgAN," said Dobronravov. "The study results raise a broader question about how the control of other potential MALT activation factors, beyond tonsillectomy, might influence the course of IgAN."

Despite the promising findings, Dobronravov noted that "we are far from setting the suggested approach in opposition to others," particularly given the rapidly evolving field of novel IgAN therapies.

"The future of effective IgAN management is likely to be based on individualized, mechanism-based treatment selection," he suggested. "Ideally, the predominant pathogenic MALT pathways should be identified in each patient and therapeutically addressed at multiple complementary levels."

George Bonifant, MD, training program director for the nephrology fellowship at Northwell Staten Island University in New York City, who wasn't involved with the study, urged similar restraint, stating he would "place [tonsillectomy] in the category of a biologically plausible intervention that deserves further investigation, but not alongside established medical treatments for non-Japanese patients."

"The tonsils and other upper-airway mucosal lymphoid tissues are considered important sites of IgA1 production, so removing tonsils was proposed to reduce a source of pathogenic IgA production," he told MedPage Today. "Thus, the rationale for this treatment choice is immunologic."

However, Bonifant said it is too early to recommend adding tonsillectomy solely to improve IgAN outcomes on the strength of this single study.

"What would be persuasive is a multicenter randomized trial in non-Japanese patients, with both groups receiving contemporary background treatment. It should measure sustained proteinuria reduction, kidney function decline, kidney failure, surgical complications, quality of life, and total exposure to immunosuppression," he noted. "The clinical question is not just whether the urine looks better after surgery; it is whether patients keep their kidney function longer with an acceptable overall treatment burden."

"This is encouraging research, but it is not yet a reason to have your tonsils removed to treat your kidney disease," said Bonifant.
