#5807. National Academies report calls for faster decisions on publication retractions and corrections

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

West Coast Biotech & Life Sciences Reporter

Jonathan Wosen is STAT’s West Coast biotech & life sciences reporter. You can reach Jonathan on Signal at jwosen.27.

A new report commissioned by the National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine urged the scientific community to respond more aggressively to claims of errors or manipulated data in published research, saying that papers containing findings that can no longer be trusted should be more swiftly corrected or retracted.

The report encouraged journals to complete editorial review of potentially problematic studies within 12 weeks, or within six months in more complex cases. The authors also stressed that study authors need to hold onto original data, adding that being unable to produce these records when concerns about a finding’s authenticity are raised could lead to a retraction. And the expert panel wrote that it is no longer “prudent” for researchers to argue against retraction of their work by asserting that flawed findings in one part of a study don’t impact the paper’s overall conclusions. That’s because issues with any part of a study can cast doubt on other findings.

The authors called on universities and other research institutions to respond more quickly and completely to research integrity inquiries, in part by making findings from internal inquiries available to journals, funding organizations, and the broader public. And the National Academies report suggested that funding organizations tie grant awards to researchers and institutions agreeing to abide by strict standards for the conduct of research and addressing errors.

“While the committee’s recommendations might appear aspirational, we are certain that the funding agencies, the public officials who legislate financial and regulatory support for science, researchers, and, most importantly, the public must be assured that the record of scientific knowledge is intact and reliable,” wrote University of California, Berkeley, cell biologist Randy Schekman, chair of the committee that produced the report.

An advanced copy of the report was sent to media outlets and embargoed until 11 a.m. EST on Thursday. But Jennifer Walsh, a National Academies spokesperson, told STAT in an email that “the report is being paused pending further review of some of the citations.” She did not provide a new release time and, as of Thursday evening, the report has not been published on the nonprofit’s website. Reporting by Science alleges that 10 of the report’s references contain apparently fabricated first names for co-authors, suggesting that a large language model may have written the references. The National Academies delayed publication after Science flagged the apparent issues.

The 157-page National Academies report comes at a time when concerns about the reliability of the scientific record have drawn increasing public attention, contributing to distrust in scientific institutions. High-profile investigations at leading research centers such as Stanford University and Dana-Farber Cancer Institute have in recent years shined an uncomfortable spotlight on how studies published in top journals can contain doctored data. Meanwhile, the number of scientific articles published each year has risen from 1.3 million in 1995 to 6.7 million in 2025, increasing the number of studies that potentially require corrective action.

Retractions have become more common in recent years, with the report noting that more than 5,000 studies were retracted worldwide in 2025. But the report alleges that this rate is lower than expected given current estimates of the proportion of problematic articles, with fewer than 0.22% of studies from any given year retracted. Data from Retraction Watch indicate that, on average, retractions happen nearly 2.4 years after a study is published.

Correcting the record more often and more quickly, the authors wrote, won’t just require clearer policies from funding organizations, journals, and universities. It will take a cultural shift in science toward viewing correction and retraction as essential tools in maintaining the reliability of the record, not as a professional failure. They encouraged stakeholders to think of the consequences of not taking corrective action on studies, from policymaking and public health decisions made based on faulty data to researchers wasting time and taxpayer dollars when they attempt to follow up on a flawed finding.

“Serious consequences for the scientific enterprise and for society in general result either from failing to correct the scientific record or from failing to do so in a timely way,” the panel wrote. “These consequences make changing the process essential.”

While many corrections and retractions result from honest errors, the Trump administration has cited cases of alleged research misconduct as justification for its focus on “gold standard” science. In the new report, experts noted that these incidents can weaken public trust in science.

“It is critical that the scientific ecosystem continues to prioritize maintaining the integrity of the scientific record so that it remains trusted and trustworthy,” the authors wrote. “Even if these cases are rare, just one such incident erodes the public’s trust.”

That recognition spurred the National Academies, a 163-year-old nonprofit created by Congress to advise the nation on issues of science and technology, to assemble a team of university, journal, and academic leaders to explore what should be done. They included Holden Thorp, editor-in-chief of the Science family of journals; Schekman, a Nobel Prize-winning researcher and former editor-in-chief of the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences; and Mark Barnes, a lawyer with the firm Ropes & Gray and former associate provost for research at Harvard University. The group was tasked with identifying current practices and suggesting new standards for stakeholders.

The authors lay out what they frame as an “ideal timeline” for addressing concerns with published studies. That includes four weeks for an editor to determine whether a concern is potentially credible, meaning that it is specific, makes sense, and merits a closer look. The committee then allots another four weeks for contacting authors and determining whether their response adequately resolves a concern before initiating a full review. They note that an initial notice, such as an editorial expression of concern, should be added to a study within 12 weeks.

It’s a well-intentioned timeline, said Lisa Rasmussen, a bioethicist at the University of North Carolina at Charlotte. But Rasmussen, who is also editor-in-chief of the journal Accountability in Research, said that editors like herself are already stretched thin with work that is often poorly compensated. She noted that, in the past five years, the number of studies submitted to her own journal has increased almost tenfold. Rasmussen and a co-editor-in-chief often message each other with drowning emojis to sum up how they are doing.

“I think under the best of circumstances, it’s very reasonable. That’s certainly the kind of time frame that I try to abide by,” she said. “I think it’s just going to be increasingly difficult for people to handle the volume of concerns [and] queries that they’re receiving in the time frame.”

Rasmussen noted that much of the rise in submissions took place after large language models powered by artificial intelligence, such as ChatGPT, became readily available. The report touches on the impact of AI on research integrity, noting that these tools have simultaneously allowed researchers to manipulate or fake data while giving journals and universities new tools to spot suspect results. But to Rasmussen, the report’s discussion on AI was surprisingly brief given the technology’s disruptive potential.

“AI is being used to review. AI is being used to critique. AI is being used to fabricate data. So it’s like jet fuel for all of the stuff they talk about in the report,” she said. “But it’s almost as though the report was written pre-ChatGPT.”

West Coast Biotech & Life Sciences Reporter

Jonathan Wosen is STAT’s West Coast biotech & life sciences reporter. You can reach Jonathan on Signal at jwosen.27.

#5806. These Are The Stories Of The First Time The Jalopnik Audience Drove A Car

Earlier this week we asked you to take a trip down memory lane and share the story of your very first time driving a car, and it seems like a lot of you have really great dads, since most of you said you first drove on your dad's lap as a kid. Beyond the lap driving though, you, the dear, sweet Jalopnik audience, shared lots of entertaining memories. This is only a roundup of a few popular answers, but feel free to go back and read through the ones that didn't make the cut.

I recounted the story of the first time I took the keys to a friend's second-generation Nissan Altima and invited myself to do some acceleration runs in a parking lot when I was in middle school, which is a bad idea and is absolutely not something that anyone else should do, to be clear. We're not glorifying car theft, underage, or unlicensed driving here, we're just sharing some stories. If you have an exceptionally entertaining tale, feel free to share it in the comments section below, but these are a few of the stories behind the Jalopnik audience's first time driving a car.

The first time i drove a car, my dad had terminal cancer, and i was 14 years old. In our huge Dallas neighborhood, he would pretend to pass out and tell me to take the wheel of the 1999 Plymouth Grand Voyager!

A few weeks later on a Saturday or Sunday he took me to an empty elementary school parking lot, and taught me how finally use the steering wheel while being in the correct seat and how to get used to the pedals.

My by the book father, broke the rules to teach me skill he knew i was itching to learn. One of the most fun weekends of my life. He made it another 8 months, and took me out to different parking lots on weekends that he was feeling well! Made that time a little bit easier on both of us i think.

The day of my 16th birthday, my mom drove me to the DMV to get my learner's permit. As we walked out to the car, me with my fresh permit in my hand, it was snowing like a banshee. My mom handed me the keys and said, "You get to drive us home."

We lived on a hill, and as I tried to slow down and turn into our driveway, (no ABS or stability control back in the '80s) I ended up sliding into the neighbor's front yard and taking out her wooden flower boxes.

For the next couple of months it was a very snowy winter, so I learned how to become a great driver on snow and ice. I took my driver's test in February during a snowstorm and passed it on the first try.

Until the day I got my learner's permit, I had never driven a car before.

I grew in dairy farm in rular Finland, so I've been operating heavy equipment since I was heavy enough to press clutch and brakes. So around 6 years old back in the golden 80's. My first run with car was our Saab 99 around 7 years old. It was first gasoline powered vehicle I've tested, so throttle surprised me a bit and I ran over our laundry drying apparatus. Father was not pleased. However first proper drive was around 10-11 year old, when my grandfather had accident with tractor PTO and lost his thumb. It was bleeding quite a lot so I drove him 5km to health center. Nobody batted eyelid to that :D.

First time I drove solo was to go from home to my parent's florist. Easy drive. Had to turn right on a busy road that had about 6 lanes. I had a green light and just took it. Didn't realize that someone was taking a left coming the other way and going into my lane. I had to brake hard to avoid hitting them. Then they brake checked and cut me off a couple times as I tried to drive safely and easily. Eventually, they got mad at someone else and intentionally caused an accident. Today we call that Road Rage. I had no concept of what it was and how it worked and was scared to death.

I grew up on a small farm, so I drove our vehicles around some well before I had my permit. But my dad would take my brother and me to the Eagle River area in Wisconsin every summer, and one time he took us to dinner and afterwards realized he was not fit to drive. So he had me drive the three of us back to the cabin. I was 14. He said I did a good job, but how would he know? 😂

I remember he had a black third-gen Nissan Maxima. But I'm sure I drove so carefully and slowly it didn't matter what it was.

For me, it was easy: Dad was the local Chevrolet dealer up until about eight months before my sixteenth birthday. He'd had a long habit of bringing home whatever was the strangest car in the used lot when he came home for lunch, so I could spend the hour crawling all over it. We had a very long driveway, and lived at the end of a three block long dead end street. So, by the time I'd turned twelve, if the car had an automatic I'd invariably get behind the wheel, first laps in the driveway, and shortly after six block long round trips. The first car was a Renault Dauphine with push-button automatic transmission.

More on the lines of what you're looking for: The first 'illegal' drive was grabbing dad's company car (1965 Impala SS hardtop) and taking it for a 15-20 mile drive in the summer of 1965 while the rest of the family was on the weekly summer drive in Mom's station wagon. Shortly thereafter, realizing he was leaving the dealership (I didn't know) dad swapped the Impala for a Corvair Monza coupe because his kid was a complete Corvair junkie. Had it out a couple of times, too.

My father was an incredibly strict disciplinarian (egged on my mom, who was the real force) except when it came to cars. I got away with murder on that, and I'm certain I wasn't getting away with nearly as much as I thought I did at the time.

I was 13. My (unknown to me at the time) doper uncle asked me, "Have you ever driven a car?" He fixed my reply by letting me drive him around Alabama backroads for an hour in his MGB. I was so excited later that afternoon to tell my parents what John let me do. They did not share my excitement.

As for me, I got a lifetime love of cars, especially rowing my own. RIP, Uncle John!

I used to live in farm country, we weren't farmers, but fields for miles in the Salinas Valley. I was 12 or 13 when my father took me to a dirt lot out in the fields. There he turned me loose in a 1972 VW squareback. With in 5 – 10 minutes I was cruising around that lot like I had been driving my whole life. Also most will admit that early air cooled VWs are extremely forgiving, and easy to drive. When done in the dirt lot he told me to go ahead and drive home. Taught me the rules of the road, signs, don't run over people, etc. I was excited to say the least. The next morning he had me drive us from home to work, then school which was 20 miles on the 101 hwy. From that day on, I drove everyday to my father's shop. And he would always tell me "straight to school, and back." Like I listened.

#5805. RISE St. James, Sierra Club Sue LDEQ to Vacate Hyundai Steel Air Permit

LDEQ issued air permit to the facility despite serious flaws in permit.

Donaldsonville, Louisiana — Today, RISE St. James Louisiana and Sierra Club filed a lawsuit challenging Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality’s decision to issue an air permit to Hyundai-POSCO for its proposed steelmaking facility. The groups claim that the air permit must be vacated because LDEQ did not conduct an environmental justice analysis, nor did the agency give full and careful consideration to mitigation measures like electrified components and green hydrogen, or to the environmental costs of Hyundai’s added pollution in an already overburdened area, as is required by the Louisiana state constitution and the Clean Air Act.

Throughout 2026, Sierra Club, working with other experts and local groups, submitted a series of comments to LDEQ in an effort to bring to light the errors, miscalculations, and omissions in the permit application. Despite some improvements to the permit application, made by Hyundai Steel following public pressure, the final air permit issued by LDEQ authorizes the facility to emit substantial quantities of greenhouse gases, nitrogen oxides, particulate matter, carbon monoxide, lead, mercury, ammonia, and other pollutants. Many of these pollutants cause significant and irreversible damage to human health even at low levels.

The area around the planned facility already bears a toxic air pollution burden greater than 96% of the rest of the country, and ranks high across a range of environmental and socioeconomic indicators. The Hyundai Steel Plant is one of multiple polluting facilities planned as part of the 17,000-acre Riverplex MegaPark in an area known as Cancer Alley in Ascension Parish, Louisiana.

In response, the petitioners issued the following statements:

Andrea Issod, Sierra Club Senior Attorney, said: “LDEQ must ensure the Hyundai Steel Plant permit complies with the legal requirements to reduce harmful pollution impacts as much as possible on the surrounding community, which already bears a 96% percentile burden of toxic releases as compared to the national average. By refusing to conduct an environmental justice analysis, consider cleaner technologies like electrifying components and replacing dirty gas with green hydrogen, or consider any environmental costs of Hyundai’s pollution, LDEQ has failed to perform the most basic function of its role and deprived the community of a transparent, thorough, and accurate assessment of the facility’s potential negative impacts. For that reason, we are asking the Court to reverse LDEQ’s final decision and vacate the permit for Hyundai Steel’s proposed facility.”

Mekaylyn Lavigne, Modeste Coordinator for RISE St. James, said: “I was born and raised in the small and peaceful community of Modeste. Although this region is now known as Cancer Alley because many chemical plants have polluted our air and water, my family and neighbors still enjoy sitting on our porches, watching birds and wildlife, and fishing. Now, LDEQ is allowing a steel mill to move in next door to my home, and tells us the air quality is fine and this massive iron and steel making facility will not have any negative impacts. Modeste residents deserve the truth. The court should make LDEQ go back to the drawing board on this permit to disclose all the impacts and use the cleanest technologies to reduce pollution.”

Additional information about the petition:

Rise St. James and Sierra Club note in their petition that Hyundai Steel’s air permit was issued despite LDEQ missing or critically lacking key elements of a public-trust analysis, including:

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5804. Is the Energy Crisis Accelerating the Clean Energy Transition? 4 Takeaways from the IEA’s World Energy Outlook 2026

Energy expert Nick Wayth unpacks findings from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

The International Energy Agency has published its World Energy Outlook 2026 amid an unprecedented global energy crisis that is reshaping how countries think about energy security. The report finds that the push to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels is accelerating electrification and the shift toward domestic energy sources, including renewables. Yet electricity demand is surging, grids are struggling to keep pace, and concerns about energy security are also giving coal a longer life in some countries. The IEA warns that the pursuit of energy security alone won’t deliver the emissions reductions needed to meet global climate goals.

What do these findings mean for the energy transition, and where should policymakers focus their efforts? Nick Wayth, global director of the WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition, explains four key takeaways from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

Q: How close will current and proposed policies get us to the COP31 pledge to raise electricity’s share of final energy consumption to 35% by 2035? What changes are needed to bridge the gap?

The crisis in the Strait of Hormuz has reinforced the case for electrification as a way for countries to diversify their power sources and reduce dependence on imports, improving energy security and affordability. While the world’s reliance on fossil fuels hasn’t changed substantially, there’s been a real shift in how countries think about the risks of that dependence—and the benefits of electrification. But progress is too slow. The IEA projects that under current and proposed policies, electricity will account for only 29% of final energy consumption by 2035. That’s only half the increase needed to reach the COP31 target of 35%.

Much of the technology is already available. The IEA estimates that nearly half of fossil fuel demand in road transport and more than a third of demand for low- and medium-temperature industrial heat could be electrified at a competitive cost today. The challenge is creating the conditions for businesses and households to make that switch.

To reach the target, countries need to develop detailed plans for shifting transport, heating, and heavy industry toward electricity and invest in transmission and distribution. Governments can help keep their industries competitive by making it more attractive for businesses to switch from gas to affordable electricity. Some countries are doing this successfully already. Germany, for example, has reduced electricity taxes for manufacturers and has subsidized grid charges, helping lower electricity costs for businesses.

Q: The IEA projects rapid growth in electricity demand from both air conditioning and data centers. How do the challenges posed by these two sources of demand differ?

Energy demand from AI and data centers has dominated public attention this year, but by 2035 the global growth in electricity demand from cooling is forecast to be about 70% greater than from data centers. Notably, the IEA finds that global demand for cooling buildings is overtaking demand for heating them for the first time in history.

Energy demand from data centers is concentrated in the U.S., China, and the EU. By contrast, demand for cooling is widespread, especially in emerging and developing economies where much of the population is vulnerable to extreme heat, an increasing threat to human life.

Under current and proposed government policies, cooling is projected to account for 12% of global electricity consumption by 2035. But during periods of peak demand, when millions of air conditioners are running at once, it could reach around 30% of the electricity needed. Electricity grids must be able to cope with these spikes in demand, even if they occur only occasionally. Without better planning and more efficient cooling, countries could face costly grid upgrades—or risk power outages during extreme heat.

This is why policymakers and utilities need to anticipate future electricity demand and plan investments in grid infrastructure accordingly. But building more isn’t the only solution. Governments and utilities should also invest in energy storage and other grid flexibility measures that enable them to adapt to fluctuating supply and demand as needed.

Q: The energy crisis is accelerating clean electrification in some countries but also encouraging greater reliance on fossil fuels in others. What does the IEA’s outlook tell us about the future of oil, coal, and gas—and the risks of investing in new fossil fuel infrastructure?

The report shows that global oil demand is expected to level off by 2030 under current and proposed policies. The slowdown in China’s oil demand, driven largely by electric vehicle adoption, is expected to continue. Other fast-growing economies, such as India, despite electrifying transport, are nonetheless projected to increase their demand for oil.

The current energy crisis is reinforcing these differences in strategy. Some countries are investing in electrification to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels, while others are turning to domestic fossil fuels.

The energy crisis has given coal demand a short-term boost, and the IEA now expects coal use in 2035 to be higher than it projected just a year ago. Even so, under current and proposed government policies, global coal demand is expected to fall by 14% between 2025 and 2035, driven largely by a decline in coal-fired power generation, particularly in China. But coal use in industry is projected to fall by just 5%, highlighting the challenge of reducing dependence on the fuel.

Countries need to be cautious about investing in infrastructure that may not be needed. The IEA estimates that about 400 billion cubic meters (bcm) of new liquefied natural gas (LNG) export capacity is under construction and could come online by 2035. Yet under current and stated government policies, export capacity could significantly exceed demand in 2035 by70 bcm. That surplus raises the risk of underused infrastructure and investments that fail to pay off.

The IEA estimates that the world will need at least 25 million kilometers of new power lines over the next decade to meet growing electricity demand and connect new sources of power, including renewables. Another 15 million kilometers of existing lines will need to be upgraded or replaced. Both will require substantial investment.

The biggest challenge is delivering these changes fast enough. Major grid projects can take 5 to15 years to plan, secure permits for, and complete—far longer than many renewable energy projects. Governments need to speed up permitting and grid connections, improve coordination between utilities and developers, and encourage investment before demand materializes. One encouraging example is the EU’s recent provisional agreement to streamline permitting for grid projects.

Equally important is to invest in ways to use existing assets more efficiently. Grid-enhancing technologies can speed up connections by allowing existing power lines to carry more electricity, reducing the need to build new infrastructure. And increasingly, governments and utilities are seeing the importance of making grids more flexible—building in capability and strategies to balance supply with demand in real time. Brazil, for example, pays large electricity users to temporarily reduce consumption when the grid is under strain. Businesses that fail to deliver the agreed reductions can lose payments or face suspension from programs. By combining energy storage, demand-response programs, smart grid controls, distributed energy generation, and other flexibility strategies, energy planners can meet growing demands and increase resilience while reducing the need to build more grid infrastructure.

Courtesy of WRI. By Sarah George, Senior Manager, Strategic Communications, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition; and Francisco Boshell, Global Lead, Decarbonized Energy Consumption, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5802. MAGA-Loving Elon Musk Says He's 'Not MAGA,' Feels 'Foolish' Over Spending $445 Million On Dud Republican Midterm Election Campaigns

Good morning! It's Friday, November 20, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift, your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition, Elon Musk knows he blew $500 million on the 2026 midterms, General Motors wants to double the money it makes off the war machine, Japan's largest automakers are banding together to standardize some processes and Mercedes-Benz says some German plants are in jeopardy as work stoppages spread.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

It's no secret that Republicans across the U.S. went through an all-time drubbing during the midterm elections earlier this month, and while there were many losers, perhaps none was as big as Elon Musk. The Tesla CEO and Republican version of George Soros dropped over $445 million during the election cycle in a futile attempt to get his candidates into office. That very much didn't work, and now he has buyers remorse.

In truth, it's possible that the garish sums of money Elon spent on the election may have hurt the candidates he backed more than helped them, providing opponents with easy targets to latch onto. I mean, it's not hard to claim that the guy you're running against is back by Big Money when the world's richest man is bankrolling their campaign.

In any case, Elon really wishes he could turn back time, and he's about to have a hell of a lot more to answer for when it comes to his companies as emboldened Democratic leaders vow to investigate Trump and his buddies. From Bloomberg:

"I feel pretty foolish about having spent so much money and not seeing much for it," the billionaire said late Wednesday at the New York Times' DealBook Summit. "Probably, if I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money."

Musk's political money overwhelmingly went to lost causes. Two super political action committees he funded — America PAC and Texas PAC — spent heavily to back Republican Senate and House candidates who lost, Federal Election Commission filings show. Ken Paxton's unsuccessful bid to win Texas' open Senate seat drew the lion's share of the money, at some $229 million.

Democrats also won major races further down the ballot in Texas, where Musk has built much of his corporate empire. Tesla has a sprawling factory near Austin, SpaceX launches rockets from its coastal hub reincorporated as Starbase, and the two companies plan to jointly build a chip factory called Terafab.

Texas Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has taken issue with departing Governor Greg Abbott agreeing to award $1.7 billion in tax breaks for the Terafab project and singled out other state grants SpaceX has received. She called for an immediate moratorium on data center development during her campaign and accused developers of raising utility rates and depleting water supply.

Musk nevertheless downplayed the likelihood of any conflict with Hinojosa, saying he expects her to be "quite moderate."

"I don't think she's a far-left governor," he said. "Based on everything I've heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas."

When asked about the top Democrat on the House Oversight Committee telling Trump and unspecified corporate actors to lawyer up, Musk said some in the party were being "a little over the top."

During his appearance over a livestream from China, Musk sought to distance himself from the Make American Great Again movement, describing himself as ideologically moderate. "I'm not MAGA," he said.

Technically, Musk is correct. He's not MAGA. As he told supporters in 2024, he is actually "Dark MAGA."

General Motors wants to expand the money it makes off of wars by more than doubling its defense revenue to $2 billion in the next few years. It looks to take advantage of global defense companies turning to its automotive technology to accelerate their weapons production.

Back in August, GM delivered its first batch of Patriot missile components to Lockheed Martin within 22 days of the two companies entering a partnership in weapons manufacturing — something championed by the Trump administration. Several other large defense companies with governmental ties have also lined up to sign deals with GM. From the Financial Times:

"We have massive manufacturing capabilities to build defence equipment components. We have so many defence companies coming to us and saying help us," Stephen duMont, head of GM Defense, said in an interview in London.

In recent years, companies including Volkswagen, Jaguar Land Rover and Renault have diversified into the defence sector in search of new sources of revenue as profits become squeezed in the traditional automotive business due to cut-throat competition and higher costs.

Jim Farley, Ford's chief executive, confirmed earlier this year that it was also in discussions with the US government on defence-related projects, while Stellantis last week agreed to sell its Brampton plant to Canadian armoured vehicle maker Roshel and explore "global collaboration".

GM's ties to defence stretch back decades. It played a pivotal part in America's "Arsenal of Democracy" campaign during the second world war, converting all of its US plants to support the war effort. The group sold its defence business in 2003 and later re-established GM Defense as a subsidiary in 2017 after Mary Barra became chief executive.

"Mary recognised from early on the opportunity to leverage GM's investment in the automotive business for profitable growth of the defence business," said duMont, a former Raytheon executive.

The defence unit, a separate subsidiary that now has about 500 to 600 employees, was "like a start-up", allowing for quicker decision-making and operational changes.

In the grand scheme of things (read: General Motors), the defense unit is minuscule. It only generates about $1 billion in revenue every year, which isn't exactly nothing, but when compared with the group's total revenue of $185 billion, it's sort of small potatoes. That's why CEO Mary Barra wants to expand it.

It isn't just parts of weapons, either. It's also vehicles. The U.S. Army recently increased orders for Infantry Squad Vehicles from an initial 649 vehicles to nearly 12,000 — which could lead to a $1 billion contract. In addition, it's looking at getting in on the defense business in Europe and the UK.

The business of war is booming, and GM wants in on the action.

Japan's largest automakers, including Toyota, Honda and Nissan, are starting a new initiative to compete less and collaborate more as a way to deal with cheap Chinese competition. The first three things they'll do are harmonize quality criteria for steel, unify recycling protocols for plastics, and create a universal color-coding scheme for wire harnesses, but more steps are on the way. The Japan Automobile Manufacturers Association says these and any other big changes are coming to any suppliers doing business with Japanese automakers. From Automotive News:

Discussions will begin with Japanese suppliers but soon encompass international ones.

JAMA Chairman Koji Sato, who is also vice chairman of Toyota Motor Corp., launched the campaign after taking the helm of the industry lobby group in January. Leaders announced an agreement on steel, resin, and wire harnesses at the group's last regular meeting of the year here to set the stage for implementing harmonization from 2027.

The urgent efficiency drive is one of the most ambitious attempts yet to reshape a sprawling supply base spanning Toyota, Nissan, Honda, Mazda, Subaru, Mitsubishi and Suzuki. The goal: sharpen Japan Inc.'s edge amid an onslaught of new technologies and cutthroat new competitors from China by cutting costs and channeling the savings into innovation.

"We all share this strong sense of crisis, creating a momentum for change," Sato said. "Based upon our sense of a crisis, we decided to choose these three areas."

A JAMA task force leading the push has been working all year to formulate a framework. They decided on steel, resin and wire harnesses partly because they are areas of the vehicle that go unnoticed by customers and do not directly impact brand value or differentiation. The areas also account for a large portion of a vehicle's input costs, allowing for a large immediate impact.

JAMA said about 70,000 variants of wire harnesses are a prime candidate for consolidation.

Japan will consolidate and rationalize low-volume steel materials.

The group will standardize steel requirements that used to be specific to each carmaker and work to implement these new standards with the Japan Iron and Steel Federation.

Japan will standardize what kind of plastic and resin materials are recycled and how the materials are evaluated. This will help secure domestic sourcing through recycling.

Standardization will focus on sorting methods, traceability and design for easy disassembly.

Even beyond the idea of fending off Chinese competition, I think collaboration like this is a solid approach. If this type of shared componentry doesn't materially change the car, there's no real harm, and it cuts down on the supply chain waste of having every manufacturer have its own supply chain and specifications.

A senior labor leader in Germany is skeptical of Mercedes-Benz's plan to solve the company's problems by simply forcing staff to work longer hours — adding that the future of its plants would still be uncertain. Even a deal that would extend the 35-hour work week would leave underutilized factories and unresolved questions over the automaker's model lineup, according to IG Metall's Barbara Resch. From Bloomberg:

"We need a management board that believes in Germany as an industrial location — and demonstrates that with firm commitments on products and investment," said Resch, who also sits on the carmaker's supervisory board. Regular rounds of cost-cutting and unreliable commitments are eroding trust and the company "needs to take responsibility and say what future it plans for its German sites," she added.

With worker representatives holding half the seats on supervisory boards at large German automakers, labor holds significant sway over decisions including factory investments and restructuring. Powerful works councils and collective bargaining agreements add another layer of involvement in changes to jobs, working hours and plant operations.

That influence is being tested as Germany's auto industry confronts one of its most difficult periods in decades. Manufacturers are struggling with weak demand in Europe and China, intense competition from Chinese rivals and the costly transition to electric vehicles, while high labor and production costs have increased pressure on German factories.

These challenges have intensified a debate over who should shoulder the burden of restructuring. Automakers argue they can no longer afford long-standing benefits, while unions and works councils say employees are being asked to pay for strategic mistakes.

At both Mercedes and Volkswagen AG, management is seeking to extend the 35-hour workweek without extra pay and freeze wages for two years. IG Metall officials at VW have called the demands a "catalog of horrors," saying the company also wants to cut profit-sharing, scrap supplemental payments and eliminate a paid break for some shift workers.

The union's sharper rhetoric coincides with a wave of 24-hour stoppages it has organized Friday, part of a separate nationwide dispute with manufacturing companies over pay, benefits and working hours. The escalation is set to disrupt activity at companies already under pressure from weak demand, high costs and tougher global competition.

The union's rhetoric comes with a wave of 24-hour work stoppages that it organized on Friday as part of a nationwide dispute with companies over pay, benefits and working hours. It's something we actually told you about yesterday, and the escalation is expected to disrupt activity at companies that are already under pressure from weak demand, rising costs and strong global competition.

It's hard to imagine what the world of vehicle travel would be like without the tree-position traffic light, but the car itself predeates it by nearly 40 years. Kudos to Morgan for coming up with something that has very much stood the test of time. I mean, traffic lights of today are just about the same as they were 103 years ago. If you want to learn more, head over to History.com.

Folks, we've never been more back. Fuel prices continue to fall, and it couldn't come at a better time as millions of Americans prepare to hit the roads ahead of Thanksgiving. Of course, they'll have to pry themselves away from their PlayStations and Xboxs and their new girlfriends, Lucia, but that's another matter. In any case, WTI Crude Oil futures and Brent Crude prices are slightly down, sitting at $82 and $91, respectively, at the time of publication.

The average price of a gallon of regular gas dropped another 3 cents overnight to $3.69 (nice), according to AAA. Diesel saw a similar 3-cent retreat and is now sitting at $5.89, and that means you've got more money in your pocket for Lucia.

Last night, I saw an advanced screening of "Artificial," and let me tell you, it's quite a bummer. It also might be the best movie of the year. I'm not sure what side of the AI fence you fall on (I asumme you can tell what side I fall on), but at the very least, you'll come away from this movie realizing that this all-powerful tool certainly should not be controlled by a few dork clowns in California we didn't ask to be put in charge.

#5801. HLTH 2026: Noom, Hims & Hers roll out peptide offerings

HLTH 2026: Noom, Hims & Hers roll out peptide offerings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

LAS VEGAS—Direct-to-consumer health companies Noom and Hims & Hers are making a play for the peptide market as the federal government loosens restrictions on compounded treatments.

The companies are betting that peptides could be the next big thing in consumer health. For Noom, the new peptide offering extends the company's business beyond weight loss into the broader longevity and wellness market. Hims & Hers signaled months ago that it was ready to tap into the multi-billion-dollar peptide market.

Federal policy shifts and U.S. Food and Drug Administration enforcement discretion around compounded peptides have opened new pathways for telehealth and compounding pharmacies.

On Tuesday, Noom launched OpenRx as a separate peptide and preventive health brand. At launch, OpenRx offers two injectable options: sermorelin, a peptide frequently prescribed for muscle support, and NAD+, a coenzyme frequently prescribed for energy. OpenRx also offers two oral dissolvable tablet options: the first formulation combines the peptides PT-141 and oxytocin with tadalafil to support sexual health. The second ODT formulation is the peptide oxytocin to support intimacy.

Noom says it plans to expand into other areas including recovery, muscle health, skin, gut health and sleep. Peptides are small pieces of protein that occur naturally in the body and help control functions such as growth, healing and appetite.

“Peptides are an important portion of the formulary of healthy aging,” Geoff Cook, CEO of Noom, said in a statement. “OpenRx enables our flagship Noom brand to deepen into cardiometabolic health while the new brand OpenRx supports patients with conditions too often neglected by traditional care models, like energy, muscle composition, skin health, sexual health, sleep, and recovery. Together, both brands support the same mission: to empower everyone, everywhere to live better longer – every day.”

Noom says every OpenRx prescription is written by a licensed clinician following an evaluation of the patient’s health history, goals and clinical appropriateness. Treatments are fulfilled through Noom’s U.S.-based pharmacy network, which operates under state boards of pharmacy oversight and adheres to USP compounding standards, the company said.

Noom is currently reviewing BPC-157 and TB-500 as part of its ongoing process of evaluating emerging treatments for inclusion in our formulary, Jeffrey Egler, M.D., Noom's chief medical officer, said in a statement. "Each therapy offered by OpenRx is selected because our clinical team and I believe it is appropriate for improving patient health. The process involves continuous monitoring of safety-related signals, gathering the judgment of our medical directors and the medical directors of affiliated clinical networks based on years of prescribing experience with the compounds in question, ensuring a compliant and stable supply chain, sourcing of active pharmaceutical ingredients, and setting up processes for compliance with state and federal adverse event reporting guidelines," Egler said.

Online health and wellness brand Hims & Hers announced earlier this month it planned to roll out a peptides business. As part of the company's new Peptides+ specialty, launched today, it plans to initially sell five U.S.-compounded peptide and longevity treatments—Sermorelin, NAD+, Glutathione, Vitamin B12 and L-Carnitine, with plans to add more peptides and therapies over time.

The company also plans to offer U.S.-made BPC-157 and TB-500 "consistent with regulatory requirements," the company said.

A survey the company conducted in October of 2,000 adults found that more than half (55%) said they were interested in or curious about peptide therapies, and 40% of U.S. adults say they are interested or very interested in a telehealth offering of injectable prescription treatments, including peptides and other compounded treatments. Nearly 1 in 10 U.S. adults (9%) say they currently use peptides, not including FDA-approved peptides such as Ozempic, Wegovy and insulin, the survey found.

Peptides are in the spotlight due to the massive success of FDA-approved GLP-1 weight loss medications like Ozempic and Wegovy, which are peptide drugs. Wellness influencers and biohackers on social media are promoting other lab-made peptides for fast-tracked healing, anti-aging and muscle growth.

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has championed looser federal rules on trendy, unapproved peptide injections. In July, an FDA advisory committee recommended broader access to six unapproved peptides. The panel recommended adding these six substances to the Section 503A Bulks List, which would remove the restrictions on how they are produced by compounders, Fierce Pharma reported.

Stopping short of a formal declaration allowing their use, Kennedy has cleared the way for the compounding of six popular wellness peptides, Fierce Pharma reported. Under Kennedy’s "enforcement discretion policy" issued in late October, HHS will not take enforcement action against pharmacies, physicians and federal facilities compounding six specific peptides, assuming those parties meet certain specific qualifications.

But medical experts have raised safety and clinical evidence questions around unapproved or experimental compounds. The American Medical Association (AMA) publicly opposes the Trump administration’s plan to make certain unapproved peptides more widely available for human use.

In a letter (PDF) to the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), the AMA pushed back what it called a "rushed" decision to permit the compounding of certain peptides because they have not been approved for safe use by the FDA.

Unproven peptides may pose serious health risks, including allergic reactions, infections, hormonal imbalances and other unexpected side effects, according to the FDA website.

"We should not bypass the science and put patients at risk by making them guinea pigs for unapproved drugs," AMA President Willie Underwood III, M.D., said in a statement. "If these substances are safe and effective, the evidence should demonstrate that through the FDA’s rigorous review process."

Hims & Hers' peptide therapy program provides a "clinically guided, personalized plan" for consumers "backed by a licensed provider who understands these therapies," according to Anant Vinjamoori, M.D., chief medical officer of the Hims brand.

Hims & Hers wants to "set the standard" for peptide therapy and offer consistency in the quality of the products, Vinjamoori said in an interview on the sidelines of the HLTH conference this week.

"If you think about what we have to bring to the table with our scale and resources, I would argue there's no one better equipped to do it than us," Vinjamoori said.

Hims & Hers has invested in its supply chain and expanded its domestic manufacturing facilities. In 2025, the company acquired a Menlo Park peptide facility to run API validation and stability testing to support its specialized peptide offerings.

"That's why we're investing in our supply chain and doing a lot of the hard work that, frankly, is not the fastest path to making these available. There are pharmacies that are already out there that are making these available, and there are companies out there that will simply just plug into those pharmacies and service patients. But there are inconsistencies," he noted.

Until now, getting these types of medications has often meant relying on unreliable and unregulated online sellers, or a high-cost longevity clinic, Hims & Hers executives said. Hims & Hers' peptides offering gives consumers more accessible care, "backed by real medical leadership and with consistent clinical oversight," the company said in a press release.

Hims & Hers believes its scale gives it an opportunity and responsibility to help build the evidence base around peptides, using growing patient interest not just to expand access but to better understand how the treatments can be used safely and effectively, Vinjamoori said.

Expanding into peptides fits with Hims & Hers' ongoing focus on personalized health, he noted. "We started with sexual health, moved on to hair loss, and then we've continued to broaden into other categories such as hormone replacement, GLP-1s, and now peptides, and many others to come. And the common theme in all of this is that we're talking about helping people accomplish a specific goal that they have. We're not talking about curing a disease or treating a disease per se," he said. "We're talking about, 'Hey, there's something you want to do in your life that you're not able to do right now. Let's help you figure out how we can put together a plan to help you accomplish that."

"This is a big part of the future of medicine itself," Vinjamoori added. "It's been the missing part of the equation, where we've been very focused on treating and responding to disease, but not as much, not just on prevention, but really on helping people use their health as an asset and as a tool to help them accomplish what they want in life."

With Hims & Hers' peptide therapy offering, consumers start with a clinical visit with a licensed provider that includes a comprehensive intake of health history and goals. The company said a licensed provider trained in peptide therapies assesses each individual and, if appropriate, tailors a personalized protocol to their needs.

Hims & Hers treatments are compounded in the U.S. and dispensed directly by licensed, U.S. pharmacies regulated by state boards of pharmacy and "held to rigorous quality and safety standards," the company said.

The buzz around peptides ties into consumers' growing interest in taking control of their health "driven by the cultural zeitgeist moment," Vinjamoori told Fierce Healthcare.

"There's a variety of different factors that catalyze that. I think the COVID-19 pandemic catalyzed a lot of it for a variety of reasons. I think you see accelerations in biotechnology where people now see something like GLP-1s that can help with a problem that seemed intractable before. I think people are now interested in figuring out what is the range of solutions that could help me. I also think peptides represent something that has a lot of promise, a lot of excitement," he said.

"But there are also some cautions that we need to take as well. Something we think a lot about is how do we meet this moment of excitement and enthusiasm with some degree of rigor and guardrails," Vinjamoori said.

"It starts with the product itself. How do we ensure the quality? The compounds are of the highest quality and are compounded in the most rigorous way, made available in the most rigorous way. That's why we are trying to have as much oversight as we can into the full process of making these available," he said.

He asserted that informed consent and transparency with consumers are critical. "I've spent a lot of time with the team to make sure that we understand that people have this enthusiasm, but we need to make sure that they understand what they're getting into. There is a difference between this and FDA-approved therapies," he said.

"Sometimes people should try the standard of care before they engage with some of these things, and so we want to make sure we have these conversations with people openly and honestly. But at the same time, we also want to respect their choice," he added.

Hims & Hers Peptides+ is available with its Care+ program that gives customers access to twice-a-year lab testing to track their progress, unlimited support and ongoing check-ins from a dedicated care team. Customers can also message providers trained in peptide therapy 24/7 with any questions. The Hims Men’s panel includes at-home blood collection and access to lab tests that provide insights across 22 biomarkers, including testosterone, vitamin D, and cholesterol. The Hers Women’s panel tests 22 biomarkers across hormones, vitamins, and heart health.

#5800. Yozma IN 11 Review — Offroad Dirt Biking with 4800W Peak Power

As a teen, I loved riding — anything, my skateboard, my bike, whatever had wheels and I could get moving on, I was there for it. Then I spent a few years racing mountain bikes in the NORBA circuit when I was in my mid-20’s. The feel of a trail, the woods, cruising through nature … it’s intoxicating. It always came with a lot of work — mountain biking pre e-bikes was a frikkin workout. Pedaling a bike up a combined 3,000 feet worth of climbing during a race would knock me out for days. If only I was in my teen years or 20s now — LOL.

With bikes like the Yozma IN 11, you can cruise mountain trails at speeds up to 45 mph, and do it completely powered by solar energy, to boot. Compared with noisy, smelly gas bikes, the Yozma can cruise on sunshine and offers the quintessential instant torque of the all-electric motor, making racing competitions with gas bikes an absolute joke — the Yozma will crush them.

It also has a number of other features, including wheelie assist, reverse gear and high visibility brake lights.

So does this all-electric dirt bike capable of going 44 mph and helping me do assisted wheelies to impress the ladies. I mean … wtf.

#5799. Cardiovascular Screening Test Called Into Question

— MedPage Today reporters offer further insights into recently covered topics

The following is a transcript of the podcast episode:

Rachael Robertson: Hey everybody! Welcome to MedPod Today, the podcast series where MedPage Today reporters take a deeper look into the week's biggest healthcare stories. I'm your host, Rachael Robertson.

Cheryl Clark is kicking off today's episode with her reporting on an ongoing debate on whether to test all adults for Lipoprotein(a). Then, Kristen Monaco will fill us in on major developments in the GLP-1 medication space. To close out, I'll give us a rundown on the latest measles numbers and what it means that the U.S. lost its measles elimination status after 26 years.

A March recommendation from the American College of Cardiology (ACC) and 10 other medical societies for the first time said all adults should have their blood tested for something called Lipoprotein(a), also called Lp(a) or "little a," which is a risk factor you may have never heard of. But many cardiologists and primary care doctors are strongly resisting doing this. Cheryl Clark is here to tell us more.

Cheryl Clark: Thanks, Rachael. Yeah, "little a" – this is a component in blood that is believed to be strongly associated with one's risk of getting an earlier cardiovascular event or aortic stenosis. A growing number of studies seem to show that. But it's genetically inherited, so the score you get basically stays the same throughout your whole life. Diet, exercise, lowering low-density lipoprotein (LDL), body mass index (BMI), they don't change it. So for a long time, doctors said there was really nothing they could do about it. So why measure it?

Scores, it turns out, that are higher than 125 nanomoles per liter might just scare people, especially those without established disease or symptoms. And some doctors are reporting otherwise healthy patients, some even in their 70s, with scores in the high 300s or even higher. So it seems like a high score is survivable, but it is scary.

Now, European cardiology associations in 2022 and again in 2025 began to rethink this. The idea was that if you knew your score, you could change other elements in your life that raise your risk, like trying harder to lose weight. I mean, like really try harder to lower LDL, blood pressure, improve your diet, exercise, do all of that stuff.

The American groups, including the ACC and the American Heart Association, in March joined in. They issued similar recommendations in a 134-page document, basically agreeing with the Europeans. So now this is a standard recommendation that everybody, all adults, should get this at least once in their lifetime.

It might be that their thinking was rooted in the fact that now pharmaceutical companies have been developing drugs that lower this "little a," but they needed clinical trials to prove it. They also needed to prove that it would prevent heart disease and strokes, which of course is the point. Why would we do this otherwise? There are at least three drugs in development, so now lots of smart people are pretty darn sure they'd be successful with this drug and save lots of lives. So a few weeks ago, the first drug trial for a drug called pelacarsen was released, but the results were shockingly disappointing.

Clark: Well, it was called the HORIZON study. It was a large placebo-controlled trial, and it found no reduced risk in these cardiovascular events. It tested pelacarsen, which did lower "little a," but participants were still getting diagnosed and dying. So there is something else clearly in play that's causing heart disease. Maybe Lp(a) is a marker for something else, or maybe something else is protecting some people. Who knows?

So I asked a lot of doctors, were they ordering these tests? Especially now that we have the HORIZON results, and several said they were. Some said they were just ordering these tests in people with established familial linkages, like genetic predisposition. But many said they vehemently opposed such a universal recommendation or aren't ordering it at all. My own doctor orders it, but said other doctors in her office practice are not.

One cardiologist said otherwise healthy patients are seeing their high scores online in their portals and are freaking out, crying, and thinking they're going to die tomorrow, and they have to spend time reassuring them. One physician told me the committee should have prioritized calcium CT scoring instead of "little a." He thinks CT scoring shows real disease that can be treated. And Rita Redberg, MD, MSc, a prominent cardiologist at UCSF – she's known for less is more approach – called "little a" a useless test.

And by the way, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services has not joined the bandwagon on testing for "little a." It allows regional Medicare jurisdictions across the country to make case by case determinations about whether to cover the tests. So some beneficiaries are going to get stuck with paying for this out-of-pocket.

Robertson: It sounds like there's still a lot of confusion.

Clark: Yes, the bottom line seems to be: live a healthy life. If you get a high score, be thankful and adhere to strategies that we know reduce your LDL and your other risk factors. We'll see in a few years if any of these other three drugs now in clinical trials clarify this complicated issue.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for your reporting here, Cheryl.

Robertson: Pediatric obesity took center stage at ObesityWeek in National Harbor, Maryland. There were two major phase III trials showing strong weight loss results for GLP-1 medications in kids and teens. This was just days after the FDA added a new vision-related safety warning across the entire drug class. Kristen Monaco is here to break down the latest developments.

So, Kristen, starting with the adolescent data from ObesityWeek, what did the SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS trial show for tirzepatide?

Kristen Monaco: SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS was a phase III trial simultaneously published in JAMA that looked at the very popular dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist tirzepatide, also known as Zepbound, in 160 adolescents ages 12 to 17 with overweight or obesity. All participants also received lifestyle intervention. At week 72, teens who were taking tirzepatide saw an average BMI reduction of 18.5%, compared with a 0.5% increase in those taking placebo. Nearly half on tirzepatide achieved at least a 20% reduction in BMI, and over half were no longer considered to have obesity by the end of the trial.

Notably, researchers also saw improvements in quality of life and physical comfort scores, with no signals of disruption to growth or pubertal maturation, which is a concern of using these agents in this age group. Tirzepatide is currently only approved for adults with obesity or kids 10 and older with type 2 diabetes. So these data lay the groundwork for potential obesity indication in teens. However, rival GLP drug semaglutide is already approved for teens with obesity.

Robertson: Those are really impressive results. Researchers also presented data looking at even younger children, those ages six to 11. What did the STEP Young trial find for semaglutide?

Monaco: STEP Young, which was published in the New England Journal of Medicine, looked at semaglutide, also known as Wegovy, in 167 children, ages 6 to 11, with obesity alongside lifestyle counseling. Right now, semaglutide is only approved for obesity in patients ages 12 and up. There are no GLP-1 therapies right now that are FDA approved for this very young age group. So treatment options are largely limited to lifestyle modifications.

In the trial at week 68, semaglutide reduced BMI by 13.5%, and nearly 40% of children on the injectable no longer had obesity by the end of the trial. Children on treatment also saw improvements in cardiometabolic markers like waist circumference, triglycerides, and blood pressure. Gastrointestinal side effects like vomiting, diarrhea, and nausea were more common, especially during the dose escalation phase, which is expected with GLP-1 drugs. Though they were mostly mild to moderate and transient, with no safety signals affecting growth or pubertal development. Drug maker Novo Nordisk hasn't announced yet whether it plans to submit these data to the FDA, so that will be something to look closely for.

Robertson: That's super interesting. So stepping back from your conference coverage, you also reported on a major regulatory update involving GLP-1 drugs and eye health. What is this FDA warning, and what led to it?

Monaco: Yes, so the FDA is now requiring manufacturers of all currently marketed GLP-1 medications to include a warning about the potential risk of non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy, also known as NAION, which is a rare but potentially blinding eye condition caused by reduced blood flow to the optic disc. This update follows a wave of observational studies, including a 2024 JAMA Ophthalmology study on semaglutide, that prompted this FDA review. The agency concluded from its review that there is indeed a rare but potentially increased risk of NAION events in patients taking GLP-1 drugs. Cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products.

So the FDA told healthcare providers to tell their patients to report any sudden, painless vision loss or visual field changes in one eye immediately, and then to refer them promptly to an ophthalmologist. They should also consider discontinuing the GLP-1 drug unless the treatment benefits clearly outweigh the risks.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for this important context as these medications continue to expand into younger populations. Thanks for being here, Kristen.

Robertson: The U.S. has officially lost its measles elimination status after 26 years. It's no surprise, considering there have been at least 830 hospitalizations and 13 measles-associated deaths in the past 2 years.

Losing this status is a big deal. Our editor-in-chief, Jeremy Faust, MD, sat down with pediatric infectious disease expert Paul Offit, MD, of Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, and public health researcher Noel Brewer, PhD, of the University of North Carolina in Chapel Hill. They talked about the resurgence of measles outbreaks, declining vaccination rates, timing of the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, the influence of vaccine misinformation, and the challenges facing public health officials as they work to restore confidence in vaccination and prevent further transmission. It's a lot, but I really recommend you listen to the full conversation. We've linked it here.

Last week, there were 334 cases of measles, and there were 319 the week before that. Pennsylvania is still really struggling with more than 100 cases in the last 2 weeks, but things are also really heating up in Wisconsin, which had more than 200 cases in that same time period. The other states that we're keeping an extra close eye on are New York, Indiana, and Minnesota. With holiday travel coming up, we'll see in the coming weeks how that influences the spread of measles.

As always, check out our interactive measles map, which is updated at the top of each week, and we've linked it right here.

This episode was hosted and produced by me, Rachael Robertson. Sound engineering by Greg Laub. Theme music by Palomar. Our guests were MedPage Today reporters Cheryl Clark and Kristen Monaco. Links to their stories are in the show notes.

MedPod Today is a production of MedPage Today. For more information about our show, check out medpagetoday.com/podcasts.

#5798. STAT+: Peptide market poised to explode with new Hims and Noom launches

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Sarah covers how industries like food and tobacco affect Americans’ bodies and minds. Her interests include ultra-processed foods; smoke-free tobacco; and wellness trends like perimenopause products and peptides. Confidential tips can be sent on Signal at sarahlizchar.47.

Katie covers the impact of health technology on patients, clinicians, and businesses. Her stories explore the price tag of clinical AI, digital health at the FDA, and the boom in direct-to-consumer telehealth. Confidential tips can be sent on Signal at palmer.01.

Peptides are poised to go mainstream with this week’s launches from telehealth companies Hims & Hers and Noom, both of which announced they will be offering prescriptions for eligible patients.

Synthetic peptides are a broad category of short-chain amino acids. Initially popular among bodybuilders, certain drugs in the class have gained traction among a broad swath of people looking to optimize their health — whether to build muscle, feel more energetic, increase libido, or recover from injuries. But many compounded peptides that are marketed to consumers are experimental. They have not been approved by the Food and Drug Administration, nor are there randomized controlled trials showing their safety and efficacy in humans. The Department of Health and Human Services, however, has foregrounded the drugs under health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., an avowed peptide enthusiast.

While peptide prescriptions were already offered by smaller telehealth companies, Hims (with a market cap of more than $3.5 billion) and Noom (a private company valued at $3.7 billion) are two of the industry’s dominant players, making the announcements a pivotal moment for peptides in the United States.

Reporter, Commercial Determinants of Health
